|                |
|  INTRODUCTION  |
|                |
 ----------------
I worked for Hokkaidou University from 2010 to 2021.  At the time, I 
guided many excursions.  I made this file based on my excursion notes.  
This is for virtual excursions that transcend time and space.  ( by T. 
Nihei ) 

In this file, place names are written as they are pronounced, for 
example, Hokkaido --> Hokkaidou.  This is because the content is for 
oral explanation ( by Nihei lab 2025a ).  

Some place names are displayed in UTF codes.  To convert UTF codes to 
characters, use the ``echo'' command on a terminal emulator, for 
example ; echo $'\u5317\u7ADC'

Or, these online command-line interpreters are available ; 
http://codapi.org/shell/
http://www.tutorialspoint.com/compilers/online-bash-compiler.htm

1. 1902 = nineteen oh two.
2. 2006 = two thousand and six.
3. 41* 23.87' North = forty one degrees, twenty three point eight seven 
   minutes north ( latitude ).  ( * = \u00B0. )
4. 9.1 *C = nine point one degrees Celsius.  ( * = \u00B0. ) 

5. National Route 228 = national route two hundred twenty eight ( two 
   twenty eight ).
6. Prefectural Route 999 = prefectural route nine hundred ninety nine 
   ( nine ninety nine, nine nine nine ).

|                 |
|     SORACHI     |
|                 |
|  MAP 1 - NO. 7  |
 -----------------
LOCATION     

Sorachi ( so-ra-chi ) ( \u7A7A\u77E5 ) is a sub-prefecture ( ``sougou-
shinkou-kyouku'' ) located in central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  
Sorachi is an inland sub-prefecture.  The area corresponds to the 
middle reaches of the Ishikari River.  The Mashike Moutains rise to the 
west.  The Yuubari Mountains rise to the east.  

The climate is that of an inland region.  Summers are warmer and 
winters are colder than the average of Hokkaidou.  It snows a lot in 
winter.  A cold, moist wind blows from the northwest during winter.  
When this wind reaches the mountain ranges, it snows. 
 
According to the Koeppen's climate classification, the area is 
classified as Dfb ( humid continental, warm summer ).  In Iwamizawa 
City, the mean annual temperature is 7.6 degrees Celsius ( average from 
1981 to 2010 ), the mean annual precipitation is 1,163 millimeters, and 
the mean annual snowfall is 753 centimeters.  #1

NAME 

The name ``Sorachi'' comes from ``so-rapci-pet'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``waterfall, fall many, river.''  It signified the waterfall of 
the Sorachi River.  This is the Sorachi Great Falls ( Sorachi-oo-taki ) 
in Ashibetsu City, Sorachi sub-prefecture.  ( The Ainu are an 
indigenous ethnic group in Hokkaidou. )

( Cf.  Takikawa City in Sorachi sub-prefecture has the same etymology.  
The name ``Taki-kawa'' means ``waterfall, river'' in Japanese. ) 

HISTORY 

In 1897 ( Meiji 30 ), Sorachi sub-prefecture ( Sorachi-shichou ) was 
established.  It covered Sorachi county ( Sorachi-gun ) in Ishikari 
country ( Ishikari-no-kuni ). 

In 1899 ( Meiji 32 ), Furano village was separated from Sorachi sub-
prefecture and included into Kamikawa sub-prefecture.  ( Furano village 
at the time covered the area that is now Kami-furano Village, Naka-
furano Village, Furano City, and Minami-furano Town. )

In 2010, Sorachi General Sub-prefectural Bureau ( Sorachi-sougou-
shinkou-kyoku ) was established.

( For Ishikari country ( Ishikari-no-kuni ), see Ishikari sub-
  prefecture. )
( Sorachi-shichou = \u7A7A\u77E5\u652F\u5E81.  Sorachi-gun = 
  \u7A7A\u77E5\u90E1.  Ishikari-no-kuni = \u77F3\u72E9\u56FD. ) 

MUNICIPALITY 

These are municipalities in Sorachi sub-prefecture ; ( 1 ) Iwamizawa 
City 90,145 ( population in 2010 ), ( 2 ) Takikawa City 43,170, ( 3 ) 
Bibai City 26,034, ( 4 ) Fukagawa City 23,709, ( 5 ) Sunagawa City 
19,056, ( 6 ) Ashibetsu City 16,628, ( 7 ) Kuriyama Town 13,340, 

( continue ) --> ( 8 ) Akabira City 12,637, ( 9 ) Naganuma Town 11,691, 
( 10 ) Yuubari City 10,922, ( 11 ) Mikasa City 10,221, ( 12 ) Nanporo 
Town 8,778, ( 13 ) Shin-totsukawa Town 7,249, ( 14 ) Naie Town 6,194, 
( 15 ) Yuni Town 5,896, ( 16 ) Tsukigata Town 4,859, 

( continue ) --> ( 17 ) Utashinai City 4,387, ( 18 ) Kami-sunagawa Town 
4,086, ( 19 ) Numata Town 3,612, ( 20 ) Moseushi Town 3,462, ( 21 ) 
Uryuu Town 3,049, ( 22 ) Chippubetsu Town 2,730, ( 23 ) Urausu Town 
2,206, and ( 24 ) Hokuryuu Town 2,193. 
 
The sub-prefectural bureau is located in Iwamizawa City.  In this sub-
prefecture, there are many cities.  This is because those 
municipalities prospered through coal mining.

NOTE  
 
#1

In Toukyou ( Chiyoda Ward, the representative data of Japan ), the mean 
annual temperature is 15.4 degrees Celsius ( average from 1981 to 
2010 ).  The mean annual rainfall is 1,529 millimeters.  The climate is 
classified as Cfa ( humid subtropical ) by Koeppen's climate 
classification.

As for the coldest month, the mean temperature of Toukyou is 5.2 
degrees Celsius in January ( average from 1981 to 2010 ).  The mean 
temperature of Iwamizawa is -5.5 degrees Celsius in January.  

As for the hottest month, the mean temperature of Toukyou is 26.4 
degrees Celsius in August.  The mean temperature of Iwamizawa is 21.3 
degrees Celsius in August.

In Hokkadou, it gets colder after Obon ( 15th August ).  In Toukyou, 
it's still hot after the equinox ( 23rd September, ``Shuubun'' in 
Japanese ). 
 _________________
|                 |
|     NANPORO     |
|                 |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 1  |
 -----------------
AREA 81 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 1, CULTIVATED 55 ; COUNTY = SORACHI ; 
POP 8,778 ( 2010 ), 5,665 ( 1990 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = POPLAR & RIVER.
 
LOCATION  
 
Nanporo ( na-n-po-ro ) ( \u5357\u5E4C ) is a town in Sorachi sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Nanporo is a small 
inland town.  The area is located in the south of the Ishikari Plain.  

The Yuubari River flows in the north.  The Kyuu-yuubari River ( old 
Yuubari River ) flows in the southeast.  The Chitose River flows in the 
west.  The topography is flat.  

NAME

The name ``Nanporo'' means ``south, Horomui'' in Japanese.  The name 
``Horomui'' comes from ``poro-moy'' in Ainu language.  It means 
``large, bend.''  The original Horomui is located in Iwamizawa City.  
At the place, the Ishikari River once meandered.  #1

HISTORY  

In 1886 ( Meiji 19 ), ten families immigrated from Niigata Prefecture.  
In 1892 ( Meiji 25 ), the land survey of Horomui wilderness ( Horomui-
guen-ya ) was completed.

( continue ) --> A group of eighty families immigrated from Miyagui 
Prefecture.  A group of fifty families immigrated from Mie Prefecture.  
They were respectivelly called Miyagui Group ( Miyagui-dantai ) and 
Mie Group ( Mie-dantai). 

In 1895 ( Meiji 28 ), the administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) 
of Horomui village was established.  It was separated from Kurisawa 
village ( now Iwamizawa City ).  In 1909 ( Meiji 42 ), Horomui Village 
( 2nd-class municipality ) was established.  #2

In 1962 ( Shouwa 37 ), Minami-horo Town was established.  The name was 
changed from Horomui to Minami-horo.  In 1968 ( Shouwa 43 ), the 
pronunciation of Kanji characters was changed from Minami-horo to 
Nanporo.  #3 

( Horomui = \u5E4C\u5411.  Minami-horo = \u5357\u5E4C. ) 
 
TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC 

The town hall of Nanporo is located near the Kyuu-yuubari River ( old 
Yuubari River ).  National Route 337 ( Douou-kokudou ) runs.  The 
national highway bends as a crank along the Colonial Rectangular Land 
Division ( Shokuminchi-kukaku ). 

The straight road that cuts through the Colonial Rectangular Division 
is the former route of the Yuubari Railway.  It connected Yuubari 
Station and Nopporo Station.

( Yuubari Station is located in Yuubari City, Sorachi sub-prefecture.  
It's a terminal station on the branch of Sekishou Line.  Nopporo 
Station is located in Ebetsu City, Sorachi sub-prefecture.  It's a 
station on the Hakodate Main Line. ) 

In the past, trains transported coal from the Yuubari mountains and 
agricultural products from the Ishikari Plain.  There were Nanporo 
Station and Bansui Station in Nanporo Town.  #4

Nanporo is included in a commuter's zone to Sapporo City.  From the 
town hall of Nanporo to Sapporo Station, the distance is 35 kilometers 
and the time is 41 minutes by car ( by internet route search in 2019).  
The population increased in the 1990s.

( For the Colonial Rectangular Land Division ( Shokuminchi-kukaku ), 
  see Shin-totsukawa Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

INDUSTRY  

Distinctive industry in Nanporo is agriculture on paddy fields. 
STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 2,343 hectares in 2015, wheat 
1,678, beans ( soybeans 452, adzuki beans 104, other beans 6 ), sugar 
beets 109, green onions 60, cabbage 55, broccoli 40, buckwheat 37, 

( continue ) --> fodder 21, onions 17, pasture 11, tomatoes 8, 
watermelons 7, potatoes 5, flowers 4, Chinese cabbages 1, radishes 1, 
other vegetables 71+X ( pumpkins, sweet corn ), and other grains 55 ; 
dairy cows X ( concealed ) head in 2015. 

As for sightseeing, Nanporo Hot Spring opened in 1993.  The restaurant 
at the hot spring serves ``cabbage mega-tendon ( cabbage large tempura 
bowl ).''  Cabbage is a local specialty.  Good cabbage is truly 
delicious.  #5

The area is surrounded by rivers.  In the past, floods were frequent.  
From the Meiji period to the Shouwa period, flood control works were 
carried out.  Agricultural land was expanded.  The downstream of the 
Yuubari River is an artificial drainage.  It was dug towards a tall 
chimney in Ebetsu.  #6  #7

NOTE  

#1

The former bend of the Ishikari River, which was called ``Horomui'' by 
Ainu people, is an ox-bow lake.  The watercourse was straightened to 
prevent flood damage.  ( The word ``ox-bow lake'' is called ``mikazuki-
ko'' in Japanese.  It means ``crescent moon, lake.'' )

#2

In Hokkaidou, 1st-class ( ``ikkyuu'' ) municipalities and 2nd-class 
( ``nikyuu'' ) municipalities were designated from 1899 to 1943.  The 
Hokkaidou government subsidized 2nd-class municipalities.  It was a 
pioneering period. 

#3

The name ``Horomui'' was changed to ``Minami-horo,'' because there was 
the same name in Iwamizawa City.  Horomui Station on the Hakodate Main 
Line is located in Iwamizawa City. 

The name ``Minami-horo'' was changed to Nanporo, because the 
pronunciation of the Kanji characters was difficult.  Also, the 
pronunciation of ``Nanporo'' is similar to that of Sapporo, the largest 
city in Hokkaidou.  

#4

As for the name ``Bansui ( \u6669\u7FE0 ),'' the place was originally 
called Bangue-sou.  In the past, the Panke-souka River and the Penke-
souka River flew.  The name ``Panke-souka'' changed to 
``Bange-sou,'' and then to ``Bansui'' ( by ``Minami-horo Town History 
( Minami-horo-chou-shi )'' published in 1962 ). 

The Panke-souka River and the Penke-souka River were tributaries of 
the Ebetsu River ( now Chitose river ).  ( The lower reaches of the 
Chitose River was called the Ebetsu River. )  In Ainu language, 
``panke'' means ``downstream'' and ``penke'' means ``upstream.''
( Also, ``so'' means waterfall and `ka'' means riverbank. )

#5

Cabbage and lettuce are leaf vegetables ( ``you-sai ( \u8449
\u83DC )'' ).  Both are Western vegetables ( ``you-sai ( \u6D0B
\u83DC )'' ).  The planted area of cabbage in Hokkaidou is 1,385 
hectares.  The planted area of lettuce in Hokkaidou is 601 hectares   
( by 2015 Census of Agriculture ).  

The largest cabbage producing municipality in Hokkaidou is Wassamu Town 
in Kamikawa sub-prefecture, whose cultivated area is 147 hectares.  The 
largest lettuce producing municipality in Hokkaidou is Date City in 
Iburi sub-prefecture, whose cultivated area is 75 hectares.  

Both are not large areas compared to the major cabbage and lettuce 
producing municipalities in Honshuu island.  But, before World War II, 
cabbage was cultivated mainly in Hokkaidou and the Touhoku region.  
Cabbage grows well under a cool climate.

Cabbage has a longer history of cultivation than lettuce in the 
country.  Cabbage has been cultivated since the Meiji period ( 150 
years ago ).  Lettuce cultivation spread after the 1970s.  Lettuce 
tastes bitter.  It pairs well with meat.  

Cabbage is eaten raw or boiled.  Lettuce is eaten raw.  My mother liked 
cabbage than lettuce.  She boiled cabbage.  In the past, raw vegetables 
could have worms ( nematodes ).

Lettuce belongs to the chrysanthemum family ( ``Asteraceae'' ).  
Cabbage belongs to the rape family ( ``Brassicaceae'' ).  Plants in the 
rape family produce cross-shaped flowers ( ``juuji-bana'' ), although 
cabbage rarely flowers.

Radishes also belong to the rape family.  Radishes flower in unkempt 
vegetable gardens.  Radishes and cabbage become inedible once they 
flower.  The status is called ``tou-ga-tatsu,'' which means ``the 
flower stalk is erect.''  It also means that she/he got old.

#6

The downstream of the Yuubari River is an artificial drainage.  It was 
constructed between 1922 and 1936.  The Yuubari River joins the 
Ishikari River.  In the past, the Yuubari River ( now Kyuu-yuubari 
River ) joined the Chitose River.  The old watercourse meanders.  The 
topography is flat.
 
Peat covered the flat land.  The land was poorly drained.  When a large 
flood occurred, it took 10 days for the water to recede.  Floods caused 
serious damage to crops. 
 
In the past, when a flood started, people gathered on both sides of the 
river.  They watched the water.  When the embankment broke, the people 
on the opposite side shouted ``banzai.''  ( The word ``banzai'' means 
``ten thousand years!'' in Japanese.  It signifies ``cheers!'' )  The 
Banzai-embankment existed 100 years ago.  
  
#7

Motoji Hobara ( 1883-1968 ) was the engineer who worked for the 
Hokkaidou Government.  He graduated from Toukyou Imperial University 
( now the University of Toukyou ).  From 1910 ( Meiji 43 ), he directed 
flood control projects on the Yuubari River.  ( Motoji Hobara --> 
http://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E4%BF%9D%E5%8E%9F%E5%85%83%E4%BA%8C )

One day, he climbed a hill in Kuriyama.  He saw a tall chimney from the 
hill.  The chimney was a paper mill Ebetsu.  it was located along the 
Ishikri River.

He thought, ``If we build a straight drainage to the chimney, the 
Yuubari River waterway will be shorten from 10.5-ri to 3-ri.  The 
chimney is the landmark.''  ( One ``ri'' is four kilometers in the 
Meiji period. )

However, due to the poor economic condition of the national government, 
construction of the waterway was postponed until 1922 ( Taishou 11 ).  
Although construction started, it didn't proceed smoothly.  So, Motoji 
Hobara and local residents submitted petitions to the national 
government.

In 1934 ( Shouwa 9 ), the dredger ``Shouwa-gou'' was transferred from 
the Toyohira River to the Yuubari River.  Then, construction proceeded. 
( The Toyohira River flows in Sapporo City. )

In 1936 ( Shouwa 11 ), amid cheers from local residents, the new 
Yuubari River waterway opened.  The section corresponds to the 
downstream of the current Yuubari River.  ( http://maps.gsi.go.jp/
#13/43.093650/141.669903 )

Then, the engineer Motoji Hobara retired the Hokkaidou Government.  He 
dedicated his life to the Yuurabri River  ( by ``Brief history of 
Yuubari River water control ( Yuubari-gawa-chisui-shi-ryaku )'' 
published by Nanporo Town in 1994 ).

There is Yoshitsune Shrine ( now Nanporo Shrine ) in Nanporo Town. 
At the shrine, Flood Control Thanksgiving Festival ( Chisui-kansha-
sai ) is held on July 1st.  A statue of Motoji Hobara stands in the 
shrine grounds.  July 1st was the holiday of Nanporo Town.  

In neighboring Naganuma Town, Water Festival ( Mizu-matsuri ) is held 
on July 2nd at Naganuma Shrine.  Both Naganuma Shrine and Yoshitsune 
Shrine in Nanporo Town convey the memory of the Banzai-embankment.
( Yoshitsune Shrine in Nanporo Town was divided from Yoshitsune Shrine 
in Biratori Town. )  

( For Banzai-embankment, see Note No. 6 above. )
( For Yoshitsune legends, see Biratori Town in Hidaka sub-prefecture. ) 
 _________________
|                 |
|    NAGANUMA     |
|                 |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 2  |
 -----------------
AREA 168 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 18, CULTIVATED 104 ; COUNTY = YUUBARI ; 
POP 11,691 ( 2010 ), 12,282 ( 1990 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 7.2 C ( AV 
1981-2010 ), PRECIPI 1,006 MM ; COUNTRY SIGN = RED TRIANGULAR ROOF.
  
LOCATION   

Naganuma ( na-ga-nu-ma ) ( \u9577\u6CBC ) is a town in Sorachi sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Naganuma is an inland 
town.  The Ishikari Plain occupies the majority of the area.  

The topography is mostly flat, but the Maoi ( \u99AC\u8FFD ) Hills are 
located to the east.  The Chitose River, the Kyuu-yuubari River ( old 
Yuubari River ), and the Maoi Hills are the borders with other 
municipalities.  #1  #2 

NAME 

The name ``Naga-numa'' means ``long, pond'' in Japanese.  It's a 
translation of ``tanne-to'' in Ainu language, which means ``long, 
pond.''  There were many ox-bow lakes.  They were reclaimed.  

( ``Ox-bow lake'' is called ``mikazuki-ko'' in Japanese, which means 
  ``crescent moon lake.''  Both represent the shape of the lake on the 
  map. )

HISTORY  

In 1887 ( Meiji 20 ), the first Japanese settlers arrived.  In 1892 
( Meiji 25 ), the name ``Naganuma'' was given.  In 1893 ( Meiji 26 ), 
the Colonial Rectangular Land Division ( Shokumin-chi-kukaku ) was 
established.  Subsequently, many settlers immigrated.  #3

In 1895 ( Meiji 28 ), the administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) 
of Naganuma village was established.  It was separated from Yuni 
village.  In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Naganuma Village ( 2nd-class 
municipality ) was established.  

In 1907 ( Meiji 40 ), Naganuma Village ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1952 ( Shouwa 27 ), Naganuma Town was established. 

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Nanporo 
  Town. )  
( For the Colonial Rectangular Land Division, see Shin-totsukawa Town 
  in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC

The town hall of Naganuma is located along the Maoi Canal ( Maoi-
unga ), which is a tributary of the Kyuu-yuubari River.  National Route 
337 ( Douou-kokudou ) runs.  Naganuma is close to Sapporo City and 
Chitose City.  The population is maintained.  

In the past, the Yuubari Railroad ran.  It was operated by Hokutan 
( Hokkaidou Colliery & Steamship ).  There were Futaba Station, Kita-
naganuma Station, and Chuuou-noushi-mae Station.

Kita-naganuma Station was located in an area of Kita-shigai-chi ( north 
built-up area ).  Chuuou-noushi-mae Station was located in front of 
Central Agricultural Experiment Station ( Chuuou-nougyou-shikenjou ).  

INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Naganuma are agriculture and rural tourism 
( green tourism ).  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = wheat 2,752 hectares in 
2015, beans ( soybeans 2,449, adzuki beans 38, other beans 4 ), paddy 
rice 2,252, buckwheat 309, onions 301, pasture 297, potatoes 112, green 
onions 90, sugar beets 64, flowers 64, Chinese cabbage 51, fodder 42, 

( continue ) --> tomatoes 41, orchards 33 ( apples 14, cherries 4, 
grapes 1, other fruits 12+X ), lettuce 13, spinach 5, carrots 4, 
cucumbers 4, strawberries 3, other vegetables 190+X ( sweet  corn, 
pumpkins ), other grains 31, and other industrial crops 3 ; dairy cows 
774 head in 2015 and hens 2,400.  #4 

In the past, floods occurred frequently.  After 1910 ( Meiji 43 ), the 
Naganuma Irrigation Canal ( Naganuma-yousui ) was developed.  Then, 
paddy fields spread.  In 1966 ( Shouwa 41 ), the head office of 
Hokkaidou Agricultural Experiment Station was moved from Sapporo to 
Naganuma.  In 1987 ( Shouwa 62 ), Naganuma Hot Spring opened.  #5 

NOTE    

#1

Today, the Yuubari River meets the Ishikari River.  In the past, the 
Yuubari River met the Chitose River.  The downstream of the current 
Yuubari River is an artificial drainage, which was constructed to 
prevent flooding.  

The old watercourse of the Yuubari River is the Kyuu-yuubari River 
( old Yuubari River ).  The old watercourse meanders.  ( The word 
``meander'' is called ''dakou'' in Japanese.  It means ``snake, go.''  
It signifies the shape of the river on the map. )  

( For the Yuubari River, see Nanporo Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

#2

As for the Maoi Hills ( Maoi-kyuuryou ), the highest peak is Mt. Umaoi-
yama ( 275 meters ).  The name ``Maoi'' comes from ``maw-o-i'' in Ainu 
language.  It means ``Hamanasu ( rugosa rose ), many, place.''  Rose 
hip is the fruit of a rose plant, including rugosa rose ( Hamanasu ).

The second peak of the Maoi Hills is Mt. Choukan-yama ( 254 meters ).  
The word ``choukan'' means ``chief secretary'' in Japanese.  The chief 
secretary of the Hokkaidou Government, Chiaki Watanabe ( 1843-1921 ), 
climbed the peak in 1891 ( Meiji 24 ). 

He made a plan to develop the Ishikari Plain from the peak ( now Mt. 
Choukan-yama ).  Mt. Choukan-yama and Mt. Umaoi-yama form the border 
that separates Naganuma Town and Yuni Town.  ( Chiaki Watanabe --> 
http://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%B8%A1%E8%BE%BA%E5%8D%83%E7%A7%8B )  
 
#3

During the Meiji period, many settlers immigrated to Naganuma village.  
But, many of them left their land because of floods.  More than 70 
floods occurred after the establishment of Naganuma village ( by 
Katsuhiko Yamashita in T. Okudaira et al. 2007 ). 

Today, paddy fields extends in Naganuma Town.  The Ishikari Plain, 
where Naganuma Town is located, is one of the largest rice producing 
regions in the country.  Today, people don't eat much rice.  So, 
farmers cultivate wheat and beans in paddy fields.

About 100 years ago, during the Meiji period, settlers ate proso millet 
( ``ina-kibi'' ), which they could harvest from the newly reclaimed 
land.  Proso millet grows under a cold climate.  Rice was the settlers' 
dream.
  
#4

Rural tourism is called agri-tourism or green-tourism in Japan.  
Several graduate theses of Faculty of Letters at Hokkaidou University 
dealt with agri-tourism in Naganuma Town. 

During agri-tourism, school children from cities stay on farms.  They 
experience farming.  Their mothers hope that agri-tourism will help 
their children become stronger both mentally and physically.  But, that 
won't happen during a stay of one or two days.

Professor Tooru Ikeda at Hokkaidou University stated that the origin of 
rural tourism is Germany.  Rural tourism was for injured soldiers 
( ``shoui-hei ( \u50B7\u75CD\u5175 )'' in Japanese ).  It was after the 
First World War.  They injured physically and mentally, and went to the 
countryside.

Western culture has a unique tendency for loving nature.  Wandervogel 
movement ( going-to-nature movement ) also started in Germany.  In 
Japan, Wandervogel clubs at universities refer mountaineering 
( mount-ne'aring ) clubs.
  
Mountaineering in Japan was imported from England in the Meiji period.  
Before that, Japanese people went to the mountains for religious 
purposes, besides forestry, hunting, and mining.  Mountaineering is a 
sport.  It's a competition with nature. 

Considering this, I think that modern Western culture is based on 
possibilism.  Possibility-based perspectives sound like a top-down 
viewpoint.  The antonyms of possibilism is determinism.  Determinism-
based perspectives sound pessimistic.  But I think people sometimes 
need to feel deeply sad ( by T. Nihei ed. 2017 ).

* * * 

A student from Italy stated that the origin of rural tourism is 
pilgrimages.  In the feudal era, it was difficult for ordinary people 
to travel across borders.  In Japan, during the Edo era, the last 
feudal period, ordinary people organized religious groups such as Ise-
kou and traveled.

Ise-kou is a religious group that studies Ise Shrine.  The members 
traveled to Ise Shrine, which is located in what is now Mie Prefecture. 
Their travel was both religious and touristic.

Recently, even Hawaii-kou was organized.  Elderly people in a rural 
area organized Hawaii-kou, studied Hawaii, and traveled to Hawaii  ( by 
T. Morimoto et al. 1998.  Changes in the livelihood of suburb mountain 
village in Hitachi-oota City.  ``Chiiki-chousa-houkoku'' http://
www.researchgate.net/publication/386424525 ).

#5

This is the list of agricultural experiment stations operated by the 
Hokkaidou Government.  Agricultural experiment stations are called 
``nougyou-shiken-jou'' in Japanese. 

( 1 ) Central Agricultural Experiment Station in Naganuma Town, Sorachi 
sub-prefecture.  This is the headquarters of the agricultural 
experiment stations operated by the Hokkaidou Government.  They breed 
varieties of soybeans, potatoes, and apples.

The headquarters of the agricultural experiment stations was moved from 
Sapporo City ( Ishikari sub-prefecture ) to Naganuma Town.  The former 
site is now Agricultural Experiment Station Park ( Noushi-kouen ) in 
Nishi Ward. 

In Nishi Ward, Sapporo City, there also was an agricultural experiment 
station operated by the national government.  It was moved to 
Hitsujiga-oka district, Toyohira Ward, Sapporo City.  Agricultural 
experiments need large areas.   

( 2 ) Iwamizawa Agricultural Experiment Station in Iwamizawa City, 
Sorachi sub-prefecture.  They breed paddy rice varieties.  Nanatsu-
boshi was bred here.

( 3 ) Genetic Resources Division of Agricultural Experiment Station in 
Takikawa City, Sorachi sub-prefecture.  This division preserves seeds.  
Seeds of paddy rice, wheat, and beans. 

( 4 ) Flower and Vegetable Technical Center in Takikawa City, Sorachi 
sub-prefecture.  This section bred Kita-kirari ( variety of lily for 
floriculture ) and Yume-temari ( variety of melon ).  

( 5 ) Kamikawa Agricultural Experiment Station in Pippu Town, Kamikawa 
sub-prefecture.  This section breeds paddy rice varieties.  It bred 
Yume-pirika, Hoshi-no-yume, and Kirara 397. 

( 6 ) Tokachi Agricultural Experiment Station in Memuro Town, Tokachi 
sub-prefecture.  This section breeds bean varieties.  It bred Yuki-
homare ( soybean ) and Kita-roman ( adzuki bean ).

( 7 ) Kitami Agriculutral Experiment Station in Kunneppu Town, Okhotsk 
sub-prefecture.  This section breeds wheat, potato, onion, and grass 
( pasture ) varieties.  It bred Kita-honami ( winter wheat ), Saya-
akane ( potato ), Sarari ( onion ), and Natsu-sakari ( timothy ).

( The ear of timothy is resemble ``neko-jarashi'' in Japanese, or
``green foxtail'' in English.  ``Neko-jarasi'' means ``cat, mess.'' )

( 8 ) Animal Husbandry Experiment Station in Shintoku Town, Tokachi 
sub-prefecture.

( 9 ) Dairy Farming Experiment Station in Naka-shibetsu Town, Nemuro 
sub-prefecture.

( 10 ) Tenpoku Branch of Dairy Farming Experiment Station in Hama-
tonbetsu Town, Souya sub-prefecture.

( 11 ) Dounan Agriculutural Experiment Station in Hokuto City, Oshima 
sub-prefecture.  This section breeds vegetable and paddy rice 
varieties.  It bred Kentarou ( strawberry ) and Fukkurinko ( paddy 
rice ).
 
Strawberries are a fruit. But, in Japanese statistics, strawberries are 
classified as a vegetable.  Fruits are perennial crops in the 
statistics.  In Canadian statistics, strawberries are classified as a 
fruit.  ( by T. Nihei et al. 2020.  Agricultural regions in the 
province of British Columbia, Canada.  ``Geographical Studies'' http://
www.researchgate.net/publication/342432003 ). 

( For lily bulb, see Makkari Town in Shiribeshi sub-prefecture. )
( For Nanatsu-boshi, see Iwamizawa City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

|                 |
|      YUNI       |
|                 |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 3  |
 -----------------
AREA 134 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 49, CULTIVATED 59 ; COUNTY = YUUBARI 
COUNTY ; POP 5,896 ( 2010 ), 7,809 ( 1990 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = STRAIGHT 
ROAD & CENTIPEDE. 
 
LOCATION  

Yuni ( yu-ni ) ( \u7531\u4EC1 ) is a town in Sorachi sub-prefecture, 
central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Yuni is an inland town.  The area 
includes the Maoi Hills to the west and the Yuubari River to the east.

NAME 

The name ``Yuni'' comes from  ``yu-un-i'' in Ainu language.  It means 
``hot spring, exist, place.''  Or, it comes from ``i-un-i'' in Ainu 
language.  It means ``snake, exist, place.''  Both signified the Yuni 
River.  There was a hot spring along the river.

HISTORY  

In 1891 ( Meiji 24 ), a group of 119 people from Kumamoto Prefecture 
immigrated.  They were called Kumamoto Group ( Kumamoto-dantai ).  In 
1892 ( Meiji 25 ), the administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) of 
Yuni village was established.  

In the same year, the Hokkaidou Colliery Railway ( now Muroran Main 
Line ) opened.  Yuni Station became a stopover point for nearby 
settlements.

In 1893 ( Meiji 26 ), the administrative office of Yuni and other three 
villages was established.  It covered Yuni village, Kakuta village 
( now Kuriyama Town ), Noborikawa village ( now Yuubari City ), and 
Naganuma village ( now Naganuma Town ).

In 1895 ( Meiji 28 ), the administrative office of Naganuma village was 
separated.  In 1897 ( Meiji 30 ), the administrative office of 
Noborikawa was separated.  In 1900 ( Meiji 33 ), the administrative 
office of Kakuta was separated.
 
In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Yuni Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1919 ( Taishou 8 ), Yuni Village ( 1st-class 
municipality ) was established.  In 1950 ( Shouwa 25 ), Yuni Town was 
established. 

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Nanporo 
  Town. )

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC

The town hall of Yuni is located to the north.  Near the town hall, the 
Yuni River meets the Yuubari River.  National Route 234 ( Yuni-
kokudou ) and the Muroran Main Line pass.  The national highway and the 
railroad connect three built-up areas ; Yuni, Furusan, and Mikawa.  #1

National Route 274 ( Mikawa-kokudou ) and the Sekishou Line run in the 
south.  There is Kawabata Station.  The national highway and the 
railroad run along Ryuusen-kyou Gorge, which was created by the erosion 
of the Yuubari River and the uplift of the Yuubari Mountains.  

INDUSTRY  

Distinctive industries in Yuni are agriculture and sightseeing. 
STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 1,998 hectares in 2015, wheat 
1,041, beans ( soybeans 519, adzuki beans 129, other beans 21 ), 
pasture 242, sugar beets 233, fodder 231, potatoes 213, onions 107, 
buckwheat 26, yams 16, cabbage 10, carrots 10, broccoli 9, 

( continue ) --> orchards 8 ( apples 7 ), flowers 6, melons 4, green 
onions 3, Chinese cabbage 2, tomatoes 2, water melons 1, other 
vegetables 131+X ( pumpkins, sweet corn, burdock ), other grains 35, 
and other industrial crops 10 ; dairy cows 1,691 head in 2015.

A dam stands out as we passed through the expressway ( Doutou-jidousha-
dou.)  It's the Kawabata Dam.  The dam was built over the Yuubari River 
for irrigation and power generation.  The Yuubari River is the border 
between Yuni Town and Kuriyama Town.

Upstream of the Yuubari River, the Kawabata Dam and the Oo-yuubari Dam 
were completed in 1962.  Then, upland fields in Yuni Town were turned 
into paddy fields.  Today, Shiitake mushroom production also thrives in 
the Yuni Town.  #2

( For the Yuubari River, see Yuubari City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
 
As for sightseeing, these are tourist attractions ; ( 1 ) Yuni Garden, 
including a large English garden, ( 2 ) a tourist herb garden, ( 3 ) 
Yun-ni Hot Spring, which opened in 1993, ( 4 ) Yumekku-kan, which is a 
library and exhibition facility, displaying mammoth and bighorn deer, 
and ( 5 ) centipede race festival.

On a bus excursion, we traveled from Kuriyama Town to Yuni Garden.  
Along the route, we passed a straight road that cuts through the Maoi 
Hills.  The road was like the one in the USA.  #3

Near the straight road flows the Yarikirenai River.  The river is 
popular among young people, because it sounds like ``I cannot bear it! 
-liver'' in Japanese.  ( Young people have dreams.  Old people have 
memories. )  #3  #4 

NOTE    

#1

As for built-up areas in Yuni Town, the name ``Furusan ( \u53E4
\u5C71 )'' comes from ``fur-sam'' in Ainu language.  It means ``hill, 
neighbor.''
 
The name ``Mikawa'' was named by the settlers from Mikawa country 
( Mikawa-no-kuni ) in Aichi Prefecture.  They applied a different Kanji 
character so as not to tarnish the reputation of their hometown.  

( Mikawa in Yuni Town = \u4E09\u5DDD.  Mikawa in Aichi Prefecture = 
  \u4E09\u6CB3. ) 

According to ``Yuni Town history ( Yuni-chou-shi )'' published 
in 1994, ``In the pioneering years, a lot of Akadamo tress grew.  Logs 
of Akadamo trees were laid on the lowlands.  The logs allowed horse-
drawn carriages to pass.''
 
Akadamo is also called ``Haru-nire'' in Japanese.  It is called 
``Japanese elm'' in English.  Large Haru-nire trees stand in the 
campus of Hokkaidou University. 
 
#2

Before the completion of the Kawabata Dam and the Oo-yuubari Dam, 
the Yuubari River frequently flooded.  Today, delicious rice is 
cultivated near the Kawabaga Dam.

These are the conditions for cultivating the delicious rice.  The soil 
contains minerals from river sand.  The rice paddies are well-drained.  
Water is plentiful.  It's cool at night and warm during the day.

( For delicious rice, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
   
#3

During a bus excursion, when we entered a straight road, students said, 
``Wow.''  The road cuts through the hills.  The road is Yuni Town's 
Yamagata Hakken-sen.  The word ``hakken-sen'' means ``8-ken ( 15 
meters ) width road.''

The road was built by Yamagata Group ( Yamagata-dantai ).  ( ``Yamagata 
Group'' means a settlers' group from Yamagata Prefecture. )  The 
members of the group moved to Yuni village in 1897 ( Meiji 30 ).  The 
straight road was built in parallel with the Muroran Main Line, which 
opened in 1892 ( Meiji 25 ). 
 
In Hokkaidou, the Colonial Rectangular Division ( Shokuminchi-kukaku ) 
provides straight roads.  But, the straight road in Yuni Town is an 
isolated road.  The nearest Colonial Rectangular Division is located in 
neighboring Naganuma Town.  It's on the other side of the Maoi Hills. 

( For the Colonial Rectangular Land Division, see Shin-totsukawa Town 
  in Sorachisub-prefecture. )

During the pioneering years in Hokkaidou, straight lines for making 
roads were set up at night.  Torches were used.  (For torches, see 
Nihei lab 2024-2025a. )

These are famous straight roads in Hokkadou ; ( 1 ) Yamagata Hakken-
sen, Yuni Town, Sorachi sub-prefecture.  This road cuts the Maoi Hills.
( 2 ) National Route 12.  The part that passes the Ishikari Plain is 
the longest straight road in the country.  ( For National Route 12, see 
Bibai City, Naie Town, and Takikawa City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

( continue ) --> ( 3 ) Slopes descending to Hakodate Bay, Hakodate 
City, Oshima sub-prefecture.  Although they are short, they are famous 
as filming locations for movies.  ( 4 ) The Road to Heaven ( Tengoku-e-
no-michi ) in Shari Town, Okhotsk sub-prefecture.

( continue ) --> ( 5 ) You will be impressed by many other straight 
roads in Hokkaidou.  For example, there is a long straight road on the 
campus of Hokkaidou University.  On the straight road, I heard students 
saying, ``Hokkaidou-wa-dekkkai-dou ( Hokkadou is big ).''  ( For 
``Hokkaidou is big ( Hokkaidou-wa-dekkai-dou ),'' see Hokkaidou. ) 
 
Japan is a mountainous country.  The road curved along the terrain.  
Straight roads were unique.  Newly developed places have straight 
roads.  For example ; ( 1 ) Hokkaidou, which mainly developed after the 
Meiji period, and ( 2 ) Shinden on Musashino Plateau in Toukyou 
Prefecture, which was developed after the mid Edo era.  

The word ``shinden'' means ``new, field'' in Japanese, although 
``shinden'' districts were developed during the Edo era ( 1603-1868 ). 
( They aren't new now. )  In the Kantou region, including Toukyou 
Prefecture, valley heads and forests on plains became ``shinden ( new 
fields ).''   

As motorization progressed, rural roads became straighter and wider.  
In traditional villages in Honshuu island, narrow roads still remain, 
especially in areas with fertile ( fa'r-tail ) soil. 

As for railroads, the longest straight section in the country is 
between Tomakomai City and Shiraoi Town.  The second longest is between 
Bibai City and Takikawa City, which runs along National Route 12.  The 
third longest is located on Musashino Plateau in Toukyou Prefecture.

Not only roads and railroads, but also rivers were straightened, for 
example, the Ishikari River and the Yuubari River in Hokkaidou.  
Students from Canada and the USA said, ``Japanese rivers are concreted. 
They aren't natural.  It's a pity.'' 

Meanwhile, students from South Asia admired the concreted riverbanks.  
They came from the countries with the Ganges River, the Jamuna River 
( Brahmaputra River ), and the Indus River.  The residents suffered 
damage from floods.

( For crank-shaped roads, see the note of Otobe Town in Oshima sub-
  prefecture and Toubetsu Town in Ishikari sub-prefecture. )
( For motorization, see the note of Otobe Town in Oshima sub-
  prefecture. )
( For straight railroads, see Tomakomai City in Iburi sub-prefecture. ) 
  
#4

As for the Yarikirenai River, the name ``Yarikirenai'' sounds like ``I 
cannot bear it any more!'' in Japanese.  Young students like the 
phrase.   A proverb says, ``Young people worry ( Nayameru-wakamono )'' 
( by T. Nihei ed. 2017 ).

Settlers near the Yarikirenai River suffered damage from floods.  They 
were disappointed.  They called the river ``Yarikirenai ( We cannot 
bear it any more! )'' ( by Yuni Town's web-page ).

Acturally, the name ``Yarikirenai'' comes from ``yanke-nai'' in 
Ainu language.  It means ``no fish, stream.''  Ainu people were also 
disappointed. 

( For funny river names, see Oshamanbe Town in Oshima sub-prefecture. )

|                 |
|    IWAMIZAWA    |
|                 |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 4  |
 -----------------
AREA 481 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 162, CULTIVATED 181 ; POP 90,145 
( 2010 ), 93,312 ( 1990 --> IWAMIZAWA 80,417, KURISAWA 8,548, KITAMURA 
4,347 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 7.6 C ( AV 1981-2010 ), PRECIPI 1,163 MM, 
SNOW 753 CM ; COUNTRY SIGN = ROSE.
 
LOCATION 

Iwamizawa ( i-wa-mi-za-wa ) ( \u5CA9\u898B\u6CA2 ) is a city in Sorachi 
sub-prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  The area extends 
from the east bank of the Ishikari River to the Yuubari Mountains.  
Rivers of the Ikushunbetsu, the Horomui, and the Kyuu-bibai flow.

NAME 
 
The name ``Iwamizawa'' is derived from ``yuami-sawa ( \u6D74\u6FA4 )'' 
in Japanese.  It means ``bathe, stream.''  In the pioneering years, 
there was an inn for road construction workers.  They could bathe 
there.  The inn was located along the Ikushunbetsu River.

HISTORY  
 
In 1882 ( Meiji 15 ), Iwamizawa Station on the Horonai Line ( now part 
of the Hakodate Main Line ) opened.  In 1884 ( Meiji 17 ), 277 
families, 1,503 people, immigrated.  They came mainly from Yamaguchi 
Prefecture and Tottori Prefecture.  The administrative office 
( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) of Iwamizawa village was established.  

In 1892 ( Meiji 25 ), the administrative office of Kurisawa village was 
established.  It was separated from Iwamizawa village.  The Hokkaidou 
Colliery Railway ( now Muroran Main Line ) opened.

In 1900 ( Meiji 33 ), Iwamizawa Village ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established.  The population was 10,284 ( by Iwamizawa City ).  The 
administrative office of Kitamura village was established.  It was 
separated from Iwamizawa Village. 

In 1906 ( Meiji 39 ), Iwamizawa Town was established.  The population 
was 19,812.  In the same year, Kurisawa Village ( 2nd-class 
municipalities ) was established.  In 1919 ( Taishou 8 ), Kitamura 
Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was established. 

In 1943 ( Shouwa 18 ), Iwamizawa City was established.  The population 
was 35,348.  In 2006, Iwamizawa City merged Kurisawa Town and Kitamura 
Village.  #1  #2

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Nanporo 
  Town. )
( Iwamizawa ( in 1884 ) = \u5CA9\u898B\u6FA4.  Kurisawa = \u6817\u6FA4. 
  Kitamura \u5317\u6751.  Iwamizawa ( in 1943 ) = \u5CA9\u898B\u6CA2. ) 

CITY HALL, TRAFFIC 

The city hall of Iwamizawa and Sorachi sub-prefectural bureau are 
located between the Ikushunbetsu River and the Horomui River.  The 
former Iwamizawa Shrine was located along the Ikushunbetsu River.  The
location of the former shrine is the original settlement.

The Hakodate Main Line and National Route 12 ( Chuuou-kokudou ) run.  
The Muroran Main Line and National Route 234 ( Yuni-kokudou ) terminate 
near the city hall.

Iwamizawa developed as a railway junction in the Meiji period.   The 
built-up area flourished.  Iwamizawa was known as ``haikara.''  
``Haikara'' is an abbreviation of ``high collar,'' which means modern 
and elegant.  Gas lamps illuminated in Iwamizawa for the first time in 
Hokkaidou.  #3  #4  #5

INDUSTRY  
 
Distinctive industries in Iwamizawa are manufacturing, agriculture, 
commerce, and sightseeing.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 
7,496 hectares in 2015, wheat 4,903, beans ( soybeans 1,335, adzuki 
beans 135, other beans 9 ), pasture 528, buckwheat 317, fodder 124, 

( continue ) --> Chinese cabbage 121, potatoes 60, orchards 55 ( apples 
12, Japanese pears 1, other fruits 9+X ), green onions 41, flowers 20, 
melons 16, radishes 14, broccoli 11, sugar beets 6, cucumbers 5, 
strawberries 5, tomatoes 5, spinach 3, watermelons 1, 

( continue ) --> other vegetables 220+X ( pumpkins, sweet corn ), other 
grains 104, other industrial crops 11, and onions X ( concealed ) ; 
dairy cows 1,074 head in 2015 and hens 400.

The planted area of paddy rice is the largest in Hokkaidou.  The 
delicious rice variety ``Nanatsu-boshi'' was bred in Iwamizawa.  As for 
manufacturing, there are seven industrial parks.  Food processing 
industry thrives.  Commerce also thrives, as Iwamizawa is the center of 
southern Sorachi sub-prefecture.  #6  #7  #8   
 
As for sightseeing, a large Ferris wheel can be seen from the express 
way.  It's Hokkaidou Greenland.  The Ferris wheel is the largest in 
Hokkaido, whose height is 85 meters.  The amusement park used to be 
Mitsui-kouzan-yuuenchi ( Mitsui Coal Mine Amusement Park ). There were 
many children. 

The former country sign ( gateway sign ) of Iwamizawa was horses.  The 
design evoked the power of horses.  The horses were used in draft horse 
racing ( ``ban-ei-keiba'' ).  There was a racetrack for draft horse 
racing in Iwamizawa. 

( For draft horse racing, see Obihiro City in Tokachi sub-prefecture. )

NOTE  

#1

As for the former Kurisawa Town, the name ``Kuri-sawa'' means 
``chestnut, stream'' in Japanese.  It's a translation of ``yam-o-nay'' 
in Ainu language, which means ``chestnut, many, stream.''  There were 
many chestnut trees. 

( For chestnut tree, see Kuriyama Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )  
 
Coal mines were located in the Yuubari Mountains in the former Kurisawa 
Town.  They were Manji Coal Mine and Miruto Coal Mine.  The coal was 
transported by the Manji Line, which was run by the Japanese National 
Railways ( Kokutetsu ).  The railway operated from 1914 to 1985. 

( For coal mining, see Yuubari City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

The Kuttari ( \u4E45\u6A3D ) Hills is located in the former Kurisawa 
Town.  The name ``Kuttari'' comes from ``kuttar-us-i'' in Ainu 
language.  It means ``Itadori, many, place.''  Itadori is a tall grass.
Its English name is knotweed.

Itadori is also called ``Kojou ( \u864E\u6756 )'' in Japanese.  The 
place name ``Kojou-hama'' in Shiraoi Town, Iburi sub-prefecture, has a 
connection to Itadori.  The word ``ko-jou'' means ``tiger, walking 
cane.'' 

The stem of Itadori is dotted.  It looks like tiger's skin.  In 
the past, large grasses such as Itadori and Akaza ( fat hen ) were 
processed into canes. 

In Hokkaidou, Itadori generally means Oo-itadori ( large Itadori, 
``Reynoutria sachalinensis'' ).  In the forest of Hokkaidou 
University, Oo-itadori grows to a height of 3 meters.

The stems of Itadori and Oo-itadori are hollow.  The light and strong 
stems were used for walking canes.  At kindergartens near the 
mountains, children play with flutes made from Oo-itadori.  
 
#2

As for the former Kitamura Village, the name comes from Yuuji Kitamura. 
( It doesn't mean ``north village ( `kita-mura' )'' ).  Yuuji Kitamura 
came from Yamanashi Prefecture in the Meiji period.  He established a 
large farm, whose area was 480 hectares. 

Place names in the former Kitamura Village are Japanese, not Ainu 
language.  These are reasons ; ( 1 ) There were no Ainu villages. ( 2 ) 
The topography has no specific features.  ( Many Ainu place names comes 
from rivers, rocks, and cliffs. )

( For large farms in the Meiji period, see Niki Town in Shiribeshi sub-
  prefecture. )
 
#3

During an excursion, I heard that the first gas lamp in Hokkkaidou was 
installed in Iwamizawa.  The article ``Folk-tales of Iwamizawa 
( Iwamizawa-no-minwa )'' describes the following ( by http://
www.dokayuki.gr.jp/minwa/burei.html ) ; 

``Iwamizawa Station was relocated to the current place in 1892 ( Meiji 
25 ).  Tamura-inn ( Tamura-ryokan ) in front of the Station had gas 
lamps.''  ( The article doesn't describe when the gas lamps were 
installed. )

According to the book ``Hokkaidou Gas centennial history ( Hokkaidou 
Gas 100-nen-shi )'' published in 2012, the company ( Hokkaidou Gas ) 
installed the first gas lamps in Sapporo, Otaru, and Hakodate in 1912 
( Meiji 45 ).  ( The gas lamps in Iwamizawa may have existed before 
those cities. )

The first gas lamp in the country was installed in Yokohama in 1872 
( Meiji 5 ).  Gas lamps were brighter than oil lamps.  They were used 
for commercial and public purposes, not for personal use.

#4

In the past, Iwamizawa was modern and elegant.  The built-up area was 
called ``hai-kara ( high, collar ).''  The antonyms of ``hai-kara'' is 
``ban-kara.''  It means ``barbarian, collar.''  Hokkaidou University is 
known as ``ban-kara'' ( by https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/
%E3%83%90%E3%83%B3%E3%82%AB%E3%83%A9 ).

Today, the population of Iwamizawa is declining.  Land prices are 
falling.  National chains stores opened in the suburbs.  Old stores in 
front of station closed.  The stores were turned into residential 
houses.  

From Iwamizawa to Sapporo, it takes about 40 minutes by train, or about 
25 minutes by express train.  Iwamizawa is not a bed-town ( suburb ) of 
Sapporo.  Iwamizwa is rather the center of Sorachi sub-prefecture.  
Still, many people commute to Sapporo. 
  
#5

Iwamizawa Station was a junction for the Horonai Line ( now part of the 
Hakodate Main Line ), the Hokkaidou Colliery Railway ( now Muroran Main 
Line ), and the Manji Line.  Iwamizawa was a traffic hub for coal 
transportation.  

The Horonai Line led to the Port of Otaru on the Sea of Japan side.  
The Hokkaidou Colliery Railway led to the Port of Muroran on the 
Pacific Ocean side.

The Horonai Line was the third railway opened in the country.  It 
connected the stations of Temiya, Sumiyoshi ( now Otaru ), Asari, 
Zenibako, Karugawa ( now Teine ), Kotoni, Sapporo, Shiroishi ( now 
Atsubetsu ), Nopporo, Ebetsu, Horomui, Iwamizawa, Horonai-puto, and 
Horonai.  

( continue ) --> Temiya Station was located in what is now Otaru City, 
Shiribeshi sub-prefecture.  Horonai Station was located in what is now 
Mikasa City, Sorachi sub-prefecture. 

The Hokkaidou Colliery Railway connected the stations of Muroran, 
Horobetsu, Noboribetsu, Shiraoi, Tomakomai, Oiwake, Yuni, and 
Iwamizawa.

The Horonai Line and the Hokkaidou Colliery Railway were the first two 
railroads in Hokkaidou island.  They were built for coal 
transportation.  The first two railroads in Honshuu island were built 
for passengers who traveled between the Port of Yokohama and Toukyou 
and between the Port of Koube and Oosaka. 

For the construction of the railroads in Hokkaidou, American advisors 
worked.  They were employed by Hokkaidou Development Commission 
( Kaitakushi ).  They were Horace Capron, Benjamin Smith Lyman, and 
Joseph Ury Crawford.

( For American advisors, see Mikasa City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
( For the Horonai Line, see Otaru City in Shiribeshi sub-prefecture. )
 
#6

The ranking of planted paddy rice area in Hokkaidou was as follows ( by 
2005 Census of Agriculture and Forestry ) ; ( 1 ) Asahikwa City 6,635 
( hectares ), ( 2 ) Fukagawa City 6,035, ( 3 ) Bibai City 4,709, ( 4 ) 
Naganuma Town 3,539, ( 5 ) Shin-totsukawa Town 3,413, 

( continue ) --> ( 6 ) Kitamura Village 3,196, ( 7 ) Iwamizawa City 
3,133, ( 8 ) Shibetsu City 3,085, ( 9 ) Nanporo Town 2,630, ( 10 ) 
Touma Town 2,611, ...  ( 20 ) Kurisawa Town 2,081.

Iwamizawa merged Kitamura and Kurisawa in 2006, then Iwamizawa became 
the largest paddy rice producer in Hokkaidou.  The topography of the 
former Kitamura Vilalge is flat.  Paddy fields spread.

Seven of the top ten municipalities were located in Sorachi sub-
prefecture.  The others in the top ten, Asahikawa, Shibestsu, and 
Touma are located in neighboring Kamikawa sub-prefecture.

Sorachi sub-prefecture is one of the leading rice producing regions in 
the country.  The area includes the Ishikari Plain, the second largest 
plain in the country.  Paddy fields in the Ishikari Plain expanded 
after drainage canals were constructed and peat-lands were improved. 

In Iwamizawa City, wheat is also cultivated in paddy fields.  Onions 
are cultivated on the natural levees of the Ikushunbetsu River.  Those 
crops increased after the enactment of the rice reduction policy 
( ``guentan-seisaku'' ) in the 1970s ( by Hiroshi Tano 1993.  Locaton 
of truck farming areas in Hokkaidou ( 3 ).  ''Chiba University of 
Commerce Review'' 31( 3 ) 29-73 HTTP://2242.MOOO.COM/PAPER/2425.PDF ).

Rice prices have been gradually falling after the abolition of the Food 
Control Act in 1995.  Elderly farmers stopped paddy rice cultivation.   
( The Food Control Act ( Shokuryou-kanri-hou ) was enacted from 1942 to
1995. )
  
( For paddy rice, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
 
#7

The main rice varieties cultivated in Hokkaidou are Nanatsu-boshi ( 44 
percent of the planted area ), Kirara 397 ( 23 ), and Hoshino-yume 
( 14 ) ( as of 2006 ). 

The delicious rice variety ``Nanatsu-boshi'' was bred in Iwamizawa 
Agricultural Experiment Station.  The name ``Nanatsu-boshi'' is 
Japanese.  It means ``seven, star.''  It signifies the Big Dipper on 
the north sky.  Today, another delicious rice variety ``Yume-pirika'' 
is spreading.  

I heard that pesticides are used less in Hokkaidou, because of the cold 
climate.  The situation may be changing for the increase in delicious 
rice varieties and climate change.  Cultivating new and delicious rice 
varieties requires meticulous attention, including the use of 
pesticides.

In the past, the rice cultivated in Hokkaidou didn't taste good.  The 
rice was said ``neko-matagui,'' which means ``cat, pass over.''  The 
rice was bred to increase its resistance to cold climates. But, the 
situation changed. 
  
( For cat rice, see Utashinai City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. ) 
( For delicious rice, see Uryuu Town in Sorach sub-prefecture. )
( For Yume-pirika, see Pippu Town in Kamikawa sub-prefecture. )
 
#8

In the early 20th century, drapers in Iwamizawa dealt high-end clothes. 
( A ``draper'' in Japanese is a ``kimono'' shop. )  The main customers 
were families working for Hokutan ( Hokkaidou Colliery & Steamship ).  
The business was a great successful ( by T. Hiraoka ed. 2001 ).

During an excursion, I heard like this ; ``Electrical wholesalers in 
Iwamizawa supported Kounosuke Matsushita in his youth.  Later, 
Kounosuke Matsushita's company, National ( now Panasonic ), grew 
significantly.  ( Kounosuke Matsushita ( 1894-1989 ) --> http://
en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%8Dnosuke_Matsushita )

In the high economic growth period ( from the late 1960s to the early 
1970s ), Kounosuke Matsushita gave priority to sending his company's TV 
sets to Iwamizawa.''

Coal miner's apartments ( ``tan-juu'' or `` tankou-juutaku'' ) were 
distributed on the Yuubari Mountains.  TV sets, record players, and 
radio cassette players sold well.

|    KURIYAMA     | 
|                 | 
|  MAP 5 - NO. 5  | 
 ----------------- 
AREA 204 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 111, CURLTIVATED 55 ; COUNTY = YUUBARI ; 
POP 13,340 ( 2010 ), 16,101 ( 1990 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = BUTTERFLY & 
MOUNTAIN. 
 
LOCATION  

Kuriyama ( ku-ri-ya-ma ) ( \u6817\u5C71 ) is a town in Sorachi sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Kuriyama is an inland 
town.  The area extends from the east bank of the Yuubari River to the 
Yuubari mountains.  

NAME

The name ``Kuriyama'' is Japanese.  The word ``kuri-yama'' means 
``chestnut, mountain.''  It's a translation of ``yam-ni-us-i'' in Ainu 
language, which means ``chestnut, tree, many, place.''  The northern 
limit of natural chestnut trees is the line connecting municipalities 
of Kuriayama, Kurisawa ( now Iwamizawa ), and Ebetsu.  #1 

HISTORY 

In 1888 ( Meiji 21 ), the former samurais from Kakuda Castle in the 
Sendai Domain immigrated.  They were seven families, twenty four 
people.  In 1892 ( Meiji 25 ), the Hokkaidou Colliery Railway opened 
( now Muroran Main Line ).  In 1893 ( Meiji 26 ), Kuriyama Station 
opened.

In 1900 ( Meiji 33 ), the administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) 
of Kakuta village was established.  It was separated from Yuni village. 
 In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Kakuta Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established.

In 1907 ( Meiji 40 ), Kakuta Village ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1949 ( Shouwa 24 ), Kuriyama Town was established. 
It was renamed from Kakuta.  In 1963 ( Shouwa 38 ), the town hall was 
moved from Kakuta district to Kuriayama district.

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Nanporo 
  Town. )
( Kakuda = Kakuta = \u89D2\u7530. )

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC, POPULATION

The town hall of Kuriyama is located near the confluence of the Yuubari 
River and the Uenbetsu ( \u96E8\u7159\u5225 ) River.  National Route 
234 ( Yuni-kokudou ) and the Muroran Main Line run.  The old Kuriyama 
Tunnel on the Muroran Line extends along National Route 234.  #2  #3

In the past, the Yuubari Railway ran.  It connected Nopporo, Nanporo, 
Kuriyama, and Yuubari.  ( Nopporo is located in Ebetsu City. )  The 
railway operated from 1926 to 1975.  Trains carried coal and coal 
miners. 

Kakuta Coal Mine operated from 1933 to 1970.  The population of 
Kuriyama Town peaked in 1960 at 23,003 ( by Population Census ).

National Route 274 ( Mikawa-kokudou ) and the Sekishou Line pass to the 
south.  There is no station on the Sekisou Line in Kuriyama Town.  The 
Kawabata Dam over the Yuubari River stands out from the expressway 
( Dou-tou Expressway ).  The dam is located on the border between 
Kuriyama Town and Yuni Town.

( For coal mining, see Yuubari City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
( For the Kawabata Dam, see Yuni Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
 
INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Kuriyama are agriculture, manufacturing, and 
sightseeing.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 1,773 hectares in 
2015, wheat 1,280, beans ( soybeans 490, adzuki beans 15, other beans 
2 ), potatoes 214, fodder 52, pasture 44, sugar beets 30, 

( continue ) --> green onions 29, melons 21, tomatoes 8, orchards 6, 
flowers 6, strawberries 4, spinach 3, carrots 1, chestnuts 1, cucumbers 
1, yams 1, other grains 82, and other vegetables 117+X ( pumpkins ) ; 
hens 45,200 head in 2015 and dairy cows X ( concealed ).

In 1895 ( Meiji 28 ), the first agricultural irrigation cooperative 
( ``suiri-kumiai'' ) in Hokkaidou was established in Kuriyama.  It 
became a model for rice paddy development in Hokkaidou.  As for 
manufacturing, quantities of wood products, gravel, and soil is high. 

As for sightseeing, Kobayashi brewery ( Kobayashi-shuzou ) is located 
along the Yuubari River.  Its old buildings, including rice wine 
warehouses, are designated as National Tangible Cultural Properties 
( Yuukei-bunkazai ).  Neighboring farmers cultivate brewery rice in 
summer and work for the brewery in winter.  #4

NOTE   

#1  

As for chestnut, the wood is hard and water resistant.  The wood of 
chestnut was used to make chests.  In Japan, the wood of chestnut was 
used for sleepers of railroads.  ( But, my father said, ``Although Kuri 
( chestnut ) is hard, the core of Hinoki ( cypress ) is harder.'' ) 

Many Japanese think that the word ``marron'' means chestnut in English. 
But, ``marron'' is French.  The word ``marronnier'' is also French.  
``Marron'' means ``horse chestnut'' in English and ``tochi-no-ki'' in 
Japanese.  The nuts of horse chestnut resemble the nuts of chestnut. 
 
#2

The Muroran Main Line used to transported coal to the Port of Muroran.  
At the time, the railway was double-tracked.  One track passed through 
the Kuriyama Tunnel.  The tunnel is abandoned now.  ( The other track 
passes through the New Kuriyama Tunnel. )

The Kuriyama Tunnel is actually a rock shed ( ``fuku-dou'' ).  It was 
built at the edge of the Kuttari Hills.  The pillars of the tunnel 
resemble to the former Taushubetsu bridge, which is called the phantom 
bridge because it emerges from a river.  

( For Taushubetsu bridge, see Kami-shihoro Town in Tokachi sub-
  prefecture. )
 
Near the old Kuriyama Tunnel, there was a weeping tree.  It was a 300-
year-old Haru-nire ( Japanese elm ).  When road constructors and 
railroad constructors tried to cut down the tree, it wept.  Today, a 
stone monument and another Haru-nire tree stand at the site.
`
Perhaps, the wind that blew between the Kuttari Hills and the Yuubari 
River made the sound like weeping.  Or, the soul of the dead during the 
tunnel construction caused the sound like weeping. 

The road near the Yuubari Mountains was constructed by prisoners from 
Sorachi Prison ( Sorachi-shuuchi-kan ), which was located in what is 
now Mikasa City.  Road construction in the past resulted in deaths.

( For prisoner road, see Tsukigata Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

#3

Hatoyama district is located near the built-up area of Kuriyama.  The 
area was developed by Kazuo Hatoyama ( 1856-1911 ).  He was a member of 
the House of Representatives ( Shuugui-in-gui-in ) and the president of 
Toukyou Professional School ( now Waseda University ).

Hatoyama Shrine is located in Hatoyama district.  Famous people became 
gods at Shintou shrines in the country.  For example, General Maresuke 
Nogui became Nogui Shrine in Toukyou.

Kazuo Hatoyama's great grandson is Yukio Hatoyama.  He became the first 
prime minister from Hokkaidou.  His electoral district ( constituency, 
``senkyo-ku'' ) was Muroran, although he was born and grown in Toukyou. 
Kuriyama and Muroran is connected by rail.

Kyuu Sakamoto memorial hall is located Near the built-up area of 
Kakuta.  Kyuu Sakamoto ( 1941-1985 ) was the only Japanese singer 
who won the first place on Billboard Hot 100 in the USA.

His song was sung in Japanese even in the USA.  It was called Sukiyaki 
song.  The original title was ``Let's walk, looking upwards ( Ue-wo-
muite-arukou ).''  He died in a jet plane crash.  He worked with 
facilities for people with disabilities in Kuriyama Town and 
Kuromatsunai Town.

#4

Kobayashi brewery uses brewery rice ( ``sakamai'' ) that is cultivated 
in Kuriyama Town.  The brewery rice varieties are ( 1 ) Guin-puu 
( which means ``poem, wind'' in Japanese ), ( 2 ) Suisei ( comet ), and 
( 3 ) Kita-shizuku ( north dew ).  Those varieties were bred in 
Hokkaidou.

The brewery also uses the ordinary rice variety Kirara 397.  ``Ordinary 
rice'' refers to the rice sold at supermarkets.  The grains of ordinary 
rice are smaller than those of brewery rice.  

( For brewery rice, see Setana Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture. )
( For Kirara 397, see Uryuu Town and Pippu Town in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. )

In the past, Kobayashi brewery was located in Sapporo.  The address was 
Minami-2, Nishi-4.  It's now a horse racing ticket shop by JRA ( Japan 
Racing Association ).  In 1900 ( Meiji 33 ), the brewery moved to 
Kakuta village ( now Kuriayama Town ) due to the prosperity of coal 
mining in the Yuubari Mountains.

The warehouses in Kobayashi brewery are designated as National Tangible 
Cultural Properties.  They are made of bircks or Sapporo soft stone.  
Sapporo soft stone is welded tuff.  It is called ``Sapporo-nanseki'' in 
Japanese.  The rice wine stored in brick warehouses doesn't freeze in 
winter.

In the past, when coal mines were in operation, Kobayashi brewery 
produced 10,000-koku of rice wine per year.  Today, the amount is 
1,200-koku.  ``Koku'' is an old unit of volume.  One ``koku'' of rice 
wine is 180 liters. 

( For one ``koku'' ( unit ) of herring, see Esashi Town in Hiyama sub-
  prefecture. )
( For one ``koku'' ( unit ) of rice, see Setana Town in Hiyama sub-
  prefecture. ) 

A rice wine brewery hung a large cedar ball in front of the shop.  The 
cedar ball is called ``sugui-dama'' in Japanese.  It means ``cedar, 
ball.''  When the rice wine of the year is produced, a cedar ball is 
hung under the eaves. 

( sugui-dama = \u6749\u7389. )

The color of the cedar ball slowly changed from green to brown.  In the 
past, people came to the shop when the cedar ball turned brown.  By the 
time, the taste of rice wine matured.  In the past, rice wine wasn't 
treated with fire. 

I visited a brewery in Canada in 2014.  They were selling apple soda 
( apple wine ) by weight in glass bottles.  I remembered an old custom 
in Japan.  In the past, rice wine was sold by weight in pottery bottles 
( ``tokkuri'' ).  Old ``tokkuri'' can be found in old warehouses. 

( tokkuri = \u5FB3\u5229. ) 

In the shop of Kobayashi brewery, the high-end rice wine is 10,000 yen 
per 1-shou ( 1.8 liters ).  The rice wine is brewed from the core of 
brewery rice.  The low-end rice wine is 1,700 yen per 1-shou.  This is 
brewed from Kirara 397, which is an ordinary rice variety.  I like the 
low-end rice wine, because it retains the taste from the past.

In the past, barrels were made of wood.  The material was cedar tree.  
The flavor of the wood was transferred to rice wine.  The rice wine 
producers ( ``touji'' ) made rice wine with a rich flavor.  The rice 
wine was sweeter. 
 
Today, rice wine breweries, including Kobayashi brewery, use enamel 
tanks.  ( Enamel tanks are glass-lined steel tanks.  ``Enamel'' is 
called ``hourou'' in Japanese. )  Today's rice wine has a light and
fresh flavor.

Japanese people prefer fresh to matured.  For example, the expiration 
date for Christmas cakes is one day.  ( In England, people prepare 
Christmas cakes several months before. )

Kobayashi brewery uses tap water to make rice wine.  The water is 
supplied by the town.  The water is soft water with a hardness of 30 
mg/L.  ( The hardness of water is measured with CaCo3 ( calcium 
carbonate ). )  They produce light-tasting rice wine using soft water.

The brewery used to use well water.  The well water was also soft 
water, but the hardness was 50 mg/L, which was higher than that of tap 
water.  Changes in water are changes in the taste of rice wine.  

( For hard water, see Kuromatsunai Town in Shiribeshi sub-prefecture. ) 
( For rice wine, see Asahikawa City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

When I traveled to Brazil, I visited a small distillery of ``pinga.''  
The word ``pinga'' means ``drip'' in Portuguese.  It signifies 
``cachaca ( cacha\u00E7a ),'' which is a spirit made from sugar cane.  

The producers used plastic barrels for preserving the spirit.  They 
said that plastic barrels were good because they didn't affect the 
taste.  But, city residents in Sao Paulo said that wooden barrels were 
better.  Wooden barrels can be displayed at restaurants. 

( For ``pinga'' production, see T. Nihei et al.  2015.  Characteristics 
  of sugarcane production in the State of Sao Paulo.  ``Geographical 
  Space'' http://www.researchgate.net/publication/370125283 ).

|     YUUBARI     |
|                 |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 6  |
