|                  |
|    ASHIBETSU     |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 12  |
 ------------------
AREA 865 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 728, CULTIVATED 34 ; POP 16,628 
( 2010 ), 25,078 ( 1990 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 7.3 C ( AV 1981-2010 ), 
PRECIPI 1,093 MM, SNOW 722 CM ( AV 1982-2010 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = STAR.
  
LOCATION  

Ashibetsu ( a-shi-be-tsu ) ( \u82A6\u5225 ) is a city in Sorachi sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Ashibetsu is an inland 
city.  The area is part of the Yuubari Mountains.

Forests cover an extensive area.  Most of them are National forests 
( Kokuyuu-rin ).  The Sorachi River and the Ashibetsu River flow.  
Those are tributaries of the Ishikari River.  

NAME 

The name ``Ashibetsu'' comes from ``as-pet'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``steep, river.''  It signified the Ashibetsu River, which is 
a tributary of the Sorachi River.

HISTORY  

In 1894 ( Meiji 27 ), two groups of immigrants settled in Panke-horonai 
district ( now Tokiwa district ).  They were Ishikawa Group ( Ishikawa-
dantai) and Toyama Group ( Toyama-dantai ), which were immigrant groups 
from Ishikawa Prefecture and Toyama Prefecture.
  
In 1900 ( Meiji 33 ), the administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) 
of Ashibetsu village was established.  It was separated from Utashinai 
village ( now Utashinai City ).  In 1913 ( Taishou 2 ), Shimo-ashibetsu 
Station on the Kushiro Main Line ( now Nemuro Main Line ) opened.

In 1923 ( Taishou 12 ), Ashibetsu Village ( 1st-class municipality ) 
was established.  In 1941 ( Shouwa 16 ), Ashibetsu Town was 
established.  In 1953 ( Shouwa 28 ), Ashibetsu City was established. 

( For 1st-class municipality, see the note of Nanporo Town. )

CITY HALL, TRAFFIC, POPULATION

The city hall of Ashibetsu is located near the confluence of the 
Ashibetsu River and the Sorachi River.  National Route 38 ( Ashibetsu-
kokudou ) and the Nemuro Line pass.  National Route 452 ( Yuubari-
kokudou ) branches off from National Route 38.

Coal mines thrived in the past.  Five companies ran coal mines.  They 
were called ``Ashibetsu Five Mountains ( Ashibetsu-gozan ).''  The 
population peaked at 75,309 in 1958 ( by Ashibetsu City ).  #1  #2

( For coal mining, see Yuubari City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. ) 

INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Ashibetsu are manufacturing ( machinery, 
electrical equipment, wood, lumber, food ), agriculture, and 
sightseeing.

STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 1,552 hectares in 2015, 
buckwheat 390, pasture 365, fodder 96, wheat 95, orchards 12, flowers 
9, beans 7, tomatoes 1, other vegetables 66+X ( pumpkins ), other 
grains 15 ; dairy cows 351 head in 2015.

After coal mines ( ``tankou'' ) decreased, sightseeing ( ``kankou'' ) 
increased.  Ashibetsu Leisure Land opened in 1970, where the large 
Buddha statue ``Hokkai-dai-kan-non'' stands out.  #3
	
Canadian World opened in 1990.  It was a theme park of the novel Anne 
of Green Gables.  It's now a municipal park.  An auto campsite is 
located along Lake Takisato, which is a dam lake along National Route 
38.  #4  #5 

NOTE     

#1

Coal mines flourished in Ashibetsu from the 1930s to 1960s.  After the 
Second World War, returners ( ``hikiagesha'' ) from Sakhalin also 
worked. 

These were Ashibetsu Five Mountains ( Ashibetsu-gozan ) ; 
( 1 ) Meiji-kougyou Ashibetsu Coal Mine, closed in 1963.  
( 2 ) Mitsubishi-kougyou Ashibetsu Coal Mine, closed in 1964.  
( 3 ) Ashibetsu-takane Coal Mine, closed in 1967.  
( 4 ) Yuya-kougyou Ashibetsu Coal Mine, closed in 1969.  
( 5 ) Mitsui Ashibetsu Coal Mine, closed in 1992. 

Mitsubishi and Mitsui were large conglomerates ( ``zaibatsu'' ) before 
the Second World War.  They operated coal mines in the Yuubari 
Mountains.  

An open-pit coal mine is still in operation in Takane district, 
Ashibetsu City.  The coal is used mainly in thermal power plants.  
( In Japanese, ``open-pit'' is called ``roten-bori.'' ``Coal mine'' is 
called ``tankou.'' ) 
 
( For thermal power plant, see Sunagawa City and Naie Town in 
  Sorachi sub-prefecture ; Atsuma Town in Iburi sub-prefecture. ) 
 
Coal miners are the typical working class in Marxist philosophy.  They 
are called proletarians.  As for university professors, they are also 
working class.  Even though they are proud of themselves, they work for 
money.  The middle class people can fly business class without any 
conditions.  Professors cannot.   

The number of professors ( including assistant and associate 
professors ) at universities is almost the same as the number of 
military personnel at Japan Self-Defense Forces.   
 
#2

Coal mines closed, schools closed.  In Ashibetsu City, 16 elementary 
schools, 10 junior high schools, and 4 high schools closed.  There were 
many students and teachers. 

The former site of Ashibetsu City Rai-jyou ( \u983C\u57CE ) Elementary 
School changed to Ashibetsu Campus of Seisa University.  The brick 
school building has been designated as National Tangible Cultural 
Property ( Yuukei-bunka-zai ). 
 
In 2008, one student entered Hokkaidou University from Hokkadou 
Ashibetsu High School.  The news is written on the high school's 
Wikipedia ( last accessed in January 2018 ).

During a bus excursion, I passed in front of Hokkadou Shizunai High 
School ( located in Shin-hidaka Town, Hidaka sub-prefecture. )  I saw 
a banner on the school building, which read ``Congratulations!!!  
Hokkaidou University, One.''  ( One student passed the entrance 
examination. )

In Hokkaidou, many families moved from the countryside to the city for 
education.  Many high school students live in dormitories in the city 
for preparing entrance examinations.   

( For high school students, see Otaru City ( West ) in Shiribeshi sub-
  prefecture. ) 
 
#3

Ashibetsu Leisure Land was an amusement park.  The landmarks are 
Hokkai-dai-kan-non ( large Kan-non statue ) and a five-story pagoda.  
( ``Kan-non'' is the lord that looks down in Buddhism. )  The statue is 
88 meters high.  The pagoda is 56 meters high.  The pagoda was used as 
a hotel. 

The amusement park operated from 1970 to 2012.  It was converted to a 
religious facility in 2013.  It is now run by a new Buddhit religious 
group.  Ordinary tourists cannot enter. 
 
* * *

The following are large Kan-non and giant Buddha ( Daibutsu ) statues 
in the country ; 
( 1 ) Ushiku-daibutsu in Ibaraki Prefecture, 120 meters, build in 1992, 
run by Higashi-honganji Temple ( the headquarter is located in 
Kyouto Prefecture ). 

( 2 ) Sendai-dai-kan-non in Miyagui Prefecture, 100 meters, build in 
1991, run by Chishakuin Temple ( the headquarter is located in Kyouto 
Prefecture ). 

( 3 ) Sekai-heiwa-dai-kan-non in Awacji-shima island, Hyougo 
Prefecture, 100 meters, build in 1977, closed in 2006, run by 
Toyokichi Oouchi ( the president of a real estate company ).

( 4 ) Hokkai-dai-kan-non in Hokkaidou Prefecture, 88 meters, build in 
1989, run by Tentoku-ikusei-kai ( a new Buddhist religious group from 
Ishikawa Prefecture ), formerly run by Ashibetsu Leisure Land ( an 
amusement park ). 

( 5 ) Kaga-dai-kan-non in Ishikawa Prefecture, 73 meters, build in 
1987, run by Kaga Temple, formerly run by Utopia Kaga-no-sato ( an 
amusement park ). 

( 6 ) Guze-jibo-dai-kan-non in Fukuoka Prefecture, 62 meters, build in 
1983, run bu Narita-san Temple ( the headquarter is located in Chiba 
Prefecture ).  
( 7 ) Aizu-jibo-dai-kan-non in Fukushima Prefecture, 57 meters, build 
in 1987, run by Aizu-mura ( an amusement park ).

The tall statues above are (were) landmarks.  Below are the hights of 
traditional large Buddha ( Daibutsu ) statues ; 
( 8 ) Nara-no-daibutsu in Nara Prefecture, 15 meters, build in 752.  
( 9 ) Kamakura-no-daibustu in Kanagawa Prefecture, 11 meters, build in 
1252. 

( For new religion, see Hokkaidou University in Kita Ward, Sapporo 
  City, Ishikari sub-prefecture. ) 
   
#4

The Takisato Dam was constructed between 1979 and 1999, for the 
purposes of flood control, agricultural irrigation, tap water supply, 
power generation, and unspecified water uses, such as maintaining river 
water.

The dam is managed by the Ministry of Land, Infrastructure, Transport 
and Tourism.  The power plant is managed by Hokkaidou Electric Power 
Company ( Hokuden ).  The outputs is 57 MW ( megawatt ).

Due to the dam construction, 136 families in Takisato district were 
relocated.  Takisato elementary school, Takisato junior high school, 
and Takisato Station on the Nemuro Main Line were abolished.  People 
moved.  Place names could not be moved. 

The Takisato Dam was constructed on the Sorachi River.  Floods 
frequently occurred in the past.  The main stream of the Sorachi River 
is the Ishikari River.  The last significant flood damage along the 
Ishikari River occurred in 1981. 

Downstream from Lake Takisato is Sorachi Great Falls ( Sorachi-oo-
taki ).  The waterfall is the origin of the name ``Sorachi,'' which 
means ``waterfall, complex falling, river'' in Ainu language.

( For natural disaster, see Okushiri Town in Oshima sub-prefecture. ) 
 
#5

The theme park ``Canadian World'' opened in 1990.  It was a theme park 
about the novel ``Anne of Green Gables'' published in 1908.  The theme 
park was run by a Tokyuu Group company.  The wife of the executive of 
the company was a fan of Anne of Green Gables.  In Japan, the novel is 
called ``Akague-no-Anne ( Anne of red hair ).''

Ashibetsu City and Charlottetown in Canada, where the original Green 
Gables is located, became sister cities in 1993.  Green Gables is a 
house where Anne Shirley, the heroine of the novel, lived.  The word 
``gable'' means the triangular section of a house.  It is called 
``tsuma'' in Japanese.

Canadian World closed in 1997.  It re-opened as a municipal park in 
1999.  The main attraction of the park is the replica of Green Gables.  
When the original Green Gables in Canada was burnt down, it was rebuilt 
based on the blueprints of the replica in Ashibetsu City.

When I traveled to Canada, I met Professor Tom Waldichuck.  He said, 
``I visited Canadian World in Hokkaidou.  It seemed fake.  The staff 
did not speak English.''

I visited the former Canadian World in July 2014.  I remembered the 
Canadian professor's words, but the replica of the Green Gables was 
great.  I could imagine the old life.  The rooms had no built-in 
closets.  Life was simple 100 years ago.  Anne's imagination arose 
from simplicity. 

( Built-in closets are called  ``oshi-ire'' in Japanese.  Japanese 
  mansions ( apartments ) sellers advertise the size of built-in 
  closets and walk-in closets. ) 

The air of Ashibetsu in summer is cool and dry.  It's similar to 
Canada.  In Canada, young people hold their wedding ceremonies in early 
summer.  ( by A. Tabayashi et al. 2016.  Significance of circle farm 
tours in the Lower Mainland of British Columbia.  ``Chiri-kuukan''  
http://www.researchgate.net/publication/342436438 )

( For the theme park of heaven, see Otaru City in Shirbeshi sub-
  prefecture. ) 
( For the theme park of old Germany, see Obihiro City in Tokachi sub-
  prefecture. )

|                  |
|  SHIN-TOTSUKAWA  |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 13  |
 ------------------
AREA 496 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 385, CULTIVATED 48 ; COUNTY = KABATO ; 
POP 7,249 ( 2010 ), 8,787 ( 1990 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 6.3 C ( AV 1981-
2010, DATA OF SORACHI-YOSHINO ), PRECIPI 1,557 MM ; COUNTRY SIGN = 
SHIN-TOTSUAWA-STORY'S PLACE.  #1 
  
LOCATION   

Shin-totsukawa ( shi-n-to-tsu-ka-wa ) ( \u65B0\u5341\u6D25\u5DDD ) is a 
town in Sorachi sub-prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  The 
area extends from the west bank of the Ishikari River to the Mashike 
Mountains.  Mt. Pin-ne-shiri ( 1,100 meters ) stands in the Mashike 
Mountains.  In late May, the snow-covered summit looks beautiful.  #2

NAME  

The name ``Shin-totsukawa'' is Japanese.  It means ``new, Totsukawa.''  
The original Totsukawa Village is located in the south of Nara 
Prefecture.  The name is derived from the Totsukawa River.  The river's 
name means that the area is far from the capital, which was located in 
the Nara basin in ancient times.

HISTORY  

In 1889 ( Meiji 22 ), the first Colonial Rectangular Land Division 
( Shokuminchi-kukaku ) was set in Tokku wilderness ( Tokku-guen-ya ).  
In the same year, a major flood disaster occurred along the Totsukawa 
River in Nara Prefecture. 

In 1890 ( Meiji 23 ), 600 families, 2,489 people, who affected by the 
flood disaster, moved to Tokku wilderness.  In the same year, the 
administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) of Shin-totsukawa village 
was established.  #3  #4  #5  #6 

For the next ten years, there were no immigrants from other 
prefectures.  It was a unique history of immigration in Hokkaidou.  In 
1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Shin-totsukawa Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) 
was established.  In 1957 ( Shouwa 32 ), Shin-totsukawa Town was 
established. 

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Nanporo 
  Town. ) 
( Shokuminchi-kukaku = \u6B96\u6C11\u5730\u533A\u753B.  Tokku-guen-ya =
  \u30C8\u30C3\u30AF\u539F\u91CE. )

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC 

The town hall of Shin-totsukawa is located near the confluence of the 
Toppu River and the Ishikari River.  National Route 275 ( Sorachi-
kokudou ) runs along the west bank of the Ishikari River.  #7

National Route 451 ( Shokan-kokudou ) branches off from National Route 
275, passes the Mashike Mountains, and reaches the former Hamamasu 
Village ( now Ishikari City ).

Shin-totsukawa Station is the terminus of the Sasshou Line.  The name 
``Sasshou'' comes from ``Satsu, shou'' in Japanese, which signifies 
Sapporo and Numata.  The railroad connected Sapporo City in Ishikari 
sub-prefecture and Numata Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture until 1972.  
#8  #9

INDUSTRY   
 
Distinctive industries in Shin-totsukawa are paddy rice cultivation and 
sightseeing.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 3,540 hectares in 
2015, buckwheat 363, beans ( soybeans 135, adzuki beans 4 ), wheat 135, 

( continue ) --> pasture 84, onions 38, green onions 8, fodder 7, 
tomatoes 5, melons 5, broccoli 4, orchards 1, and other vegetables 
36+X ; dairy cows X ( concealed ) head in 2015.

In the past, rice paddies stretched along the valley of Socchi ( \u7DCF
\u5BCC\u5730 ) River.  In the 1970s, the rice reduction policy 
( ``guentan-seisaku'' ) was enacted, because rice production exceeded 
the consumption.  Then, rice paddies receded from the valley.  The land 
that settlers cleared returned to forest. 

As for sightseeing, the Pioneer Museum ( Kaitaku-kinen-kan ) is located 
near the town hall.  Next to the museum is Izumo-taisha Shrine.  On the 
terrace ( ``dan-kyuu'' ) of the Ishikari River, Green Park Shin-
totsukawa is located.  The park is home to the Shin-totsukawa Story 
Museum, a hot spring, and sports facilities.  #9  #10

NOTE 

#1

``Shin-totsukawa Story ( Shin-totsuawa-monogatari )'' is a novel for 
children.  It was written from the 1970s to 1980s.  The story depicts 
immigrants who moved to Hokkadou from Nara Prefecture.  They moved 
because of a natural disaster.  ( For the natural disaster, see Note 
No.3 below. )

Shin-totsukawa Story was broadcast as a TV drama in the 1990s and 
became popular.  It was before the expansion of the internet.  Many 
children watched TV.  ( Today, children watch their smart-phones.  
They live with one arm. ) 

#2

Mt. Pin-ne-shiri stands out from the Hokkaidou Expressway ( Dou-ou-
dou ) in late May.  The summit is covered with snow.  Mt. Pin-ne-shiri 
is located at the border between Shin-totsukawa Town and Toubetsu Town. 
The name ``Pin-ne-shiri'' comes from ``pin-ne-sir'' in Ainu language.  
It means ``man, be, mountain.''

Mt. Machine-yama ( \u5F85\u6839\u5C71 ) is located near Mt. Pin-ne-
shiri.  The name ``Machine'' comes from ``mat-ne-sir'' in Ainu 
language.  It means ``woman, be, mountain.''

Mt. Pin-ne-shiri, Mt. Machine-yama, and Mt. Kumaneshiri are called 
Kabato three mountains.  Those mountains are located on the borders of 
Shin-totsukawa Town, Toubetsu Town, Urausu Town, and Tsukigata Town.

( For Mt. Kumaneshiri, see Urausu Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
( For Mt. Pin-ne-shiri, see Kimobetsu Town in Shiribeshi sub-
  prefecture. ) 
( For the name ``Kabato,'' see Tsukigata Town in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. )

#3

Totsukawa Village in Nara Prefecture is the largest village in Japan 
( excluding towns and cities ).  The area is 672 square kilometers.  It 
occupies one fifth of Nara Prefecture.  ( Toukyou 23 Wards are 622 
square kilometers ( 62,200 hectares ). )

In 1889, a large-scale flood disaster occurred along the Totsukawa 
River.  One fifth of the residents immigrated to Hokkaidou.  In 1890, 
six villages along the Totsukawa River merged, and Totsukawa Village 
was established.  After that, the area of Totsukawa Village is the 
same. 

Many municipalities in the country were merged from 1953 to 1961 and 
from 1999 to 2010.  They are called, respectively, the Great Merger of 
the Shouwa Period ( Shouwa-no-dai-gappei ) and the Great Merger of the 
Heisei Period ( Heisei-no-dai-gappei ).  Areas of municipalities 
expanded.  Many municipalities disappeared.

In Totsukawa Village, Nara Prefecture, Tamaki Shrine and its pilgrimage 
roads are designated as World Heritage Site.  They are part of Sacred 
Sites and Pilgrimage Routes in the Kii Mountain Range.

The Totsukawa River flows south and empties into the Kumano Sea 
( Kumano-nada ), Pacific Ocean.  The river's name changes from the 
Totsukawa River to the Kumano-gawa River in the lower reaches.  The 
upper reaches are Nara Prefecture and the lower reaches are Wakayama 
and Mie prefectures.

As well as the Totsukawar River, the Yoshino River changes its name.  
It is called the Yoshino River in Nara Prefecture and the Kino-kawa 
River in Wakayama Prefecture.

At a continental scale, the Brahmaputra River changes its name.  In 
China and Northeastern India, the name is the Brahmaputra River.  In 
Bangladesh, the name is the Jamuna River. 
    
#4

The first Colonial Rectangular Land Division ( Shokuminchi-kukaku ) was 
set in Tokku wilderness ( Tokku-guen-ya ).  The name ``Tokku'' means 
``protruding'' in Ainu language.  It signified the topology of the 
confluence of the Toppu River and the Ishikari River.

The second Colonial Rectangular Land Division ( Shokuminchi-kukaku ) 
was set in Nae wilderness ( Nae-guen-ya ).  It is located in Naie Town, 
Sorachi sub-prefecture. 

The Colonial Rectangular Land Divisions were set only in Hokkaidou 
Prefecture during the Meiji period.  The shape of the land division is 
similar to Township in the USA.  Both land divisions have straight 
roads running along the square plots. 

The roads of Township run north-south.  The north means true north 
( ``makita'' ).  The roads of the Colonial Rectangular Land Division 
don't run north-south. 

( There are three types of ``north.''  One is true north 
  ( ``makita'' ).  One is magnetic north (``jihoku'').  One is 
   coordinate system north  (``zahyou-kita'' ).  As for coordinate 
  system north, the direction of north may slightly differ between 
  the center and the edges of the map. )
 
A large section ( ``dai-kukaku'' ) of the Colonial Rectangular Land 
Division is 900-ken on a side.  The length ``900-ken'' is 15-chou or 
1,620 meters. 

A middle section ( ``chuu-kukaku'' ) of the Colonial Rectangular Land 
Division is 300-ken on a side.  The length ``300-ken'' is 5-chou or 
540 meters. 

In the cases of the Ishikari Plain and the Tokachi Plain, five families 
settled in a middle plot.  The area of a middle ploto is aobut 30 
hectares ( 540 x 540 meters = 291,600 square meters ).

So, each family could own six hectares of land in the Colonial 
Rectangular Land Division. They cultivated grains, beans, potatoes, 
corn ( maize ), and vegetables.  ( In Honshuu island, the average farm 
size is about 1.5 hectares after World War II. ) 

As for grains, in the Ishikari Plain, farmers cultivated millet 
( ``awa'' ), glutinous proso millet ( ``ina-kibi'' ), and rice.  In the 
Tokachi plain, farmers cultivated millet and glutinous proso millet.  

Mechanization progressed in the 1970s.  The average farm size 
increased.  The number of farms decreased.  According to 2010 Census 
of Agriculture and Forestry, the average farm size is 38 hectares in 
Tokachi sub-prefecture, 14 hectares in Sorachi sub-prefecture, and 
1.6 hectares in the country ( excluding Hokkaidou ). 
 
In the case of Township in the USA, one section is one mile on a side.  
( One mile is 1,600 meters. )  A quarter section is a quarter mile 
on a side.  ( A quarter mile is 800 meters. )  A quarter section is 
160 acres ( 64 hectares ).  One acre ( 0.4 hectares ) was an area that 
a yoke of oxen could plough in one day.  

A quarter section was the area that an immigrant could acquire in the 
western USA.  Township was the beginning of natural environment 
management ( by N. Yagasaki et al. eds. 2003.  ``American High 
Plains.''  Kokon-shoin.  ISBN 4772215727 ).

Immigrants in the USA engaged in animal husbandry and agriculture.  The 
combination is Western culture.  McDonald's hamburgers, which are made 
from cattle and wheat, represent the culture ( by Nihei lab 2025b ).
 
#5

The administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) of Shin-totsukawa 
village was one of the first administrative offices in the middle and 
upper reaches of the Ishikari River.  

#5

Development went up the Ishikari River.  Shin-totsukawa was the 
forefront on the west bank of the Ishikari River in the early 1890s.  
The administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) of Shin-totsukawa 
village also served as the administrative office of Uryuu village and 
Fukagawa village.

On the west bank of the middle reaches of the Ishikari River, 
administrative offices opened in this order ; ( 1 ) Tsukigata village 
in 1881 ( Meiji 14 ), ( 2 ) Shin-totsukawa village in 1890 ( Meiji 
23 ), and ( 3 ) Uryuu village in 1897 ( Meiji 30 ).

( Uryuu village at the time covered an area what are now Uryuu Town, 
  Hokuryuu Town, and Numata Town.  Fukagawa village at the time 
  covered an area what are now Fukagwa City, Chippubetsu Town, and 
  Moseushi Town. )

On the east bank of the middle reaches of the Ishikari River, 
administrative offices opened in this order ; ( 1 ) Iwamizawa village 
in 1884 ( Meiji 17 ), ( 2 ) Ichikishiri village in 1884 ( Meiji 17 ), 
( 3 ) Takikawa village in 1890 ( Meiji 23 ), ( 4 ) Nae village in 1895 
( Meiji 28 ), and ( 5 ) Fukagawa village in 1895 ( Meiji 28 ).

( Ichikishiri village covered an area what are now Mikasa City and 
  Bibai City.  Nae village covered an area what are now Sunagawa City, 
  Naie Town, Kami-sunagawa Town, Utashinai City, Akabira City, 
  Ashibetsu City, Kami-furano Town, Naka-furano Town, and Furano City. 
  The area of Nae included the basin of the Sorachi River. ) 

#6

The early settlers in Shin-totsukawa cultivated flax as a cash crop.  
Flax suffered heavy damage due to Youtou-mushi ( armyworm 
caterpillars ) in 1897.  After that, paddy rice increased.  The word 
``Yotou-mushi'' means ``night, steal, worm'' in Japanese.  The bug 
appears at night from the ground.

( ``Flax'' is called ``Ama'' in Japnase.  Linen is a textile made from 
  flax fibers.  This is a piano prelude by Claude Debussy ;
  La fille aux cheveux de lin ( French )
  The girl with flaxen hair   ( English )
  Ama-iro-no-kami-no-otome    ( Japanese ) )  
  
#7

National Route 275 runs on the west bank ( right bank ) of the Ishikari 
River.  The highway is called the ``back road ( `ura-kaidou' ).''  The 
main road ( ``omote-kaidou'' ) is National Route 12 on the east bank of 
the Ishikari River.

Professor Katsuhiko Yamashita said, ``National Route 275 has fewer 
traffic lights, allowing drivers to drive at high speeds.  Residents in 
the northern Ishikari Plain use the `back road' to travel to Sapporo.  
But, police officers check for speeding.  In winter, whiteouts causing 
traffic accidents.''

This reminds me of a story about the Cimarron Cutoff on the Santa Fe 
Trail in the USA.  Travelers could move faster using the shortcut 
Cimarron Cutoff, but they risked encountering Indians.
  
#8

During an excursion, I heard this ; ``Real estate companies recommend 
Shin-totsukawa, because ( 2 ) Here is the terminus of the Sasshou Line, 
( 2 ) housing prices are low, and ( 3 ) children can commute to three 
high schools by bicycle ( Shin-totsukawa Agriculture High School, 
Takikawa High School, and Takikawa West High School ).'' 
 
#9

As for Izumo-taisha Shrine, all of the immigrants who moved to Shin-
totsukawa in 1890 ( Meiji 23 ) were believers of Izumo-taisha Shrine.  
The religion is called Izumo-ooyashiro-kyou. 

The original Izumo-taisha Shrine is located in Shimane Prefecture. 
It's one of the oldest Shintou shrine in the country.  But, Izumo-
ooyashiro-kyou is counted as one of the new religions that emerged 
during the Meiji period ( 1868-1912 ).

During the 1870s, there was a movement to abolish Buddhism.  It was 
the Abolishing Buddhism, Denying Siddhartha Gautama ( Buddha ) 
movement.  In Japanese, it was called ``Haibutsu-kishaku ( \u5EC3\u4ECF
\u6BC0\u91C8 ).''

In the case of Totsukawa village in Nara Prefecture, all Buddhist 
temples were abolished from 1871 to 1873 ( Meiji 4 to 6 ).  Then, 
Shintou Shrines took over local religious activities.  
 
Then, religious activities by Shintou priests were also prohibited from 
1882 to 1884 ( Meiji 15 to 17 ).  The prohibition was the Priest 
Magisterium Separation Order ( Shinkan-kyoudoushoku-bunri-rei ( \u795E
\u5B98\u6559\u5C0E\u8077\u5206\u96E2\u4EE4 )''. )

So, Izumo-ooyashiro-kyou, a new religion based on Shintou, was 
organized for the purpose of religious activities.  Villagers in 
Totsukawa changed their religion to Izumo-ooyashiro-kyou after 1885 
( Meiji 18 ).  Meiji was a drastic period. 

( For new religion, see Hokkaidou University in Kita Ward, Sapporo 
  City, Ishikari sub-prefecture. )

* * * 
 
In Shin-totsukawa Town,	Izumo-taisha Shrine is located in Kikusui 
district.  The name ``Kiku-sui'' is Japanese.  It means 
``chrysanthemum, water.''

The name ``Kikusui'' in Shin-totsukawa Town is related to Masakatsu 
Kusunoki, who was a warlord in the 14th century.  He lived in 
Totsukawa, Yamato-no-kuni ( now Nara Prefecture ).  His family crest 
was Kikusui.  The design is like this ; 
                            ....
                     .gNN, dMMMM ; +NNa )
                    .MMMMM MMMMM ) MMMMN

Kikusui district is located in Sapporo City too.  The name also means 
``chrysanthemum, water.''  But, the name comes from the sir name of 
Yukisue Kikutei and the water of the Toyohira River.
 
Yukisue Kikutei was one of the court nobles ( ``kugue'' ) in Kyouto.  
He was one of the founders of Uryuu Farm, which was located in the 
northern part of the Ishikari Plain.  ( For Uryuu Farm, see Uryuu Town 
in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

( For the name ``Kikusui,'' see Shiroishi Ward in Sapporo City, 
  Ishikari sub-prefecture, and Akabira City in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. )
  
#10

In the Pioneer Memorial Museum in Shin-totsukawa, there is a painting 
that depicts a festival at Tamaki Shrine in 1895 ( Meiji 27 ).  In the 
painting, villagers enjoy dancing and target shooting.  Today, there is 
a shooting range of Japan Ground Self-defense Force in Shin-totsukawa.  
The painting is prophetic.

The original Tamaki ( \u7389\u7F6E ) Shrine is located in Nara 
Prefecture.  It is listed as a World Heritage Site.  The location is 
near the top of Mt. Tamaki ( 1,077 meters ). 
 __________________
|                  |
|     SUNAGAWA     |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 14  |
 ------------------
AREA 79 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 29, CULTIVATED 12 ; POP 19,056 ( 2010 ), 
23,152 ( 1990 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = YACHT & PYRAMID. 
 
LOCATION 

Sunagawa ( su-na-ga-wa ) ( \u7802\u5DDD ) is a city in Sorachi sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  The area is located on 
the east bank ( left bank ) of the Ishikari River.  The Sorachi River 
meets the Ishikari River to the north of the city.
 
NAME 

The name ``Suna-gawa'' means ''sand, river'' in Japanese.  It's a 
translation of ``ota-us-nay'' in Ainu language, which means ``sand, 
exist, river.''  It signifies the Penke-utashinai River and the Panke-
utashinai River.  In Ainu language, ``penke'' means ``upper'' and 
``panke'' means lower.
 
HISTORY

In 1887 ( Meiji 20 ), a road between Ichikishiri ( now Mikasa City ) 
and Asahikawa opened.  The road was constructed by 500 prisoners.  
Sunagawa became a hub for road and river transportation.

In 1890 ( Meiji 23 ), Nae village was established in what is now 
Sunagawa City.  The administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) was co-
operated in Takikawa village ( now Takikawa City ).

In 1891 ( Meiji 24 ), Sunagawa Station and Naie Station on the 
Hokkaidou Colliery Railway ( now Hakodate Main Line ) opened.  In 1895 
( Meiji 28 ), an administrative office was built in Nae village. 

In 1897 ( Meii 30 ), Utashinai village was separated from Nae village.  
Utashinai village covered the area what are now Utashinai City, 
Ashibetsu City, and Akabira City.  In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Nae Village 
( 2nd-class municipality ) was established.

In 1903 ( Meiji 36 ), the name changed from Nae to Sunagawa.  In 1907 
( Meiji 40 ), Sunagawa Village ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1923 ( Taishou 12 ), Sunagawa Town was established.

In 1944 ( Shouwa 19 ), Naie Village was separated ( now Naie Town ).  
In 1949 ( Shouwa 24 ), Kami-sunagawa Town was separated.  In 1958 
( Shouwa 33 ), Sunagawa City was established.

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Nanporo 
  Town. )
( For prisoner, see Tsukigata Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

CITY HALL, TRAFFIC, POPULATION   

The city hall of Sunagawa is located between the Penke-utashinai River 
and the Panke-utashinai River.  National Route 12 ( Chuuou-kokudou ) 
and the Hakodate Mein Line pass.  The national highway is the longest 
straight road in Japan. 

In the past, Sunagawa was a hub for coal transportation.  The Utashinai 
Line and the Sunagawa Branch Line were separated from Sunagawa Station 
on the Hakodate Main Line.  The population of Sunagawa peaked at 44,443 
in 1948 ( by Sunagawa City ).  Sunagawa flourished from coal mines in 
the Yuubari mountains.  #1  #2 

( For straight roads, see Yuni Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

INDUSTRY   

Distinctive industries in Sunagawa are manufacturing, power generation, 
commerce, and sightseeing.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 474 
hectares in 2015, buckwheat 275, onions 116, pasture 87, fodder 77, 

( continue ) --> tomatoes 20, orchards 11, flowers 7, cucumbers 2, 
adzuki beans 1, cabbage 1, Chinese cabbage 1, radishes 1, other 
vegetables 9, and other grains 3 ; dairy cows 363 head in 2015.

Manufacturing factories in Sunagawa produce these souvenirs ; ( 1 ) 
Yuubari Melon Pure Jelly ( by Hori ), ( 2 ) Kaitaku-okaki ( pioneers' 
rice cracker ) ( by Kitakarou ), and ( 3 ) bags and harness ( by Somes 
Saddle ).  Also, Mitsui Chemical produces adhesives and ammonia water.  
Ouji Cornstarch, which is a Mitsui group company, produces starch.

As for power generation, Sunagawa Thermal Power Plant is operated by 
Hokuden ( Hokkaidou Electric Power Company ).  The chimneys stand out 
when we travel along the Hokkaidou Expressway.  There is another 
thermal power plant in neighboring Naie Town.  #3

Small local shops open along National Route 12 in Sunagawa City.  In 
neighboring Takikawa City, small local shops closed.  In Sunagawa 
City, there are many confectionery shops.  In the past, coal miners 
bought sweets.  A project ``Sunagawa Sweet Road'' aims for revitalizing 
the region.

The country sign ( gateway sign ) shows Sunagawa Reservoir and 
Hokkaidou Children's Country ( Hokkaidou-kodomo-no-kuni ).  Hokkaidou 
Children's Country is a recreational facility, being connected to a 
service area on Hokkaidou Expressway.  There are a pyramid, Torre di 
Pisa, and Lighthouse of Pharos.

Sunagawa Reservoir is located downstream of the Panke-utashinai River.  
The reservoir is used for yachting, canoeing, and fishing.  It used to 
be an ox-bow lake of the Ishikari River. 

NOTE    

#1

Upstream from Sunagawa City are Utashinai City and Kami-sunagawa Town.  
In Utashinai City, large companies such as Sumitomo, Mitsui, and 
Hokutan operated coal mines.  In Kami-sunagawa Town, Mitsui operated 
the company's main coal mine.  On the paydays of the coal mines, 
shopping streets in Sunagawa were bustling. 

( Mitsui and Sumitomo were large conglomerates ( ``zaibatsu'' ) before 
  World War II.  Hokutan is an abbreviation for Hokkaidou-tankou-kisen 
  ( Hokkaidou Colliery & Steamship ), which is a Mitsui group 
  company. ) 

( For coal mining, see Utashinai City, Kami-sunagawa Town, and Yuubari 
  City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
 
#2

Sunagawa is the hometown of a writer who published a story ``One bowl 
of buckwheat noodle ( Ippai-no-kake-soba ).''  The story is like this ; 

``Every year, at the end of the year, a mother and her two children 
  came to a buckwheat noodle shop in Sapporo.  ( Japanese people eat 
  buckwheat noodle ( `soba' ) at the end of the year. )

  The mother ordered one bowl of simple buckwheat noodle ( `kake-
  soba' ).  ( Nowadays, people order buckwheat noodle and `tempura' 
  ( deep-fried seafood and vegetables ) rather than the simple one. )

  The three people shared one bowl.  The shop owner knew she was poor, 
  so, he served a large helping for the same price.  One year, they 
  didn't appear.  The following year, they didn't appear either.  The 
  shop owner worried about them, but then he forgot about them.  

  Twenty years later, the mother and her two children came to the shop. 
  The shop owner remembered them.  The children grew into fine adults. 
  At the time, they ordered three bowls of buckwheat noodle.'' 

( For child-rearing, see Hidaka Town in Hidaka sub-prefecture. )
 
#3

There are thermal power plants in Sunagawa City and Naie Town.  They 
are unique in that they are located inland.  They use coal from the 
Yuubari Mountains and cooling water from the tributaries of the 
Ishikari River.

Generally, thermal power plants in the country are located along the 
coast, because they use imported coal and sea water.  Japan is a 
maritime country.  ( In the past, a governmental official Sada-tarou 
Hiraoka said, ``Sea is railroad.'' )

Almost all underground coal mines were closed, but open-pit coal mines 
still open.  They are located in Ashibetsu City, Utashinai City, Mikasa 
City, Bibai City in Sorachi sub-prefecture, and Obira Town in Rumoi 
sub-prefecture.  ( The last underground coal mine in the country is in 
operation in Kushiro City. )

The coal from open-pit mines is used mainly in inland thermal power 
plants and paper mills.  In the 2000s, due to rising oil prices, the 
production of open-pit coal mines increased.

At the time, in the USA and Canada, shale gas and oil sands extraction 
increased.  The energy efficiency of coal changes in relation to oil 
prices ( by T. Nihei 2000.  Energy efficiency of crop production in 
Japan, 1970-1990.  ``Geographical Review of Japan'' http://
www.researchgate.net/publication/272277534 ).

As for energy efficiency, when I visited the Coal Museum in Yuubari 
City, I thought, ``How energy efficiency was coal when people used 
pickaxes?'' 

Professor Hiroshi Hata, the director of Hokkaidou University Ranch, 
said, ``How energy efficiency is this bird?''  At the time, we were 
watching a woodpecker.  

( A woodpecker makes a hole in the tree to eat a worm.  Watching the 
  rapid movement of the head, the energy required to make a hole may 
  be larger than the energy of the worm.  The worm may taste very 
  good.  Our lifes are like woodpeckers. )

* * *

As for the difference between open-pit and underground, higher quality 
coal was extracted from underground.  It was used for home stoves.  
High-quality coal reduced the hassle of cleaning chimney.  There was a 
sales pitch, ``For dads who hate chimney sweeping, Haboro Coal.''   

Even though high-quality coal was used, coal stoves emitted smog.  
Because of the smog, children in Sapporo didn't go out much during the 
winter. 

We don't use coal stoves now.  After the energy revolution, home 
heating shifted from coal to oil.  The energy is changing from oil to 
electricity.  In Hokkaidou, electricity demand peaks in winter.  In 
Toukyou, electricity demand peaks in summer. 
 __________________
|                  |
|  KAMI-SUNAGAWA   |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 15  |
 ------------------
AREA 40 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 35, CULTIVATED 0 ; COUNTY = SORACHI ; 
POP 4,086 ( 2010 ), 6,440 ( 1990 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = SHIITAKE MUSHROOM.
 
LOCATION  

Kami-sunagawa ( ka-mi-su-na-ga-wa ) ( \u4E0A\u7802\u5DDD ) is a town in 
Sorachi sub-prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Kami-
sunagawa is located in the Yuubari Mountains.  Forests occupy 88 
percent of the area.

The area corresponds to the upper reaches of the Panke-utashinai River. 
The source of the river is in Utashinai City.  Downstream of the river 
is Sunagawa City.  Kami-sunagawa is the smallest municipality in 
Hokkaidou.  The area is about half the size of Lake Shikotsu.

NAME 

The name ``Kami-sunagawa'' means ``upper, Sunagawa'' in Japanese.  The 
name ``suna-gawa'' means ''sand, river.''  It's a translation of ``ota-
us-nay'' in Ainu language, which means ``sand, put, river.''  It 
signified the Panke-utashinai River and the Penke-utashinai River.

( For the Panke-utashinai and Penke-utashinai rivers, see Sunagawa 
  City. )

HISTORY 

In 1897 ( Meiji 30 ), immigrants from Uzura Village in Fukui Prefecture 
settled in Nae village.  ( Today, there are two Uzura ( \u9D89 ) 
districts.  One is in Kami-sunagawa Town and the other is in 
Sunagawa City. )

( The adminitrative office of Nae village covered the area what are now 
  Sunagawa City, Naie Town, Kami-sunagawa Town, Utashinai City, Akabira 
  City, Ashibetsu City, Kami-furano Town, Naka-furano Town, and Furano 
  City.  The area included the basin of the Sorachi River. )

In 1914 ( Taishou 3 ), Mitsui Sunagawa Coal Mine started operations.  
In 1918 ( Taishou 7 ), a railroad opened for coal transportation.  
Later, the railroad became the Kami-sunagawa Line of JR Hokkaidou.  In 
1949 ( Shouwa 24 ), Kami-sunagawa Town was established.  The area was 
separated from Sunagawa Town and Utashinai Town.  #1  #2 

( For coal mining, see Yuubari City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC, POPULATION  

The town hall of Kami-sunagawa is located along the valley of the 
Panke-utashinai River.  There is no national highway or railway.  In 
the past, the Kami-sunagawa Line ran.  #3

The population peaked at 31,406 in 1950 ( by Population Census ).  Coal 
miners' apartments ( ``tan-juu'' ) lined in the valleys and on the 
mountain slopes.  

INDUSTRY   

Distinctive industry in Kami-sunagawa is manufacturing, including 
``shiitake'' mushroom factories.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = - 
( zero ) for all crops and animals in 2015.

Factories were attracted after the decline of coal mining.  Factories 
produce cutlery, semiconductors, and food.  The food category includes 
``shiitake'' mushroom, breadcrumbs ( ``panko'' ), and materials for 
``shiitake'' mushroom cultivation such as bulk substrates 
( ``kin-shou'' ) and wood logs ( ``hoda-gui'' ). 

As for sightseeing, the Kami-sunagawa Coal Museum is located near the 
town hall.  Kami-sunagawa-dake Hot Spring is located in the upper 
reaches of the Panke-utashinai River. 

On the former site of Mitsui Sunagawa Coal Mine, a Zero Gravity 
Underground Experiment Center was established.  Zero gravity 
experiments were conducted using a shaft ( ``tate-kou'' ), which had a 
drop of 710 meters.  The altitude of the former coal mine is 120 
meters.  The experiment facility operated from 1991 to 2003.

NOTE   

#1

In 1896 ( Meiji 29 ), Mitsui Mining Company started geological surveys. 
In 1914 ( Taishou 3 ), Mitsui Sunagawa Coal Mine started coal mining.  
In 1987 ( Shouwa 62 ), it closed. 

It was said, ``One mountain, thirty thousand.''  This means that a 
large coal mine supported a population of 30,000.  This saying applied 
to Kami-sunagawa Town, which used to have 30,000 inhabitants.

Large coal mines were managed by ``zaibatsu'' ( huge conglomerate ) 
such as Mitsui, Mitsubish, Sumitomo, and Hokutan.  ( Hokutan is a 
Mitsui group company. )

In the case of Kami-sunagawa, this was also said, ``One company, one 
town ( `icchou-ichizan' ).''  It means that Kami-sunagawa was a compnay 
town of Mitsui. 

As a large coal mine started operation, the number of school students 
increased.  A circus tent was set up at the festival.  Concerts by 
famous singers started earlier than in Sapporo.

( For Sumitomo and Hokutan, see Mikasa City in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. )

When I read stories about coal miners, I remember stories about gold 
miners in Brazil.  ( ``Gold miners'' are called ``garimpeiros'' in 
Portuguese. )

These are quotes from the book ``Types and aspects of Brazil ( Tipos e 
aspectos do Brasil )'' published in 1975 by Brazilian Institute of 
Geography Foundation ( Fundacao Instituto Brasileiro de Geografia e 
Estatistica ) ; 

``A vida nos garimpos e regulada por um codigo nao escrito, mas 
  conhecido e por todos respeitados.''
  ( Life of gold miners is governed by an unwritten code.  They know it 
    and respect it. ) 
``Joviais e cavalheirescos ao seu modo, os garimpeiros sao, por 
  outro lado, amantes da música e das danças.''
  ( Gold miners are jovial and chivalrous in their own way, meanwhile 
    they are music and dance lovers. )

( For coal miner's taboo, see Utashinai City in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. ) 
( For gold, see Naka-tonbetsu Town in Souya sub-prefecture. )
  
#2

Kami-sunagawa is the birthplace of novelist Jun-ichi Watanabe.  His 
famous works include the essay ``The Power of Insensitivity ( Donkan-
ryoku )'' and the novel ``A Lost Paradise ( Shitsu-rakuen ).''  He was 
the son of a mathematics teacher in a coal mining village.  He became a 
medical doctor and then a novelist. 
 
#3

The Kami-sunagawa Line was a branch of the Hakodate Main Line.  In the 
1981 film ``Station ( Eki ),'' a scene where Yoshimatsu is arrested was 
filmed at Kami-sunagawa Station.  The scene also depicts a coal loading 
facility, whose concrete wall is high.  It looks like an ancient castle 
in the desert.
 __________________
|                  |
|    UTASHINAI     |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 16  |
 ------------------
AREA 56 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 42, CULTIVATED 0 ; POP 4,387 ( 2010 ), 
8,279 ( 1990 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = SKI GROUND. 
  
LOCATION  

Utashinai ( u-ta-shi-na-i ) ( \u6B4C\u5FD7\u5185 ) is a city in Sorachi 
sub-prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Utashinai is a small 
city in the Yuubari Mountains.  It is located east of Sunagawa City.  
The Penke-utashinai River and the Panke-utashinai River flow. 

NAME  

The name ``Utashinai'' is derived from ``ota-us-nay'' in Ainu language. 
It means ``sand, put, river.''  It signified the rivers of Penke-
utashinai and Panke-utashinai.  The word ``penke'' means ``up,' and the 
word ``panke'' means ``down'' in Ainu language.

HISTORY    

In 1897 ( Meiji 30 ), an administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) of 
Utashinai village was established.  It was separated from Nae village 
( now Sunagawa City ).  The administrative office covered Furano 
village ( now Kami-furano Town, Naka-furano Town, and Furano City in 
Kamikawa sub-prefecture ). 

In 1900 ( Meiji 33 ), Ashibetsu village was separated.  In 1906 ( Meiji 
39 ), Utashinai Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was established.  In 
1919 ( Taishou 8 ), Utashinai Village ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1922 ( Taishou 11 ), Akabira Village was separated.

In 1940 ( Shouwa 15 ), Utashinai Town was established.  In 1949 
( Shouwa 24 ), a part of the town was separated and incorporated in 
Kami-sunagawa Town.  In 1958 ( Shouwa 33 ), Utashinai City was 
established. 

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Nanporo 
  Town. )

CITY HALL, TRAFFIC, POPULATION
 
The city hall of Utashinai is located along the Penke-utashinai River.  
In the past, the Utashinai Line ran between Utashinai Station and 
Sunagawa Station.  Utashinai was developed as a coal mining city.  
Large coal mines were run by Sumitomo and Hokutan ( Hokkaidou Colliery 
& Steamship ). 

The population peaked at 46,171 in 1948 ( by Utashinai City ).  Large 
coal mines in Utashinai closed from the 1950s to the 1990s.  The energy 
source in the country changed from coal to oil.  Utashinai has become 
the least populated city in the country.  #1  #2  #3  #4  

( For coal mining, see Yuubari City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. ) 

INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Utashinai are sightseeing, coal mining, and 
glass production.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = orchards 6 hectares 
( grapes for wine ) in 2015 and paddy rice - ( zero ) ; dairy cows - 
( zero ) head in 2015.

As for sightseeing, there are Kamoidake International Ski Resort and 
Tyrolean Hot Spring.  The city touts itself as ``The smallest city in 
the country.''  As for coal mining, although all under-ground mines 
closed, an open-pit coal mine is still in operation.  

Kanashibetsu Romanza was a movie theater and concert hall.  It was the 
filming location for the 1970s film ``The Yellow Handkerchief 
( Shiawase-no-kiiroi-hankachi )'' and also the 1980s TV drama 
``Yesterday in Kanashibetsu ( Kinou-kanashibetsu-de ).''  #5  #6  #7

( For open-pit coal mining, see Sunagawa City in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. )
 
NOTE   
 
#1

These were the former underground coal mines in Utashinai.  ( 1 ) 
Hokutan Sorachi, opened in 1890 and closed in 1995.  It was the last 
under-ground coal mine in Sorachi sub-prefecture and the last coal mine 
of Hokutan ( Hokkaidou Colliery & Steamship ). 

( 2 ) Sumitomo Kami-utashinai.  Its coal strata were discovered in 
1888.  Sumitomo Kami-utashinai opened in 1895.  It was merged by 
Sumitomo Akabira in 1953.  ( Sumitomo Akabira was located in Alabira 
City and closed in 1994. )

( 3 ) Sumitomo Utashinai.  Nakamura Coal Mine opened in 1905.  It was 
acquired by Sumitomo in 1928.  Gas explosions occurred in 1969 and 
1971.  It closed in 1971.

( 4 ) Sumitomo Shin-utashinai ( Sumitomo Saka ).  Nihon-kougyou opened 
in 1907.  It was acquired by Honbetsu in 1911, and by Sumitomo Saka in 
1928.  It closed in 1953.  The site was acquired by Kobukuro-kougyou in 
1957.  It closed in 1963.

( 5 ) Mitsui-monju.  Three men opened a coal mine in 1900.  Then, the 
ownership changed several times;  to Hokkai in 1908,  to Monju in 1912, 
to Tanaka-seisakusho in 1916, and to Mitsui-monju in 1922.  Mitsui-
monju closed in 1954.

The word ``monju'' is Japanese.  It comes from an old proverb, ``Three 
people gather and their wisdom is Manjushri ( San-nin-yore-ba-monju-no-
chie ).''  Manjushri ( Monju ) is a bodhisattva ( Bosatsu ) of wisdom.  
The name signified that the coal mine was started by three men ; 
Toragorou Yuuki, Nisaburou Nishida, and Tomoichi Saitou.
 
#2

These are coal miner's taboos.
( 1 ) Pouring miso-soup over the rice.  --> It looks like a landslide.  
It reminds the miners of a tragedy.  ( Insidentally, the rice with 
miso-soup is called ``cat rice ( neko-man-ma )''. ) 

( 2 ) Whistling in the mine shaft.  --> The Mountain Goddess (Yama-no-
kami) loosens her grip and will cause a landslide.  She loves music and 
reacts to whistling.  May the Mountain Goddess bless you.  ( The word 
``Yama-no-kami'' may refer a wife after many years of marriage. )

( 3 ) The barking of a dog or the cawing of a crow sounds different 
than usual.  --> You had better taking a day off.  Those are ominous.  
( My mother also said, ``The cawing of a crow is bad ( `Karasu-no-naki-
ga-warui' ).''  I use the phrase to cancel work. ) 

( 4 ) Taking back the leftover lunch box ( ``bentou'' ) that you ate in 
the tunnel.  --> You will bring the spirits of the dead into your 
house.  You must throw the leftover lunch in the tunnel.

The American song ``New York Mining Disaster 1941'' contains this 
lyric, ``Don't go talking too loud, you'll cause a landslide.''  Not 
only in the USA, but also in the UK existed many coal mines in the 
past.  The 2000 film ``Billy Elliot'' depicts life in a coal mining 
town in the UK. 
  
#3

In the World Encyclopedia ( Okudara et al. 2007 ), professor Jirou 
Okamoto wrote ; ``Around 1873, Takeaki Enomoto discovered coal deposits 
in Utashinai.  In 1890, Hokutan ( Hokkaidou Colliery Railway ) opened 
Sorachi Coal Mine.  In 1891, a railway that connected Utashinai Station 
and Iwamizawa Station opened.
 
( continue ) --> The built-up area around Utashinai Station prospered.  
Utashinai was a typical coal mining town.  In 1948, 12 coal mines were 
in operation.  The two major companies were Hokutan and Sumitomo.''
  
Takeaki Enomoto was the commander of the Tokugawa Shogunate forces.  
After the defeat in the Boshin War, he was incarcerated from 1869 to 
1872.  Then, he worked for Hokkaidou Development Commission ( Kaitaku-
shi ).

Takeaki Enomoto studied in the Netherlands.  He also spoke English.  
Horace Capron was an American advisor who worked for Hokkaidou 
Development Commission.  He organized coal mine development in 
Hokkaidou.  Horace Capron and Takeaki Enomoto didn't have a good 
relationship.  ( Capable people are as nervous as thoroughbreds. )
 
( For Horace Capron, see Mikasa City in Sorachi sub-prefecture and 
  Chuuou Ward in Sapporo City, Ishikari sub-prefecture. )
( For Takeaki Enomoto, see Hakodate City in Oshima sub-prefecture. )

#4

Doppo Kunikida was a novelist.  He visited Utashinai in 1895.  He 
published ``On the Banks of the Sorachi River ( Sorachi-gawa-no-
kishibe )'' in 1902.  It was a travelogue.  He wrote ;

``The train arrived at the valley of Utashinai.  The rain had stopped.  
The sun was about to set.  I left the station and looked for an inn.  
Several innkeepers were inviting guests.  It was a place that supported 
thousands of miners.  Hundreds of houses were clustered in the narrow 
valley.'' 

( For Doppo Kunikida, see Akabrira City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
 
#5

Agriculture and agricultural cooperatives are the main industries in 
rural areas in Hokkaidou.  However, in Utashinai, there is no 
agricultural cooperative.  There is almost no arable land.  It is said 
that the largest workplace is the city hall.

Why Utashinai City remains a small city?  The answers are ; ( 1 ) The 
status of a city cannot be reverted to the status of a town.  ( 2 ) 
There were no municipalities that merged with the financially 
struggling municipality.  ( 3 ) Nostalgia for the place name.  ( Rather 
than the word ``nostalgia,'' the Portuguese word ``saudade'' will be 
more suitable. )

The web-page of Utashinai City includes the vacant house bank.  The 
city also enacted a settlement promotion ordinance ; ``1.5 million yen 
for new housing'' and ``2 million yen for moving in'' ( as of 2018 ).  
I would like to live in the small city for about two years.

#6

With the decline of coal mining, the city attracted manufacturing 
companies.  One of them was the Orient Leather, which opened in 1964. 
Orient Leather aimed to export high-quality horse harnesses.

The company employed horse harness craftsmen in Hokkaidou.  Horse 
harness craftsmen supported agriculture in Hokkaidou when horses were 
used.  Tractors replaced horses after the 1960s.

The company's name was changed from Orient Leather to Somes Saddle.  
The head office was moved to neighboring Sunagawa City.  Somes Saddle 
sells bags, wallets, and saddles.
 
Utashinai Kousan Co. was established as a subsidiary of Hokutan 
( Hokkaidou Colliery & Steamship ).  The company started businesses in 
glass processing, wine grape cultivation, and poultry farming.  The 
poultry farming was discontinued.  The vineyard was donated to the 
city.

Utashinai Kousan succeeded the imported glass business from Hokutan.  
( Hokutan is a Mitsui group company. )  The company imports glass from 
Saint Gobain ( sa'n-go'ban ) in France and processes double-layered 
glass and triple-layered glass for homes and offices in Hokkaidou. 

Currently, it's difficult for a new company to start manufacturing 
glass in the country.  The top three companies occupy 90 percent of the 
market share.  They are AGC ( Asahi Glass Co. ), Nippon Sheet Glass, 
and Central Glass. 

AGC is a Mitsubishi group company.  Nippon Sheet Glass is a Sumitomo 
group company.  Central Glass produces automotive glass with Saint 
Goban.

( For glass, see Otaru City in Shiribeshi sub-prefecture. )
 
#7

Kanashibetsu Romanza was a welfare facility for coal miners.  It opened 
in 1953.  The name was Sumitomo Kamiuta Hall.  It hosted movies, plays, 
and concerts.  Famous singers gave concerts in mining villages at 
first, and later in large cities such as Sapporo.  Coal mining villages 
were important to entertainment industries. 

In 1971, a gas explosion happened, killing 30 miners.  Sumitomo 
Utashinai Coal Mine closed.  Sumitomo Kamiuta Hall closed too.  After 
being featured in a movie and a TV drama, local volunteers have been 
conserving the building since 1985.  There is a coffee shop in 
Kanashibetsu Romanza.  You can reflect on the passage of time.
 __________________
|                  |
|      URYUU       |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 17  |
 ------------------
AREA 191 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 121, CULTIVATED 34 ; COUNTY = URYUU ; 
POP 3,049 ( 2010 ), 3,981 ( 1990 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = SKUNK CABBAGE & 
MOUNTAIN. 
 
LOCATION   

Uryuu ( u-ryu-u ) ( \u96E8\u7ADC ) is a town in Sorachi sub-prefecture, 
central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  The area stretches west of the Uryuu 
River and the Ishikari River.  The western part of the town is the 
Mashike Mountains.  Mt. Shokanbetsu-dake ( 1,492 meters ) rises.  

NAME 

The name ``Uryuu'' comes from ``urir-o-pet'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``cormorant ( co'-mu-rant ), many, river.''  It signified the 
Uryuu River.  Cormorant is waterfowl.  It is called ``u'' in Japanese.  

HISTORY  
 
In 1889 ( Meiji 22 ), Uryuu Farm was established.  It was a huge farm 
with 49,500 hectares, which occupied the northern part of the Ishikari 
Basin.  The area corresponded to what are now Uryuu Town, Hokuryuu 
Town, Moseushi Town, Numata Town, and part of Fukagawa City.  

Uryuu Farm was a peerage cooperative farm, also called aristocratic 
farm.  In Japanese, it was ``kazoku-noujou ( \u83EF\u65CF\u8FB2
\u5834 ).''  Uryuu Farm was abolished in 1893 ( Meiji 26 ).  In the 
case of Uryuu villgage, the site was divided into Hachisuka Farm, Toda 
Farm, and Machimura Farm.  #1  #2 

In 1897 ( Meiji 30 ), an administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) of 
Uryuu village was established.  In 1915 ( Taishou 4 ), Uryuu Village 
( 2nd-class municipality ) was established.  In 1961 ( Shouwa 36 ), 
Uryuu Town was established. 

( For 2nd-class municipality, see the note of Nanporo Town. ) 

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC

The town hall of Uryuu is located near the confluence of the Uryuu 
River and the Ishikari River.  National Route 275 runs.  In the past, 
the Sasshou Line ran.  #3

INDUSTRY  

Distinctive industries in Uryuu are rice cultivation and sightseeing.  
STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 2,362 hectares in 2015, 
buckwheat 334, wheat 108, beans ( soybeans 29, adzuki beans 7 ), 

( continue ) --> melons 7, onions 2, watermelons 2, flowers 2, fodder 
1, green onions 1, broccoli 1, tomatoes 1, and other vegetables 12+X 
( concealed ) ; dairy cow - ( zero ) head in 2015.

Uryuu rice is delicious.  It is cultivated using the abundant meltwater 
from Mt. Shokanbetsu-dake.  As for sightseeing, Uryuu-numa Marsh is 
located on Mt. Shokanbetsu-dake.  Travelers enjoy seeing wild flowers. 

In Uryuu Town Historical Park ( Uryuu-chou-shiseki-kouen ), an old 
building is preserved.  It was the headquarters of the Hachisuka Farm.  
Large red pine trees grow there.  #4  #5  #6  #7  #8 

NOTE 

#1

Uryuu Farm was established under the Hokkaidou Land Sale Act 
( Hokkaidou-tochi-haraisague-kisoku ).  The law was enacted in 1886.  
It was the so-called the Large Land Plots Disposal Law ( Daitochi-
shobun-hou ). 

The owners of Uryuu Farm were ; ( 1 ) Sanjou Sanetomi, a court noble 
( ``kugue'' ) in Kyouto, ( 2 ) Mochiaki Hachisuka, a duke ( ``kou-
shaku'' ) and the former lord of the Tokushima Domain, and ( 3 ) 
Yukisue Kikutei, a court noble in Kyouto.  ( The place name ``Kikusui'' 
in Sapporo City is related to Yukisue Kikutei. ) 

The investors were ; ( 1 ) Toda Yasuhiro, a viscount ( ``shi-shaku'' ) 
and the son of the lord of the Matsumoto Domain, ( 2 ) Ootani Kouei, an 
earl ( ``haku-shaku'' ) and the head priest of Higashi-honganji Temple, 
and ( 3 ) Okitomo Akimoto, a viscount and the former senior retainer 
( ``karou'' ) of the Utsunomiya Domain ( by ``Uryuu Town history 
( Uryuu-chou-shi )'' published in 1969 ).

The owners and investors intended to introduce Western-style 
agriculture.  The development started in 1890 ( Meiji 23 ).  In the 
year, prisoners from Kabato Prison ( Kabato-shuuchi-kan ) cleared 125 
hectares.  They cut trees, dug ditches, constructed bridges and farm 
buildings, and cultivated crops.

In the first year, the arable land was 105 hectares.  The crops were 
corn ( 38 hectares ), rye ( 19 hectares ), soybeans ( 18 hectares ), 
adzuki beans ( 4 hectares ), sugar beets, vegetables, potatoes, and 
buckwheat. 

Prisoners from Kabato Prison also constructed the roads that connected 
Kabato with Fukagawa and Kabato with Mashike.  Then, settlers came to 
Uryuu Farm.  They were tenant farmers.  ( Kabato Prison was located in 
what is now Tsukigata Town, Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

In the early stages of development, tenant farmers sold forestry 
products, such as timbers, matchsticks, and railroad sleepers.  Those 
were exported to Naichi ( Honshuu island ) and the Chinese continent. 

The headquarters of Uryuu Farm was located at the current bus stop 
``Kyuu-shigai ( old town )'' in Uryuu Town.  The bus stop is located 
near the Ishikari River. 

In the Meiji period, paddle-wheel steamers sailed on the Ishikari 
River.  The main river ports were Ishikari, Ebetsu, and Sorachi-buto 
( now Takikawa ).  Settlers boarded paddle-wheel steamers and headed to 
Sorachi-buto.  They crossed the Ishikari River by boat, followed a path 
surrounded by grass, and entered Uryuu Farm. 

( For large farms in the Meiji period, see Niki Town in Shiribeshi sub-
  prefecture. )
( For paddle-wheel steamers, see Takikawa City in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. )

#2

Sanetomi Sanjou was the most influential owner of Uryuu Farm.  In 1891 
( Meiji 24 ), he died of influenza at the age of 53 years old.  It was 
during Russian influenza pandemic.

After his death, the huge Uryuu Farm was dismantled in 1893 ( Meiji 
26 ).  In Uryuu Town, three farms were established on the site of Uryuu 
Farm.  They were ; 

( 1 ) Hachisuka Farm, 3,164 hectares of flat land, 3,000 hectares of 
mountains, ( 2 ) Toda Farm, 1,231 hectares of flat land, ( 3 ) 
Machimura Farm, 50 hectares of arable land, 30 hectares of pasture. 

These farms were also large.  Currently, the average cultivated land 
area per farm is 23 hectares in Hokkaidou and 1.6 hectares in the rest 
of Japan ( calculated by 2010 Census of Agriculture ). 

In 1893 ( Meiji 26 ), tenant farmers in Hachisuka Farm cultivated 
adzuki beans ( 70 hectares ), corn ( 21 hectares ), wheat ( 21 
hectares ), oats ( 16 hectares ), and pasture ( 11 hectares ).  The 
cumulative revenue of Hachisuka Farm was in the red for 11 years ( from 
1893 to 1903 ).

The owners of Hachisuka Farm and Toda Farm were absentee landlords.  In 
Machimura Farm, Kin-ya Machimura engaged in dairy farming.  He was the 
former manager of Uryuu Farm.  

Hachisuka and Toda farms gradually opened up their land to tenant 
farmers.  Disputes between landlords and tenant farmers ( ``kosaku-
sougui'' ) arose from 1920 to 1932.  The Hokkai Times documented this ; 
``Peasants uprising.  150 tenant farmers stormed the headquarters of 
Hachisuka Farm.  The situation isn't easy.''

In 1917, Hirotaka Machimura, who was a son of Kin-ya Machimura, opened 
Machimura Farm in Tarukawa ( now Ishikari City ).  In 1928, Machimura 
Farm moved from Tarukawa to Tsuishikairi ( now Ebetsu City ).  In 1944, 
Machimura Farm in Uryuu Village was disbanded.

After the Second World War, Hachisuka and Toda farms were disbanded due 
to the post-war agrarian reform ( ``nouchi-kaihou'' ) led by Douglas 
MacArthur.  

( For Machimura Farm, see Ebetsu City in Ishikari sub-prefecture. )

#3

There was Ishikari-oiwake Station on the Sasshou Line.  A monument to 
the former station is located near Uryuu Rice Center.  The name 
``Oiwake'' is Japanese.  It means ``junction.'' 

Ishikari-oiwake was a junction between Ishikari country ( Ishikari-
no-koni ) and Teshio country ( Teshio-no-kuni ).  The road to Teshio 
was built by prisoners.  The name ``Oiwake'' also exists in Abira Town 
( Iburi sub-prefecture ) and Sapporo City ( Ishikari sub-prefecture ). 

#4

Uryuu Rice Cener is managed by JA Kita-sorachi.  ( The abbreviation 
``JA'' means Japan Agricultural Cooperatives.  It is also called 
``noukyou'' in Japanese. )  I visited Uryuu Rice Center in 2015, during 
the bus excursion organized by Hokkaidou Geographical Society.  

At Uryuu Rice Center, I heard this ; ``The main rice varieties are 
Nanatsu-boshi ( 55 percent of planted rice ), Yume-pirika ( 20 
percent ), and Kirara 397 ( 20 percent ).''

As for Nanatsu-boshi, the taste was ranked as Special A in 2010 and 
2011.  Special A is the highest rating inspected by Japan Grain 
Inspection Association.  The taste of Yume-pirika was also ranked as 
Spacial A in 2011 and 2012. 

In the past, only Uonuma-koshihikari from Niigata Prefecture was ranked 
as Special A.  The standards may have changed.  Kirara 397 is the first 
famous rice variety from Hokkaidou. )  ( For Kirara 397, see Note No. 6 
below. )

Explanation at Uryuu Rice Center continues ; ``The abundant melt water 
from Mt. Shokanbetsu-dake makes Uryuu rice delicious.  Some say that 
global warming is good for paddy rice cultivation in Hokkaidou.  But,

( continue ) --> in Uryuu, the amount of snowfall is important.  The 
more snow on the mountain ( Mt. Shokanbetsu-dake ), the tastier the 
rice.  So, the grain elevator was named ``The Tower of Shokan ( Shokan-
no-tou ).'' 

The same thing can be said to Uonuma-koshihikari in Niigata Prefecture. 
The abundant melt water from the Echigo Three Mountains, including Mt. 
Hakkaisan, makes the rice delicious. 
 
``We agricultural cooperative strives to maintain the deliciousness of 
rice.  For example, we don't sell Yume-pirika before polishing, because 
ordinary polishing machine ruin the taste.  ( Rice polishing is called 
``sei-mai'' in Japanese. )  

( continue ) --> The rice is also preserved with the latest storage 
technology.  That's a low-temperature storage method developed by the 
Faculty of Agriculture, Hokkaidou University.

( continue ) --> We don't use the name `Uryuu-mai ( Uryuu Rice )' for 
the rice cultivated after other crops.  The taste is inferior.  The 
rice cultivated on peatland also testes inferior.''   

#5

This is the brief history of rice cultivation in Uryuu Town ( by 
``Uryuu Town history ( Uryuu-chou-shi )'' published in 1969 ) ;
In 1893, Hachisuka Farm surveyed the possibility of irrigation from 
the Oshirarika River.  In 1904, irrigation canals and paddy fields were 
established. 

In 1910, the direct seeding method ( ``jika-maki'' ) was introduced and 
paddy rice cultivation expanded.  The method skipped the work of making 
nursery beds.  The rice varieties at the time were Kurogue-wase and 
Bouzu.  ``Kurogue-wase'' means ``black hair, early ripen'' and  
``Bouzu''' means ``no hair'' or ``monk.''   

In particular, Bouzu ( no hair ) variety was suitable to the direct 
seeding method, because it easily sank into the mud of rice paddy.  
Awns of Bouzu were very short.  ( Awn is called ``nogui'' or ``nogue'' 
in Japanese. )  For the direct seeding method, farmers select heavy 
seeds by floating them in salt water.  

( For Akague ( red hair ) variety, see Kita-hiroshima City in Ishikari 
  sub-prefecture. ) 
( Oshirarika = \u5C3E\u767D\u5229\u52A0.  nogui = \u8292.  nogue = 
  \u91CE\u6BDB. ) 
 
Uryuu became known as the ``Uryuu of Rice'' during the Taishou period 
( from 1913 to 1926 ).  But, poor harvests continue as follows ; In 
1912, crop failure by low temperatures.  In 1913, crop failure.  In 
1926, crop failure by unfavorable weather after spring. In 1928, crop 
failure by hailstorm.  

In 1931, severe crop failure by low temperatures after spring.  In 
1932, severe crop failure by flooding.  Farmers purchased government-
disposed rice ( ``seifu-haraisague-mai'' ).  In 1934, crop failure by 
low temperatures in August.  In 1938, crop failure by drought.  In 
1940, crop failure by rice blast ( ``imochi-byou'' ).

In 1941, crop failure by low temperatures.  In 1943, crop failure by 
drought.  In 1945, crop failure by unfavorable climate.  In 1954, crop 
failure by typhoon.  In 1956, crop failure by low temperatures in 
summer.  From 1964 to 1966, crop failures by low temperatures.

Tenant farmers in Hachisuka Farm and Toda Farm cultivated not only 
paddy rice but also foxtail millet ( ``awa'' ) and glutinous proso 
millet ( ``ina-kibi'' ) for food.  There were hopes and 
disappointments.   

In 1968, the production of rice in Uryuu exceeded 200,000 hyou ( 12,000 
tons ).  That was at the same level as current production.  In the 
same year, farmers established an organization to produce high-quality 
rice.  They studied how to change from quantity to quality.

In 1985, the rate of 1st-class rice ( ``ittou-mai'' ) exceeded 50 
percent.  In 1988, Kirara 397 became the recommended variety.  In 1989, 
the rate of 1st-class rice exceeded 70 percent.  Today, the rate 
exceeds 90 percent.  

The grade ``1st-class rice'' is judged by the appearance before 
polishing.  The grade ``Special A'' is judged by the taste of the 
cooked rice.  ( For Special A rice, see Note No. 4 above. )  
 
#6

These are delicious rice varieties in Hokkadou ; ( 1 ) Kirara 397, 
cultivated from the early 1990s to today, ( 2 ) Hoshino-yume, 
cultivated from the late 1990s to the early 2000s, ( 3 ) Nanatsu-boshi, 
cultivated from the early 2000s to today, and ( 4 ) Yume-pirika, 
cultivated from the 2010s to today. 

In the past, Hokkaidou's rice wasn't delicious.  The former rice was 
bred to be resistant to the cold climate.  The rice was called ``neko-
matagui ( cat, step over ).''  The situation changed.  ( For cat rice, 
see Utashinai City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

Kirara 397 is the first delicious rice variety.  It's resistant to the 
cold climate and rice blast.  It can be cultivated even in peatland.  
Kirara 397 is still popular as commercial rice.  Commercial rice is 
used in restaurants and lunch box shops.  It's not as expensive as the 
ordinary rice that we can buy at supermarkets. 

Kirara 397 has been used at beef bowl ( ``gyuu-don'' ) chain 
restaurants.  Beef bowl is topped with a lot of soup ( ``tare'' ).  
Kirara 397 doesn't get watery right after the soup is poured over the 
top.
 
Nanatsu-boshi is the third delicious rice variety. It was bred for home 
consumption.  At last, Hokkadou's rice was used for home consumption in 
the 2000s.  Before that, urban residents in Hokkaidou purchased the 
rice cultivated in Honshuu island.  ( In Hokkaidou, the rice cultivated 
in Honshuu island is called  ``naichi-mai.'' )

Rice for home consumption is more expensive than commerce rice.  It is 
sold at supermarkets.  Rice for commerce is not sold at supermarkets.  
It includes imported rice.  The distribution route of imported rice is 
unknown to ordinary people. 
   
A master's thesis submitted to Graduate School of Letters, Hokkaidou 
Univerisy, dealt with the rice cultivation in Heilongjiang Province 
( Kokuryuu-kou-shou ), China.  The province is now a large producing 
area of japonica rice.

Heilongjiang Province is located in northern China.  The climate is 
cold.  The rice cultivation method was imported from Hokkaidou.  When I 
was a student, northern China was a large producing area of wheat.  The 
situation changed. 
  
( For average cultivated paddy rice area per farm, see Kaminokuni Town 
  in Oshima sub-prefecture. )
( For delicious rice production, see Yuni Town in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. ) 

( For Kirara 397 and Yume-pirika, see Pippu Town in Kamikawa sub-
  prefecture. ) 
( For Nanatsu-boshi, see Iwamizawa City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
( For old rice varieties, see Kita-hiroshima City in Ishikari sub-
  prefecture. ) 

( For rice consumption, see the note of Enbetsu Town in Rumoi sub-
  prefecture. ) 
( For Rice Land, see Fukagawa Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )  

( For rice seed production, see Chippubetsu Town in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. )
( For the 1993 crop failure, see Kaminokuni Town in Oshima sub-
  prefecture. )
 
#7

As for Uryuu-numa Marsh ( Uryuu-numa-shitsu-guen ), travelers enjoy 
wild flowers.  Those are skunk cabbage ( Mizu-bashou ) in June, 
Hokkaidou daylily ( Ezo-kanzou ) in July, and large plantain lily 
( Tachi-gui-boushi ) in August.

In Honshuu island, skunk cabbage of Oze Marsh is famous.  The altitude 
is 1,400 meters.  Uryuu-numa Marsh is at an altitude of 850 meters.
Uryuu-numa Marsh is called the Oze of Hokkaidou.
 
In Hokkaidou, skunk cabbage grows from an altitude of zero meters.  In 
the case of Hokkaidou University, skunk cabbage grows in the Sakushu-
kotoni River.  The altitude is 14 meters.  The leaves of skunk cabbage 
are big.

There is a famous Japanese song ``Memory of summer ( Natsu-no-
omoide ).''  It includes these lyrics, ``Flowers of skunk cabbage 
( Mizu-bashou ) are blooming, far away Oze.''

Because of this song, many Japanese people think that skunk cabbage 
blooms in summer.  Actually, skunk cabbage blooms in early spring. 
In Sapporo, skunk cabbage blooms in early May.  It's around the same 
time as the cherry blossom.

Although its English name is skunk cabbage, Japanese people have a good 
image of the plant because of the song.  Flowers of skunk cabbage and 
cherry have no scent.  Flowers of lilac and false acacia ( black 
locust, ``nise-akashia'' ), which bloom later, have good scents. 
 
#8

Large red pine trees grow at the site of the former Hachisuka Farm. 
They were transplanted from Honshuu island.  Red pine trees are not 
native in Hokkaidou.  They are ``thinking-of-hometown tree ( `bou-kyou-
ju' ) ( \u671B\u90F7\u6A39 ).'' 

Mochiaki Hachisuka was the owner of Hachisuka Farm.  He bought red pine 
seedlings from a Buddhist temple in Toukyou.  The temple is Kaizenji in 
Matsugaya, Taitou Ward.  The name ``Matsugaya'' means ``pine tree 
valley.''  There used to be many pine trees in central Toukyou.
 
There are three good-luck trees in Japanese culture.  They are pine, 
bamboo, and plum.  They are called ``Shou-chiku-bai.'' ( Bamboo is 
actually a grass. )  

Pine tree is called ``matsu'' in Japanese.  The word ``matsu'' 
signifies ``wait ( `matsu' ).''  Bamboo signifies to grow straight.

Plum tree is called ``ume'' in Japanese.  The word ``ume'' signifies 
``give birth ( `ume' ).''  ( In Portuguese, ``give birth'' is said 
``dar a luz.''  It means ``give the light.''  People desire light and 
freedom. ) 

( For red pine tree, see Nanae Town in Oshima sub-prefecture. )
( For thinking-of-hometown tree, see Furubira Town in Shiribeshi sub-
  prefecture. )
