|                  |
|     TAKIKAWA     |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 18  |
 ------------------
AREA 116 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 14, CULTIVATED 43 ; POP 43,170 ( 2010 ), 
49,591 ( 1990 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 6.7 C ( AV 1981-2010 ), PRECIPI 
1,164 MM, SNOW 921 CM ( AV 1982-2010 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = GLIDER & 
COSMOS.
 
LOCATION    

Takikawa ( ta-ki-ka-wa ) ( \u6EDD\u5DDD ) is a city in Sorachi sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Takikawa is an inland 
city located at the confluence of the Ishikari River and the Sorachi 
River. 

NAME 

The name ``Taki-kawa'' is Japanese.  It means ''waterfall, river.''  
It's a translation of ``so-rapci-pet'' in Ainu language, which means 
``waterfall, complexly falling, river.''

The original name of Takikawa was Sorachi-buto.  The word ``buto'' 
comes from ``put'' in Ainu language, which means a river confluence.  
Sorachi-buto was the confluence of the Sorachi River and the Ishikari 
River.  #1  
 
HISTORY  

In the Meiji period, paddle-wheel steamers navigated on the Ishikari 
River.  The terminus was Sorachi-buto ( now Takikawa ).  The port was 
located on the east bank ( left bank ) of the Ishikari River.  It was 
built on a river terrace.  

In 1890 ( Meiji 23 ), Takikawa military settlers' colony ( Takikawa-
tonden-heison ) was established.  The administrative office ( ``kochou-
yakuba'' ) of Takikawa village was established.  In 1898 ( Meiji 31 ), 
Takikawa Station on the Governmental Hokkaidou Railway ( now Hakodate 
Main Line ) opened. 

In 1906 ( Meiji 23 ), Takikawa Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1909 ( Meiji 42 ), Takikawa Village ( 1st-class 
municipality ) was established.  Ebeotsu ( \u6C5F\u90E8\u4E59 ) Village 
was established by separating from Takikawa.

In 1910 ( Meiji 43 ), Takikawa Town was established.  In 1952 ( Shouwa 
27 ), Ebeotsu Town was established.  In 1958 ( Shouwa 33 ), Takikawa 
City was established.  In 1971 ( Shouwa 46 ), Takikawa City merged 
Ebeotsu Town.  #2  #3  #4

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Nanporo 
  Town. ) 

CITY HALL, TRAFFIC 

The city hall of Takikawa is located near the confluence of the 
Ishikari River and the Sorachi River.  National Route 12 ( Chuuou-
kokudou ) and the Hakodate Main Line run.  ( Naitonal Route 12 is 
straight between Bibai City and Sunagawa City.  It turns 45 degrees 
west, crosses the Sorachi River, and enters Takikawa City. )

Near the city hall, National Route 38 ( Ashibetsu-kokudou ) branches 
off from National Route 12.  The Nemuro Main Line branches off from the 
Hakodate Main Line.  The bypass of National Route 12 opened in 1990.  
Takikawa is a traffic hub in the center of the Ishikari Plain.  #5 
 
INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Takikawa are agriculture, food processing, 
commerce, and sightseeing.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 
2,191 hectares in 2015, wheat 796, buckwheat 455, beans ( soybeans 179, 
adzuki beans 24, other beans 5 ), pasture 131, onions 54, 

( continue ) --> orchards 30 ( apples 23, plums 1, other fruits 1+X ), 
tomatoes 6, green onions 5, fodder 4, cucumbers 3, flowers 3, melons 2, 
radishes 1, yams 1, other grans 117, and other vegetables 41+X 
( asparagus ) ; dairy cows 191 head in 2015.  

Local specialties are onions, domestic duck, mutton for barbecue, 
rapeseed oil, and rape flower.  Domestic duck is called Aigamo in 
Japanese.  Grilled mutton is called Jinguisu-kan.

The planted area of rape is 180 hectares in 2014.  It's the largest in 
the country.  Rape blooms in May.  ( In Toukyou, rape blooms in 
March. )  As for sightseeing, Takikawa Sky Park is located on the east 
bank of the Ishikari River.  

As for commerce, national chain stores are located along the 
bypass of National Route 12.  Along National Route 12, small local 
shops are closed.  They became the residences of the former shop 
owners.  ( In neighboring Sunagawa City, small local shops still open 
along the national highway. )  #6  #7  #8  #9  #10
 
NOTE    
 
#1

A confluence is a border.  The confluence of the Ishikari River and the 
Sorachi River borders municipalities of Takikawa, Sunagawa, and Shin-
totsukawa.  

The border of these municipalities is curved.  The line signifies an 
old meander.  The etymology of ``meander'' is Maiandros, which is a god 
of the river in Greek mythology.  In Japanese, meander is called ``da-
kou,'' which means ``snake, go.''  A river was likened to a dragon. 
 
#2

In 1892 ( Meiji 25 ), Sorachi-buto Station on the Hokkaidou Colliery 
Railway opened.  The station was located on the east bank of the 
Sorachi River.  The site of the former station is now located in 
Sunagawa City.

In 1898 ( Meiji 31 ), the railroad crossed the Sorachi River and 
reached to Asahikawa.  Sorachi-buto Station closed.  Takikawa Station 
opened.

To the west of Takikawa Station was a river port.  Paddle steamers and 
ferry boats arrived at the river port in the Meiji period.  The former 
site of the port is now Pier Park ( Hatoba-kouen ).  ( An old river 
port in Honshuu island is called ``kashi ( \u6CB3\u5CB8 ) '' in 
Japanese. )

An iron bridge made in the UK spanned the Sorachi River.  The location 
was between Takikawa Station and the former Sorachi-buto Station.  
Later, the bridge was relocated to the upper reaches of the 
Chitose River in Chitose City, Ishikari sub-prefecture.

The old iron bridge is now used for sightseeing.  Old iron bridges are 
beautiful.  They are made from many parts.  People think, ``Well 
done!''  ( The Yotsuya-mitsuke bridge was also relocated from the 
center of Toukyou to the Tama Hills. ) 

In 1909 ( Meiji 42 ), the Kushiro Line opened.  It connected stations 
of Asahikawa, Kami-furano, Shimo-furano, Obihiro, and Kushiro.  In 1913 
( Taishou 2 ), the Kushiro Main Line opened.  It connected stations of 
Takikawa, Shimo-furano, Obihiro, and Kushiro.  The section between 
Asahikawa and Shimo-furano became the Furano Line.

In 1921 ( Taishou 10 ), the railway was extended to Nemuro Station.  It 
was renamed the Nemuro Main Line.  In 1942 ( Shouwa 17 ), Shimo-furano 
Station was renamed Furano Station.

In the 1993 film ``Not Yet ( Maadadayo ),'' a railway nerd 
( ``tetsudou-otaku'' ) appears.  He recits the names of every station 
from Nemuro to Nishi-kagoshima.  Nemuro Station is located in the east 
of Hokkaidou island.  Nishi-kagoshima Station was located in the south 
of Kyuushuu island ( now Kagoshima-chuuou Station ).   
   
#3

``Paddle-wheel steamers'' are called ``gairin-sen'' in Japanese.  
Paddle-wheel steamers operated on the Ishikari River from 1884 to 1935.
In Honshuu island, paddle-wheel steamers operated on the Tone River and 
Lake Kasumiga-ura from 1877 to 1931, and in the Kanmon Straits from 
1919 to 1942.

Susquehanna of United States Navy was also a paddle-wheel steamer.  
Susquehanna came to Japan in 1853 and 1854.  The commander was Matthew 
Perry.  He pressured the Tokugawa Shogunate to open Japanese ports.  In 
1865, the Tokugawa Shogunate bought the battleship Kaiten from the USA. 
Kaiten was a paddle-wheel steamer too. 

Models of paddle steamers on the Ishikari River are displayed at the 
Ebetsu City Museum and Hamanasu Park in Ishikari City.  A model of 
Susuquehanna is displayed in Shimoda City, Shizuoka Prefecture 
( HTTP://2242.MOOO.COM/POSI/95IZU/1483.JPG ).  ( When I was a child, 
boys made plastic models of ships, planes, castles, and Gundam. ) 

Today, paddle wheelers are for sightseeing.  They navigate in Lake 
Biwa, Lake Tadami, and Toukyou Disneyland.  The wheels splash water.  
Paddle-wheel steamers are slower than screw ships, but they can 
navigate in shallower waters.

Paddle-wheel steamers on the Mississippi River in the USA are famous.  
They are stern-wheelers.  Japanese paddle-wheel steamers and 
Susquehanna were side-wheelers.  Stern-wheelers are more useful in 
shallow waters.

( For Kaiten ( ship ), see Mori Town in Oshima sub-prefecture. ) 
( For Matthew Perry, see Hakodate City in Oshima sub-prefecture. )

#4

In 1890 ( Meiji 23 ), Takikawa military settlers' colony ( Takikawa-
tonden-heison ) was established.  440 families immigrated.  The 
location was around where the city hall is now.

In 1894 ( Meiji 27 ), Ebeotsu military settlers' colony ( Ebeotsu-
toden-heison ) was established.  400 families immigrated.  The location 
was north of Takikawa.  Those military settlers fought in the fierce 
battle of Hill 203 during the Russo-Japanese War ( 1904-1905 ). 

( For military settlers ( Tonden-hei ), see Fukagawa City and 
  Chippubetsu Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

#5  

National Route 12 starts from Chuuou Ward in Sapporo City, Ishikari 
sub-prefecture.  It runs parallel with the Hakodate Main Line.  The 
terminus is the 4-jou ( yo-jou ) street of Asahikawa City, Kamikawa 
sub-prefecture. 

National Route 38 starts from Takikawa City, Sorachi sub-prefecture.  
It runs parallel with the Nemuro Main Line, passes the Furano Basin and 
the Tokachi Plain, and reaches Kushiro City.  The terminus is Nusamai 
Rotary.  ( Kushiro City is located in Kushiro sub-prefecture. )
 
In a 1981 TV drama ``From the North Country ( Kita-no-kuni-kara ),'' a 
family moves from Toukyou to Hokkaidou.  The first scene in Hokkadou is 
on the Nemuro Main Line.  The protagonist Gorou and his daughter Hotaru 
look out the train window and say ; 
( Hotaru ) ; River!  ( ``Kawa!'' )
( Gorou )  ; Sorachi River.  ( ``Sorachi-gawa-da.'' ) 
 
#6  

The main rice varieties cultivated in Takikawa are Nanatsu-boshi and 
Yume-pirika.  Nanatsu-boshi was bred in Iwamizawa City.  Yume-pirika 
was bred in Pippu Town.  Takikawa is located at the mid point between 
Iwamizawa City and Pippu Town.

Hokuren Rice Seed Center is located in Takikawa City.  ( ``Hokuren'' 
means the Hokkaidou Federation of Agricultural Cooperatives. )  Also, 
Genetic Resources Division of Hokkaidou Agricultural Experiment Station 
is located in Takikawa City.  Takikawa is a navel of rice cultivation 
in Hokkaidou.
 
( For rice, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

Wheat is the second largest in area.  Farmers cultivate wheat for hard 
flour.  Hard flour is called ``kyouriki-ko'' in Japanese.  ``Ramen'' 
noodles are made from locally produced hard flour.  They are sold as 
Takikawa-ramen.  

The wheat varieties for hard flower are Haru-yutaka and Haru-yo-koi.  
The word ``haru'' means ``spring'' in Japanese.  The word ``haru'' 
implies spring wheat.  

Cultivating spring wheat is difficult.  One reason is that its growing 
period is shorter than winter wheat.  Spring wheat seeds are sown in 
spring and harvested in summer.  Winter wheat seeds are sown in autumn 
and harvested in summer.

Cultivating modern wheat varieties is difficult.  One reason is that it 
can rain during the harvest.  Ripe ( ra'ip ) wheat can sprout on the 
ears when it rains. 

There is a word ``baku-shuu'' in Japanese, which means ``wheat, 
autumn.''  Wheat ripens and turns yellow from early summer to mid 
summer, when other trees and grasses are green.  The landscape is 
beautiful.  In Honsyuu island, the wheat autumn ( ``baku-shuu'' ) is 
late May.  In Hokkaidou island, the wheat autumn is early August. 
 
#7  

Onions of Takikawa are sold with the trade name ``Taki-tama.''  
( ``Onion'' is called ``tama-negui'' in Japanese. )  The onions are 
also processed into onion sauce.  The onion sauce is used in Jinguisu-
kan ( grilled mutton ). 

Apples are sold with the trade name ``Ebeotsu-ringo.''  The word 
``ringo'' in Japanese and means ``apple.''  ( The Beatles member Ringo 
Starr was named after Johnny Ringo, who was an outlaw in the USA. )   
 
#8  

Grilled mutton is called Jinguisu-kan.  Matsuo Jinguisu-kan Co. sells 
seasoned mutton for Jinguisu-kan.  The head office is located in 
Takikawa City.  The mutton is seasoned with a sweet sauce.

There was Takikawa Sheep Ranch from 1906 to 2010.  It was managed by 
the Ministry of Agriculture.  In the beginning of the 20th century, the 
government aimed to become self-sufficient in wool.  Wool was used in 
the army, police officers, and railroad workers.  The government 
launched the One Million Sheep Project.

Vikings wore wool.  British fishermen wore wool.  Wool in Japan was 
needed especially to prepare for the war with Russia.  The Battle of 
Nomonhan ( Battle of Khalkhin Gol ) occurred in September.  The Battle 
of Hill 203 was happened in November. 

Japanese people started eating mutton in the eary 20th century.  But, 
they didn't like the smell.  The government sheep ranch developed a 
sweet sauce for grilled mutton.  Raw mutton was dipped in the sauce and 
grilled.  The sauce is the origin of Matsuo Jinguisu-kan.

Grilled mutton was named ``Jinguisu-kan'' in the 1930s.  It was named 
by Tokuzou Komai.  He was a railroad officer for the South Manchuria 
Railroad.  ( Tokuzou Komai --> http://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/
%E9%A7%92%E4%BA%95%E5%BE%B3%E4%B8%89 ) 

Manchuria was located in what is now north China.  Local people ate 
mutton hotpot.  It was brought to Japan and named Jinguisu-kan after 
the Mongolian emperor Genghis Khan.  

The railroad officer Tokuzou Komai graduated from College of 
Agriculture, Tohoku Imperial University ( now Hokkaidou University ).  
His graduation thesis was about soybean production in Manchuria.

Before World War II, Japan occupied Manchuria.  After the war, mainland 
Japan was occupied until 1952.  The Bonin islands ( Ogasawara islands ) 
was occupied until 1968 and Okinawa island was until 1972.  The islands 
were returned by the USA. 

After World War II, mutton began to be imported.  Today, almost 100 
percent of the mutton consumed in Japan is imported from Australia and 
New Zealand. 

In Hokkaidou University, students and teachers enjoy ``Jinpa.''  Jinpa 
is an abbreviation for Jinguisu-kan party.  It's Mutton barbecue party. 
When I heard the mysterious word for the first time, I imagined 
something like a dance party.  The pronunciation resembles Jitterbug 
( http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jitterbug ).

The sweet sauce used for grilled mutton tastes like ``teri-yaki.''  
Traditionally, fish grilled with a sweet sauce is called ``teri-yaki.'' 
Today, McDonald's sells teri-yaki burgers.  I think ``teri-yaki'' is 
salty for the elderly people ( like me ).  Westerners eat a lot of 
meat.  They season grilled meat with a little salt ( Nihei lab 2025b ).
 
( For sheep, see Toyohira Ward in Sapporo City, Ishikari sub-
  prefecture, and Shibetsu City in Kamikawa sub-prefecture. ) 
 
#9  

Rape ( plant ) is called ``abura-na'' in Japanese.  It means ``oil, 
vegetable.''  Rape is also called ``na-tane'' in Japanese.  It means 
``rape, seed.''  The major producer of rape is Canada.  Canola oil is 
the rapeseed oil developed in Canada.  Japan imports 99 percent of 
rapeseed from Canada.

Before kerosene lamps became popular, rapeseed oil was used in lamps.  
That was before the early 20th century.  Imports of rapeseed were 
liberalized in 1971.  Before that, Japan was 100 percent self-
sufficient in rape oil. 

In the past, rape was cultivated in paddy fields.  It was cultivated 
before rice.  The song ``Hazy Moonlit Night ( Oboro-zuki-yo )'' was 
sung in elementary school.  It describes the rape flower fields in 
spring like this ; 
``Nano-hana-batake-ni-irihi-usure, 
  ( The sun sets over the rape flower field ),
  Miwatasu-yama-no-ha-kasumi-fukashi.
  ( The edge of the mountain I see is hazy ).''

Rape flowers in paddy fields disappeared.  But, rape cultivation 
revived in Takikawa City and Obihiro City.  Today, rape is a crop for 
the landscape. 
  
#10  

Takikawa Sky Park includes a glider airfield, baseball fields, and golf 
courses.  In 1981, the Ishikari River suffered major flood damage.  At 
the time, the mayor inspected the flood damage from the air.  He 
suggested the possibility of bringing air sports in the city.

In the following year, a glider airfield opened.  Projects to 
revitalize the city through sports started.  As for baseball fields, 
Takikawa West Hgh School is strong in baseball.

Popular sports came from the UK and the USA, for example, baseball, 
soccer ( football ), volleyball, basketball, ice hockey, cricket, golf, 
and horse riding.  Those people are geniuses at playing. 

( For baseball, see Toyohira Ward in Sapporo City, Ishikari sub-
  prefecture. )
( For sports, see Tomakomai City in Iburi sub-prefecture and Moseushi 
  Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. ) 
 __________________
|                  |
|     AKABIRA      |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 19  |
 ------------------
AREA 130 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 93, CULTIVATED 7 ; POP 12,637 ( 2010 ), 
19,409 ( 1990 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = MINE SHAFT, MINE DUMP, STATION, & 
BURNING OF THE CHARACTER BIG. 
  
LOCATION   

Akabira ( a-ka-bi-ra ) ( \u8D64\u5E73 ) is a city in Sorachi sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Akabira is an inland 
city.  The area is located in the basin of the Sorachi River.  The 
river flows across the Yuubari Mountains. 

NAME  

The name ``Aka-bira'' is a mixture of a translation of an Ainu word and 
an Ainu word.  The word ``aka'' means ``red'' in Japanese, which was 
translated from ``fure'' in Ainu language.

The word ``bira'' comes from ``pira'' in Ainu language, which means 
``cliff.''  So, the name ``Aka-bira'' means ``red, cliff.''  It 
signified that a cliff along the Sorachi River appeared red.  #1

HISTORY   

In 1891 ( Meiji 24 ), agricultural settlement started.  In 1897 ( Meiji 
30 ), the first road opened.  In 1913 ( Taishou 2 ), Akabira Station 
opened on the Kushiro Main Line ( now Nemuro Main Line ).  In 1922 
( Taishou 11 ), Akabra Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established.  It was separated from Utashinai Village. 

In 1929 ( Shouwa 4 ), Akabira Village ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1943 ( Shouwa 18 ), Akabra Town was established.  In 
1954 ( Shouwa 29 ), Akabira City was established. 

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Nanporo 
  Town. )

CITY HALL, TRAFFIC, POPULATION 

The city hall of Akabira is located along the Sorachi River.  The 
Nemuro Main Line and National Route 38 ( Ashibetsu-kokudou ) pass.  
In the past, Akabira thrived on coal mining.  The population peaked at 
58,713 in 1959 ( by Akabira City ).

These were the main coal mines ; ( 1 ) Akabira Coal Mine, operated by 
Sumitomo group, closed in 1994, ( 2 ) Akama Coal Mine, operated by 
Hokutan ( Hokkaidou Colliery & Steamship ), closed in 1973, ( 3 ) 
Yuubetsu Moshiri Coal Mine, operated by Mitsubishi, closed in 1969, and 
( 4 ) Toyosato Coal Mine, operated by Shouwa-denkou, closed in 1967. 

INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Akabira are manufacturing, agriculture, and 
sightseeing.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 362 hectares in 
2015, buckwheat 112, pasture 80, flowers 2 ( green house ), potatoes 1, 
tomatoes 1, other vegetables 7+X, and other grains 1 ; dairy cows X 
( concealed ) head in 2015.

As for manufacturing, after the declining of coal mining, four 
industrial parks were established.  The city attracted factories of 
paper ( tissue, toilet paper ), food processing, woodworking, metal 
processing, and electronic components. 

As for agriculture, roses and orchids are cultivated in greenhouses.  
Roses were drawn on the former country sign ( gateway sign ).  
Floriculture is possible in small land.

As for sightseeing, the old shaft tower of a coal mine stands near 
Akabira Station.  It's a landmark that transcends time and space.  
Travelers are not allowed to climb the tower.  Akabira Station is a 
six-story building.  It looks like a Western castle.  It was built in 
1999.  #2  #3  #4 

There is a tourist stairway on a mine-dump mountain ( ``zuri-yama'' ) 
near Akabira Station.  It features 777 steps and marks the longest 
stairway on a mine-dump mountain in the country.  Travelers can 
observe the Sorachi River and the built-up area of Akabira City from 
the mine-dump mountain.  #5  #6 

The current country sign depicts the burning of the character Big 
( Dai-monji-yaki ) on the mine-dump mountain during a summer 
festival.  

The candy ``Kai-tan-ame'' is a specialty of the city.  It means 
``block, coal, candy.''  It is a coal-like candy.  The color comes from 
bamboo charcoal.  It has been sold since 1932.  Children in the past 
must have played a prank putting real coal in the candy tin.  #7  #8

NOTE   

#1  

As for the name ``Akabira,'' which means ``red, cliff,'' Furubira Town 
in Shiribeshi sub-prefecture has the same etymology.  The place name 
``Red Cliff'' can be found all over the world.  The battle of Red 
Cliffs ( ``Sekiheki-no-tatakai'' ) in ancient China is famous.  Red 
Cliff in Colorado, USA, is a nostalgic mining town.  

( For place names including ``red,'' see Akaigawa Village in 
  Shiribeshi sub-prefecture. )
  
#2  

The mine shaft tower near Akabira Station belonged to Sumitomo Akabira 
Coal Mine.  The shaft sent miners 650 meters underground. ( The 
altitude of Akabira is 55 meters. )

The length of mining tunnels reached 200 kilometers.  One of the 
tunnels connected to a coal mine in neighboring Utashinai City.  Cf. T
he length of the Seikan Tunnel, an undersea tunnel, is 54 kilometers.  

( For Seikan Tunnel, see Fukushima Town in Oshima sub-prefecture. )
 
#3  

In the past, railroads connected Akabira Station and nearby coal mines. 
In 1960, Akabira Station handled the largest freight ( f'reit ) in the 
country.

Today, Akabira Station on the Nemuro Main line looks like a European 
castle.  It includes not only the ticket gates but also Akabira City 
Community Center.  

The distance between Akabira Station and Sapporo Station is 97 
kilometers, using the Nemuro Main Line and the Hakodate Main Line.  
Both railways are operated by JR Hokkaidou. 

The fare is 1,900 yen for local trains and 3,550 yen for express trains 
( as of 2018 ).  Local trains cost 19.6 yen per kilometer.  It's almost 
impossible to commute from Akabira to Sapporo.  But, the train trip 
will be enjoyable. 

In Toukyou, the fare between Shinjuku Station and Minami-oosawa Station 
is 390 yen.  The railroad is operated by the Keiou Line.  The distance 
is 34 kilometers.  It costs 11.5 yen per kilometer.  On the train, no 
one looks at the landscape.  ( We can walk between stations on the 
Keiou Line, but it's difficult to do so on the Hakodate Main Line. )
 
#4

I bought Kikusui ( Kikusui-funaguchi ) at a convenience store in front 
of Akabira Station.  Kikusui is the name of rice wine.  Kikusui is sold 
in cans, whereas rice wine is usually sold in glass cups or small paper 
boxes at stations.
 
Kikusui is produced in Niigata Prefecture.  The alcohol content is 19 
percent.  Generally, the alcohol content of rice wine is 15 percent, 
because water is added.  Kikusui is raw rice wine.  It isn't 
pasteurized ( pa'schu-rized ) or diluted with water. 

The taste of raw rice wine changes by time.  The Kikusui I bought in 
Akabira was seven months old.  According to the explanation on the can, 
it was at the stage of ``Brandy ( matured ).''  It was good.

When I buy Kikusui in Sapporo, the stage is always ``Fruit ( fresh ).'' 
 ( ``Fruit'' is from zero to two months.  ``Brandy'' is from six months 
to one year.  ``Laochuu ( more matured )'' is after a year. ) 

According to the company's web-page, the name ``Kikusui'' comes from a 
traditional Japanese theater ``Nou.''  The title of the theater is 
``Kiku-ji-dou.''

Kiku-ji-dou is a boy ``xian'' in old China.  ( Xian is hermit or wizard 
in English.  It is called ``sen-nin'' in Japanese. )  The boy xian 
drunk dew on the leaves of chrysanthemum.  He was 700 years old.
( ``Kiku'' in ``Kikusui'' refers the chrysanthemum. ``Sui'' refers the 
dew. )

Chrysanthemum ( ``kiku'' ) is a flower that symbolizes longevity.  It 
is used for family crests and family names.  Chrysanthemum is also the 
Imperial crest of Japan.  Ruth Benedict ( 1887-1948 ) wrote the book 
``The Chrysanthemum and the Sword.''

Chrysanthemum is also a flower associated with the dead.  It is 
displayed on the days when we think of the dead.  They are Bon ( mid 
August or mid June ), Higan ( equinox ), and funerals.

( For raw rice wine, see Otaru City in Shiribeshi sub-prefecture. )
( For rice wine, see Asahikawa City in Kamikawa sub-prefecture. )
( For the name ``Kikusui,'' see Shiroishi Ward in Sapporo City, 
  Ishikari sub-prefecture, and Shin-totsukawa Town in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. ) 

#5

A mine-dump mountain is called ``zuri-yama'' or ``zuri'' in Japanese.  
In Kyuushuu island, it is called ``bota'' or ``bota-yama.''  The word 
``zuri'' means stones and low-quality coal extracted from mines.  They 
were piled up for a long time and became a mountain.  A mine-dump 
mountain can spontaneously combust because it contains coal.

When I see mine-dump mountains, I remember this proverb in Portuguese ; 
``Agua mole em pedra dura tanto bate ate que fura.  
  ( Water is soft, but constant dripping makes a hole on a hard 
    rock. )''   ( Agua = \u00C1gua.  ate = at\u00E9. )

#6  

There is a song for coal miners.  It's ``Tankou-bushi ( Coal Mine 
Song ).''  It was sung in a coal mining village in Kyuushuu island. 
These are part of the lyrics ; 
``Tsuki-ga-deta-deta, tsuki-ga-deta, yoi-yoi
  ( The moon rose, the moon rose, yoi-yoi ),
  Uchino-oyamano-ueni-deta
  ( It rose above our coal mine. )''

When I listen to the song, I think that mining villages were utopias, 
although the work was hard.  Our life is also like a long dream.  
 
#7 
 
In Akabira, there is a stone monument to the novelist Doppo Kunikida 
( 1871-1908 ).  His famous work is ``Musasino,'' in which he described 
the natural environment of Musashino.  His works are naturalism.  
( Musashino is a plateau ( pla'-tow ) in western Toukyou. )

He visited the hills along the Sorachi River in 1895.  He wanted to buy 
land to live freely.  He wanted to live with Nobuko.  Before coming to 
Hokkaidou, he proposed to Nobuko, but her parents opposed it.

He was physically too weak to live in Hokkaidou during the pioneering 
period.  He soon returned to Toukyou.  He died of tuberculosis 
( tu-ber-cu-lo'-ses ) at the age of 36. 

( For Doppo Kunikida, see Utashinai City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

#8

There is a castle along National Route 38.  It's a Japanese style 
castle.  It was a showroom for a doll company.  Selling dolls is a 
seasonal business.  Dolls for girls are displayed in March.  Dolls for 
boys are displayed in May.  The number of children decreased.  

The castle is not in use.  By this time, surely tonight, snow will be 
piling up on the roof, silently. 
 __________________
|                  |
|     HOKURYUU     |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 20  |
 ------------------
AREA 159 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 104, CULTIVATED 32 ; COUNTRY = URYUU 
COUNTY ; POP 2,193 ( 2010 ), 3,009 ( 1990 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = SUNFLOWER.
 
LOCATION  

Hokuryuu ( ho-ku-ryu-u ) ( \u5317\u7ADC ) is a town in Sorachi sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Hokuryuu is located on 
the west bank ( right bank ) of the Uryuu River.  

The area extends from the Ishikari Plain to the Mashike Mountains.  Mt. 
Shokanbetsu-dake ( 1,491 meters ) is the border between Hokuryuu, 
Uryuu, Shin-totsukawa, and Mashike. #1

NAME   

The name ``Hoku-ryuu'' means ``north, Uryuu.''  The name ``Uryuu'' 
comes from ``urir-o-pet'' in Ainu language.  It means ``cormorant, 
many, river.''

( For the name ``Uryuu,'' see Uryuu Town in sub-prefecture. )

HISTORY   

In 1893 ( Meiji 26 ), Uryuu Farm was demolished.  An immigrant group 
from Chiba Prefecture immigrated.  They were called Chiba Group 
( Chiba-dantai ).  Baihon ( \u57F9\u672C ) Company managed the 
development.

In 1897 ( Meiji 30 ), an immigrant group from Ishikawa Prefecture 
immigrated.  They were called Kaga Group ( Kaga-dantai ).  ( Kaga is 
an old name of Ishikawa Prefecture. )  In 1899 ( Meiji 32 ), an 
administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) of Hokuryuu village was 
established.  It was separated from Uryuu village. 

In 1914 ( Taishou 3 ), Kami-hokuryuu village ( now Numata Town ) 
separated.  In 1915 ( Taishou 4 ), Hokuryuu Village ( 2nd-class 
municipality ) was established.  In 1961 ( Shouwa 36 ), Hokuryuu Town 
was established.  #2 

( For 2st-class municipality, see the note of Nanporo Town. )

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC 

The town hall of Hokuryuu is located near the confluence of the Uryuu 
River and the Etaibetsu ( \u6075\u5CB1\u5225 ) River.  National Route 
275 ( Sorachi-kokudou or Himawari road ) runs near the town hall.  #3  
#4

National Route 233 ( Rumoi-kokudou ) passes through the northern part 
of the town.  The Sasshou Line ran before 1972.  It was operated by 
Japanese National Railways ( Koku-tetsu ).
 
INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Hokuryuu are agriculture and sightseeing.  
STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 1,881 hectares in 2015, 
buckwheat 585, beans ( soybeans 204, adzuki beans 23, other beans 6 ), 

( continue ) --> wheat 101, melons 13, fodder 7, watermelons 5, pasture 
4, flowers 4, tomatoes 2, other vegetables 21+X, and other grains 11 ; 
dairy cow - ( zero ) head in 2015. 

The cultivated area of sunflowers ( ``himawari'' ) is the largest in 
the country.  Sunflowers cover more than 100 hectares in the whole town 
and 23 hectares in Village of Sunflower ( Himawari-no-sato ).

Sunflowers are cultivated for oil in Western countries and for 
landscape in Japan.  Sunflowers aren't listed in Census of Agriculture 
in Japan.  #5 

NOTE 

#1

The Uryuu River flows from the border between Nakagawa Town and 
Horokanai Town in Kamikawa sub-prefecture.  The length of the Uryuu 
River is 155 kilometers.  It's the second longest tributary in the 
country.  ( The longest tributary is the Sorachi River, whose length is 
195 kilometers.  Both rivers flow into the Ishikari River. )

The Ishikari River is the third longest in the country.  ( The main 
river is 268 kilometers.  The river system is 3,675 kilometers. )  The 
catchment area of the Ishikari River is the second largest.  The 
Ishikari River creates the Ishikari Plain. 
 
#2

In 1910 ( Meiji 43 ), Numata Station ( now Ishikari Numata Station ) on 
the Rumoi Line opened.  It was located to the north of Hokuryuu 
village.  Residents near the station campaigned to relocate the 
administrative office to the north. 

Residents in the south opposed.  After fierce debates, the northern 
part of the village separated.  It became Kami-hokuryuu village ( now 
Numata Town ).

It had already been agreed that several village would be established in 
the former site of Uryuu Farm.  ( Uryuu Farm was a huge aristocratic 
farm in the northern Ishikari Plain. )  

However, the separation of the village led to conflict among the 
residents.  The border was defined by two rivers ; the Biba-ushi River 
and the Horo-niitachi-betsu River  ( by ``Hokuryuu Town history 
( Hokuryuu-chou-shi )'' published in 1969 ). 

( For Uryuu Farm, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
( Biba-ushi = \u7F8E\u8449\u725B.  Horo-niitachi-betsu =
  \u5E4C\u65B0\u592A\u5200\u5225. ) 

#3

The town hall of Hokuryuu is located in Yawara ( \u548C ) district. The 
name ``Yawara'' means ``harmony'' in Japanese.  The district was 
reclaimed by Baihon Company ( Baihon-sha ). 

Baihon Company was an immigration company.  It recruited immigrants 
from Chiba Prefecture.  The company's policy was based on Houtoku 
Thought ( Houtoku-shisou ), which was advocated by Sontoku Ninomiya.  
The immigrants were prohibited from drinking alcohol and smoking.  

Sontoku Ninomiya ( 1787-1856 ) was an agriculturalist in the Edo era.  
He refurbished the economy in rural areas.  He was born in Sagami 
country ( now Kanagawa Prefecture ). 

Ninomiya Town in Tochigui Prefecture was named in honor of Sontoku 
Ninomiya.  Generally, the Japanese place names ``Ichi-no-miya,'' ``Ni-
no-miya,'' and ``San-no-miya'' come from Shintou shrine names.  They 
refer to Ichinomiya ( 1st-grade ) Shrine, Ninomiya ( 2nd-grade ) 
Shrine, and Sannomya ( 3rd-grade ) Shrine.

Statues of Sontoku Ninomiya were displayed in the yards of elementary 
schools.  The statue was carrying firewood and reading a book.  That 
was a model of hardworking Japanese people.  ( In the past, Japan was 
the world's second-largest economy. ) 

The essence of Sontoku Ninomiya's Thought ( Houtoku Thought ) lies in 
( 1 ) contributing to society and ( 2 ) abandoning unnecessary things. 

( For Sontoku Ninomiya's Thought, see Toyokoro Town in Tokachi sub-
  prefecture and Hokuto City in Oshima sub-prefecture. )
  
#4

Prefectural Route 94 ( Mashike-inada-sen ) passes near the town hall of 
Hokuryuu.  The road was part of a prisoner-made road.  Prisoners from 
Kabato Prison built it in the Meiji period.

The prisoner-made road connected Tsukigata in the Ishikari Plain and 
Mashike on the Sea of Japan side.  The road was called the Mashike Road 
( Mashike-douro ).  It's a mountain road that crosses the Mashike 
Mountains. 

( Mashike Town is located in Rumoi sub-prefecture.  Inada district is 
  located in Otoe-chou district, Fukagawa City, Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. )
( For prisoner-made roads, see Tsukigata Town in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. )
 
#5

The interchange is named ``Hokuryuu-himawari ( Hokuryuu sunflower ).''  
Sunflowers bloom from mid-July to mid-August.  The Sunflower Festival 
is held at Village of Sunflower ( Himawari-no-sato ).  Village of 
Sunflower is a municipal park.  It opened in 1989.

In 1979, Hokuryuu Agricultural Cooperative ( now Kita-sorachi 
Agricultural Cooperative ) organized a study tour to Europa.  At the 
time, participants were impressed by the scenery around Belgrade 
airport, Yugoslavia. 

The airport was surrounded by sunflower fields.  The plane was like a 
butterfly in a flower field.  After coming back to Japan, the women's 
division of the agricultural cooperative started to plant sunflowers.

Sunflowers are native to North America.  They are a crop for making 
oil, although Japanese people think they are garden plants, or 
something that American baseball players nibble on, or food for guinea 
pigs.

I traveled to the state of Kansas in the USA.  The receptionist at a 
motel recommended that I go to a nearby sunflower field.  I went.  The 
sunflower field reminded me of the 1970 film ``Girasoli'' 
( Sunflower ).  

In the film, an old soldier has another wife in another country where 
sunflowers bloom.  ( For ``Girasoli,'' see Nihei lab 2023-2025.)
 __________________
|                  |
|     MOSEUSHI     |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 21  |
 ------------------
AREA 49 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 0, CULTIVATED 34 ; COUNTY = URYUU 
COUNTY ; POP 3,462 ( 2010 ), 5,002 ( 1990 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = A COW 
HOLDING RICE.
 
LOCATION  

Moseushi ( mo-se-u-shi ) ( \u59B9\u80CC\u725B ) is a town in Sorachi 
sub-prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Moseushi is located 
in the north of the Ishikari Plain.  The Ishikari River and the Uryuu 
River flow.

Moseushi is an inland town.  It's the third smallest municipality in 
Hokkaidou after Kami-sunagwa Town and Chippubetsu Town.  The area is 
flat.  Oxbow lakes ( crescent moon lakes ) are distributed. 

NAME   

The name ``moseushi'' is derived from ``mose-us-i'' in Ainu language.  
It means ``Irakusa ( hairy nettle ), many, place.''  #1

( Although the town's name includes the word ``ushi ( \u725B ),'' which 
  means ``cow'' in Japanese, and the country sign also includes a cow, 
  there are no cows in the town ( by Agricultural Census ).  Paddy 
  fields spread. )
 
HISTORY 

In 1893 ( Meiji 26 ), Uryuu Farm was abolished.  ( Uryuu Farm was a 
huge noble farm. )  Part of the farm became Kikutei Farm, which was 
located in what are now Moseushi Town and the west of Fukagawa City. 

In the same year, Guenzou Mori, who was the 2nd principal of Sapporo 
Agricultural College ( now Hokkaidou University ), started land 
reclamation.  It was the uncultivated land of Kikutei Farm.  The area 
was included in Fukagawa village at the time. 

In 1898 ( Meiji 31 ), Moseushi Station opened on the Governmental 
Hokkaidou Railway ( Hokkaidou-kansetsu-tetsudou ) ( now Hakodate Main 
Line ).  In 1923 ( Taishou 12 ), Moseushi Village ( 1st-class 
municipality ) was established.  It was separated from Fukagawa Town.  
In 1952 ( Shouwa 27 ), Moseushi Town was established.  #2

( For 1st-class municipality, see the note of Nanporo Town. ) 
( For Uryuu Farm, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
( For Yukisue Kikutei, the owner of Kikutei Farm, see Shiroishi Ward in 
  Sapporo City, Ishikari sub-prefecture. )

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC  

The town hall of Moseushi and the center of the town are located in 
front of Moseush Station on the Hakodate Main Line.  The Memu River 
flows through the center of the town. 

INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industry in Moseushi is agriculture.  Seventy percent of 
the area is cultivated land.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 
2,387 hectares in 2015, wheat 490, beans ( soybeans 89, adzuki beans 
11 ), buckwheat 26, flowers 20, onions 5, other vegetables 10+X, and 
other grains 5 ; dairy cow X ( concealed ) head in 2015.  #3

As for sports, curling is popular.  In the past, girls' volleyball club 
at Moseushi Commercial High School was strong.  

When the volleyball club won the national championship in 1978, the 
newspaper reported ; ``A small town of 6,000 people, minus 25 degrees 
Celsius in winter, pushed away handicaps.''  The high school closed in 
2009 due to decreases in students.  #4

NOTE   

#1 

Hairy nettle ( Irakusa ) is a plant that grows in the mountains and 
abandoned farmland.  It prefers fertile soil.  A curator from Hokkaidou 
University Museum said, ``There are small thorns on the leaf of 
Irakusa.  If you touch the leaf, you feel pain and irritation, so the 
name is Ira-kusa.''

In Japanese, irritation is expressed as ``ira-ira.''  Grass is called 
``kusa.''  The pain of electric fence is instantaneous, but the pain of 
Irakusa continues for a day.

Ainu people made clothes from Irakusa.  They cut dead and dried Irakusa 
and extracted fibers from the bark.  The clothes made from Irakusa are 
displayed in museums and stations.  They are not available for 
purchase. 

The leaf of Irakusa resembles Aoso ( ramie ) and Shiso ( Lamiaceae 
herb ).  Also, gympie stinger in Australia is a plant from the same 
family of Irakusa. 

Aoso ( ramie ) grows On the roadside in the suburbs of Toukyou.  The 
another name of Aoso is Karamushi.  Aoso is also a plant from the same 
family of Irakusa, although the leaves do not cause pain.  Japanese 
people made mosquito nets and clothes from Aoso. 

( For Ainu culture, see Biratri Town in Hidaka sub-prefecture. )
  
#2  

According to ``Moseushi Town history ( Moseushi-chou-shi )'' published 
in 1979 ; ``The Rumoi Line of the Governmental Railway ( Kansetsu-
tetsudou ) opened in 1910 ( Meiji 43 ).  The population of Fukagawa 
Village increased.  In 1918 ( Taishou 7 ), Fukagawa Town was 
established. 

( continue ) --> Then, the gap between urban and rural areas widened.  
Rural residents opined ( ou-pa'ined ) on the need to divide the 
municipality.  In 1923 ( Taishou 12 ), the rural area of Fukagawa Town
was separated as Moseushi Village.''  ( The gap between the nobility 
and the common people caused the French Revolution. ) 
 
#3  

According to ``Moseushi Town history ( Moseushi-chou-shi )'' published 
in 1979 ; ``Agriculture suffered from floods, cold weather, diseases, 
and insect pests.  After indescribable hardships, agriculture shifted 
from upland field farming to paddy field farming.

( continue ) --> Land consolidation completed, and agriculture shifted 
from horse-powered farming to large-scale mechanized farming.  Moseushi 
developed into the rice-producing area that boasts the highest yield 
per land in the prefecture.''  Today, rice fields spread silently.

( For the history of crop failures, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. )
  
#4  

As for the strong volleyball team, the following is written in the 1993 
book ``Moseushi centennial history ( Moseushi-hyakunen-shi )'' ; 
``Teacher Yoshino was assigned to Moseushi Commercial High School in 
1970.  He provided practical coaching and built strong teamwork at the 
women's volleyball club.  Students came from all over the prefecture.'' 
 
Today, sports is a business.  Rich teams gather strong players from all 
over the country.  In the case of high school baseball, the first team 
in northern Japan that won the national championship was Tomakomai High 
School of Komazawa University.  The ace pitcher came from Itami City in 
western Japan.  He later became a pitcher for New York Yankees.  

Private universities including Komazawa University put money on sports. 
For example, Hakone relay marathon is the inter-university competition 
that is broadcast on TV on New Year's Day.  There is no place for 
national universities like Hokkaidou University to compete in the relay 
marathon. 

High school students and their parents watch the relay marathon on TV.  
The university that wins the relay marathon gathers a large number of 
applicants.  That is a matter of dead or alive for their management.  
( Professors at the university get a bonus for checking extra answer 
sheets. )  

I wrote about Brazilian soccer ( football ) in textbooks.  The contents 
were like this ; ``Soccer is popular in Brazil.  Even if you live in 
`favela' ( slum ), if you succeed in soccer, you can become a 
millionaire.  Boys in `favela' play soccer every day.''

When I traveled to Brazil, I heard like this ; ``Today, boys who want 
to be professionals need to join club teams.  They need money.''  The 
saying that everyone has a chance to have great success is a thing 
of the past. 

( For sports, see Tomakomai City in Iburi sub-prefecture and Takikawa 
  City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. ) 
 __________________
|                  |
|     FUKAGAWA     |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 22  |
 ------------------
AREA 529 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 314, CULTIVATED 109 ; POP 23,709 
( 2010 ), 30,671 ( 1990 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 6.5 C ( AV 1981-2010 ), 
PRECIPI 1,022 MM, SNOW 1,029 CM ( AV 1982-2010 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = 
APPLE & RICE. 
   
LOCATION   

Fukagawa ( fu-ka-ga-wa ) ( \u6DF1\u5DDD ) is a city in Sorachi sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  The area is located 
where the Ishikari River enters the Ishikari Plain.

The Ishikari River passes a gorge between the Horonai Mountains and the 
Teshio Mountains and enters the Ishikari Plain.  The Horonai Mountains 
is the northern part of the Yuubari Mountains. 

NAME 

The name ``Fukagawa'' is Japanese, which means ``deep, river.''  It's a 
translation of ``ooho-nay'' in Ainu language, which means ``deep, 
river.''  The original name signified the Oohou ( \u5927\u9CF3 ) River 
that flows near the city hall.  The Oohou River is a tributary of the 
Uryuu River.  The Uryuu River is a tributary of the Ishikari River.

HISTORY 

In 1892 ( Meiji 25 ), Fukagawa village was established.  Its 
administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) was jointly operated with 
the administrative office of Uryuu county, which was located in Shin-
totsukawa village.  

In 1893 ( Meiji 26 ), the huge Uryuu Farm was abolished and divided.  
Kikutei Farm was established in the site what are now Fukagawa City and 
Moseushi Town.  #1

In 1895 ( Meiji 28 ) and 1896 ( Meiji 29 ), three military settlers' 
colonies ( Tonden-heison ) were established.  They were Minami-ichiyan, 
Kita-iciyan, and Osamunai.  Minami-ichiyan was located in the current 
city center.  #2  #3

In 1895 ( Meiji 28 ), the administrative office of Fukagawa village was 
built in Fukagawa village.  In 1898 ( Meiji 31 ), Fukagawa Station on 
the Governmental Hokkaidou Railway ( now Hakodate Main Line ) opened.  

In 1899 ( Meiji 32 ), the administrative office of Otoe ( \u97F3
\u6C5F ) village was established.  It was separated from Takikawa 
village.  Kikutei Farm was abolished and tenant farms acquired the 
land. 

In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Fukagawa Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1906 ( Meiji 39 ), Ichiyan ( \u4E00\u5DF2 ) Village 
( 2nd-class municipality ) was established. 

In 1907 ( Meiji 40 ), Fukagawa Village ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1910 ( Meiji 43 ), the Rumoi Line opened between 
Fukagawa Station and Rumoi Station.  In 1915 ( Taishou 4 ), Tadoshi 
( \u591A\u5EA6\u5FD7 ) Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established by separating from Ichiyan Village. 

In 1918 ( Taishou 7 ), Fukagawa Town ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established.  Fukagawa became the center of the northern part of 
Sorachi sub-prefecture.  In 1920 ( Tashou 9 ), Osamunai ( \u7D0D
\u5185 ) Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was established by 
separating from Ichiyan Village.

In 1962 ( Shouwa 37 ), Tadoshi Town was established.  In 1963 ( Shouwa 
38 ), Fukagawa City was established by the merger of Fukagawa Town, 
Ichiyan Village, Osamunai Village, and Otoe Village.  In 1970 ( Shouwa 
45 ), Fukagawa City merged Tadoshi Town. 

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Nanporo 
  Town. )

CITY HALL, TRAFFIC 

The city hall of Fukagawa is located on the right bank ( north bank ) 
of the Ishikari River.  National Route 233 ( Fukagawa-kokudu ) passes.  
On the left bank ( south bank ) of the Ishikari River, National Route 
12 passes.  The national highway goes through a gorge of the Ishikari 
River and reaches Asahikawa City.  #4

Fukagawa Station is the junction of the Hakodate Main Line and the 
Rumoi Main Line.  In the past, the Shin-mei Line branched off from 
Fukagawa Station.  #5

INDUSTRY  

Distinctive industries in Fukagawa are agriculture ( rice ), food 
processing, wood processing, and gravel mining.  STATISTICS OF 
AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 5,844 hectares in 2015, buckwheat 2,622, wheat 
510, beans ( soybeans 367, adzuki beans 89, other beans 1 ), pasture 
366, potatoes 102, 

( continue ) --> orchards 63 ( apples 18, grapes 4, other fruits 1+X ), 
fodder 59, flowers 31, sugar beets 12, cucumbers 11, melons 8, onions 
7, yams 5, tomatoes 2, green onions 1, other grains 32, other 
vegetables 31+X, and other industrial crops 2 ; beef cattle 6,086 head 
in 2015 and dairy cows 361.

Paddy fields extend on lowlands along the Ishikari River and the Oohou 
River.  They were expanded after the completion of the Taishou Canal 
in 1916 ( Taisou 5 ) and the Ryuujin Canal in 1927 ( Shouwa 2 ).

After the enactment of the rice reduction policy ( ``guentan-
seisaku'' ), buckwheat increased.  The area of buckwheat is the second 
largest in the country.  ( The largest is neighboring Horokanai Town in 
Kamikawa sub-prefecture. )

In the past, Dalmatian chrysanthemums and edible lily bulbs were 
cultivated in upland fields.  Dalmatian chrysanthemum is called 
``jochuu-guiku'' in Japanese.  The flowers ( ovules ) were processed 
into mosquito coils. 

Today, apples, grapes for wine, vegetables, and flowers are cultivated 
in upland fields.  Beef cattle grazing thrives too.  Local products are 
sold at Fukagawa Roadside Station.  #6  #7 

( For lily bulb, see Makkari Village in Shiribeshi sub-prefecture. )

NOTE   
 
#1  

Kikutei Farm covered an area of 5,756 hectares.  It was located in what 
are now Fukagawa City and Moseushi Town.  In Fukagawa City, the farm 
covered what are now Kita-kikusui, Minami-kikusui, and Memu districts.  
They are located near the Fukagawa West Interchange on Fukagawa Rumoi 
Expressway.  Rice fields stretch out there.  

The word ``memu'' is Ainu language.  It means ``spring ( fountain ).''  
There were many ``memu'' in and around Hokkaidou University, because 
it is located at the end of an alluvial fan.  

( For the names Kikusui and Kikutei, see Uryuu Town, Akabira Cityi in 
  Sorachi sub-prefecture, and Shiroishi Ward in Sapporo City, 
  Ishikari sub-prefecture. )
 
#2  

During the pioneering years, an outbreak of malaria occurred in 
Fukagawa military settlers' colonies ( Tonden-heison ).  It was quartan 
fever malaria.  Military investigations revealed that the cause was 
Anopheles mosquitoes in rice paddies.

( ``Quartan fever malaria'' is called ``mikka-netsu malaria'' in 
  Japanese. ``Anopheles mosquitoes'' is called ``hamadara-ka'' in 
  Japanese. ) 

A military settler ( Tonden-hei ) recalled as follows ( by ``Tonden'' 
No. 17, 1944 ) ; ``Immigration started and diseases emerged.  Malaria 
was prevalent for four to five years.  Almost all the immigrants 
contracted malaria.'' 

I met a geographer who contracted malaria in Africa.  I told him about 
malaria in Fukagawa, Hokkadou.  He didn't believed it, saying ; ``There 
is no malaria in cold regions.''  But, malaria spread not only in 
Fukagawa but also in the Tokachi Plains.  

The geographer recommended that in Africa, you shouldn't lie by the 
river at sunset and drink beer.  Keep standing.  Anopheles mosquitoes 
don't fly very high. 

In the past, Japanese people who immigrated to Brazil settled in 
lowlands and cultivated paddy rice.  They contracted malaria.  Some 
villages disappeared due to malaria. 
 
Meanwhile, Western immigrants migrated to plateau ( pla'-tow ) and ran 
cattle ranches.  The word ``criador'' in Portuguese means ``ranch 
owner.''  It means ``creator'' in English. 

Today, both in Brazil and in Hokkaidou, people don't worry about 
malaria.  In Japan, Anopheles mosquitoes ( ``hamadara-ka'' ) became 
extinct due to improvements in farming.  In Brazil, my Brazilian friend 
said that people from the USA sprayed insecticides after the Second 
World War. 

A soft drink Antarctica Tonica ( T\u00F4nica ) is sold in Brazil.  The 
description on the can reads, ``Agua Tonica De Quinino'' ( Tonic Water 
of Quinine ).  Quinine is used to treat malaria. 

Japanese people don't touch their skin when greeting.  They bow instead 
of hugging or shaking hands.  The custom may be related to memories of 
infectious diseases and leeches from paddies.  Their culture is based 
on warm and humid places.

Japanese people take off their shoes when they enter a house.  The 
custom may be related memories of the mud of paddies.  When I visited a 
cattle ranch in northern British Columbia, Canada, the family took off 
their shoes when they enter the house.  I told them it was the same as 
in Japan.

( Northern British Columbia is a snowy region.  I didn't take of my 
  shoes in houses and apartments in and around Vancouver, southern 
  British Columbia. )

#3  

Military settlers ( Tonden-hei ) in Fukagawa City recall their old 
lives as follows ( by ``Tonden'' No. 17, 1944 ) ; 

 ( 1 ) Three years after the immigration, a typhoid fever epidemic 
broke out.  A ward for typhoid patients was build in the military 
settlers' colony.  At its peak, there were 50 patients in the ward.  
( There were 400 families in Kita-ichiyan and Minami-ichiyan colonies.  
``Typhoid fever'' is called ``chou-chifusu'' in Japanese. )

Suzuki was a very good military doctor in the ward.  Thanks to him, no 
one died.  He touched the patient's stomach, guessed what the patient 
had eaten, and pointed out that he/she should not have eaten. 

For example, ``You ate `okayu-no-moto'?''  Or, ``You ate `soup-no-
kasu'?"'  ( ``Okayu-no-moto'' is hard boiled rice before cooking 
`okayu ( rice grue ).  ``Soup-no-kasu'' is low quality meat for 
broth. ) 

( 2 ) During the pioneering years, rice was scarce.  The staple foods 
were adzuki beans, millet ( ``kibi'' ), foxtail millet ( ``awa'' ), and 
white potatoes ( ``bareisho'' ).  Some people brought potatoes for 
lunch during military exercises.

( 3 ) During the family survey, communication was difficult.  I could 
not understand what the families from Toyama Prefecture were speaking, 
especially, the families from Tonami and Gokayama in the prefecture. 

( About 100 years' ago, when people from Aomori Prefecture and 
  Kagoshima Prefecture met, they could not understand each other's 
  languages.  Even today, when I traveled to Nagasaki, I found it a 
  little hard to hear the taxi driver.  In my courses, my English may 
  be difficult to hear for foreign students. )

#4  

To the east of Fukagawa City is a gorge of the Ishikari River.  The 
gorge is called Kamui-kotan.  The name comes from ``kamuy-kotan'' in 
Ainu language.  It means ``god, village.''  

In the gorge, there are 13 stone circles ( ``kanjou-resseki'' ).  They 
have been designated as National Historic Site ( Shiseki ).  They are 
seen as tombs from the Joumon era.  

It is estimated that the stone circles were made about 2,000 years ago.
In Honshuu island, 2,000 years ago, it was the Yayoi era.  The Yayoi 
era contined from 1,000 BC to the 3rd century AD.  Yayoi was the era of 
paddy rice.

In Hokkaidou, the climate is too cold for ancient people to cultivate 
rice, so the Joumon era continued.  The period is also called the Zoku-
joumon era, which means the ``Post-joumon era.''  

The houses in the Joumon and Yayoi eras were pit houses ( ``tateana-
juukyo'' ).  The former sites of circular pit houses remain at 
Hokkaidou University.  

( For Kamui-kotan, see Asahikawa City in Kamikawa sub-prefecture. )
( For ruins from the Zoku-joumon era, see Otaru City and Yoichi Town in 
  Shiribeshi sub-prefecture. )
 
Otoe-hei-son was a military settlers' colony ( Tonden-heison ) near the 
gorge Kamui-kotan.  Although clearing of trees was hard work, the soil 
was fertile ( fu'r-tl ).  The settlers didn't use fertilizer.   
  
#5  

Fukagawa is like a three pronged fork.  National Route 12 and the 
Hakodate Main Line head north to Fukagawa. ( 1 ) Then, they head east, 
run along the Ishikari River, go through the Kamui-kotan gorge, and 
reach the Kamikawa Basin.
 
( 2 ) National Route 233 and the Rumoi Main Line head west.  They run 
between the Teshio Mountains and the Mashike Mountains, and reach Rumoi 
City on the Sea of Japan side. 

( 3 ) National Route 275 heads north, passes Horokanai Town and Lake 
Shumarinai, and reaches the Nayoro Basin.  In the past, the Shin-mei 
Line followed the same route.  The railroad opened in 1924 and closed 
in 1995.  

( When I traveled through a vast grassland in Brazil, I met a cowboy 
  ( ``peao'' ) ( pe\u00E3o ).  He said, ``When you come to a three 
  pronged road, choose the middle one.'' ) 
 
#6  

Fukagawa Roadside Station is called Rice Land.  Rice is a local 
specialty.  Farmers' shop on the first floor sells rice, vegetables, 
and melons.  The horticultural crops ( vegetables and melons ) are 
harvested in greenhouses on the hill.  The restaurant on the second 
floor is good.
 
#7  

A souvenir from Fukagawa Station is Uroko-dango dumplings.  Uroko-dango 
means ``fish scale,  dumpling.''  In the past, freight ( fre'it ) 
trains on the Rumoi Main Line carried herring and coal.  Fish scales 
were put on the trains. 

Uroko-dango dumplings are scale-shaped, but they don't contain fish 
scales.  Uroko-dango dumplings are sold at Fukagawa Station, Fukagawa 
Roadside Station, and Sapporo Station.

Fukagawa-yushi Co. sells potato chips in supermarkets and souvenir 
shops in Hokkadou.  These potato chips are made with ingredients from 
Hokkaidou, including potatoes, rice oil, and Okhotsk salt.  The name 
``Fukagawa-yushi'' means ``Fukagawa oil.''  The company was founded in 
Fukagawa Town in 1940.
 __________________
|                  |
|   CHIPPUBETSU    |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 23  |
 ------------------
AREA 47 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 3, CULTIVATED 30 ; COUNTY = URYUU 
COUNTY ; POP 2,730 ( 2010 ), 3,735 ( 1990 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = BELL.
 
LOCATION 

Chippubetsu ( chi-ppu-be-tsu ) ( \u79E9\u7236\u5225 ) is town in 
Sorachi sub-prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  It is 
located in the northern part of the Ishikari Plain.  

Chippubetsu is the second smallest municipality in Hokkaidou 
Prefecture.  ( The smallest is Kami-sunagwa Town in Sorachi sub-
prefecture. )  The topography is flat.  Paddy fields extend.  

NAME 

The name ``chippubetsu'' comes from ``ci-kus-pet'' in Ainu language.  
It means ``we, go through, river.''  It signified the Chippubetsu 
River.  In the past, Ainu people used boats to move the Ishikari Plain. 
The Chippubetsu River is a tributary of the Uryuu River.  The Uryuu 
River is the tributary of the Ishikari River. 

HISTORY 

In 1895 ( Meiji 28 ) and 1896 ( Meiji 29 ), two military settlers' 
colonies ( Tonden-heison ) were established.  To the east was Higashi-
chippu-heison.  To the west was Nishi-chippu-heison.  The colonies were 
established on the undeveloped land of the former Uryuu Farm.  400 
families from 24 prefectures settled.  #1 

In 1901 ( Meiji 34 ), the administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) 
of Chippubetsu village was established.  It was separated from Fukagawa 
village.  In 1906 ( Meiji 39 ), Chippubetsu Village ( 2nd-class 
municipality ) was established. 

In 1923 ( Taishou 12 ), Chippubetsu Village ( 1st-class municipality ) 
was established.  In 1959 ( Shouwa 34 ), Chippubetsu Town was 
established.

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Nanporo 
  Town. )
( For Uryuu Farm, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC

The town hall of Chippubetsu is located in front of Chippubetsu Station 
on the Rumoi Main Line.  National Route 233 ( Fukagawa-kokudou ) 
passes.  #2 

INDUSTRY

Distinctive industry in Chippubetsu is rice cultivation.  STATISTICS OF 
AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 2,122 hectares in 2015, buckwheat 269, wheat 
191, soybeans 68, broccoli 34, pasture 4, flowers 3, tomatoes 1, other 
grains 18, and other vegetables X ( concealed ) ; dairy cow - ( zero ) 
head in 2015.

The headquarters of JA Kita-ibuki ( Japan Agricultural Cooperatives 
Kitaibuki ) is located in Chippubetsu Town.  It sells high-quality 
rice.  The rice varieties are Yume-pirka, Nanatsu-boshi, and Kirara 
397.  Chippubetsu is one of the rice seed producing areas in Hokkaidou. 
Rice seed is called ``tane-momi'' in Japanese.  #3  #4 

As for sightseeing, Chippubetsu Hot Spring is located near Chippubetsu 
Station.  There is a tower on the hot spring grounds.  It was built to 
commemorate the centennial anniversary of the town.  There is a bell at 
the top of the tower.  It commemorates the bell that told the time in 
the military settlers' colonies. 

( For paddy rice, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )

NOTE   

#1  

Military settlers ( Tonden-hei ) recall their old lives as follows ( by 
``Chippubetsu Town history ( Chippubetsu-chou-shi )'' published in 
1964 ) ; 

( 1 ) In 1896 ( Meiji 29 ), I came from Hiroshima Prefecture.  In 1902 
( Meiji 35 ), there was no harvest from paddy fields, because of plant-
hopper damage.  ( Plant-hopper is ``unka'' in Japanese. )  In the 
spring of 1903 ( Meiji 36 ), we ate bamboo grass seeds. *

*  In the past, Japanese people said ``nari-doshi-wa-fusaku'' ( the 
   year in whichn bamboo-grass bears fruit will be a crop failure ).  
   ( For the fruit of bamboo-grass, see Nihei lab 2023-2025. )  
 
( 2 ) What I thought was horse manure was bear droppings.  I heard 
``Woooo, Woooo.''  The sound was the terrifying, earth-shattering roars 
of a bear.  When I went outside, I always hit a tin can.  In a year, a 
bear ate 3-tan ( 30 ares ) of corn ( ``toukibi'' ).  It ate the field 
completely. 

( 3 ) I was a samurai ( ``shi-zoku'' ) and was a complete novice in 
agriculture.  My grandfather fell ill two months after settling here.  
He died two months after becoming ill.  I was the head of the household 
and had to go to military training every day.  My wife did almost all 
the clearing by herself. 

( 4 ) I was a child in a military settler ( Tonden-hei ) family.  When 
I became independent and had a house along Ichijou-douri ( 1-jou 
street ), I was given ``itto ( 1-to )'' of rice as a celebration.  
( ``Itto'' is 15 kg in the case of rice. )  I consumed the `itto' of 
rice for one year.

The staple foods at the time were ``ina-kibi'' ( glutinous proso 
millet ), wheat, and barley.  If someone bought ``ippyou ( 1-hyou, 
60 kg )'' of rice, everybody's eyes widened.

In the military settlers' colony, the rice allowance for a family was 
limited to five people, so the life of a large family was difficult.  
When they got the harvest of ``gosho-imo'' ( white potatoes ) in 
autumn, they relieved. 

( For gosho-imo, see Shihoro Town in Tokachi sub-prefecture. )

( 5 ) I was a child of a military settler ( Tonden-hei ) family.  I was 
often sent on errands to other families.  It was tough, because I could 
not understand their languages.  The families came from all over the 
country, including Shikoku, Ouu, Hokuriku, and Kyuushuu regions. 

Roads were bad.  The land was a swamp.  Water seeped onto the roads.  
Weeds and bamboo grass were dense.  Trees were tall.  The branches of 
trees were so intertwined that I could not see the sky when I looked 
up.  It was home to many animals.  

Nowadays, if I call loudly, the neighbors can hear me.  Or, I can put 
on my sandals and can run to the neighbors.

( For dialect, see Fukagawa City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
( For rice consumption, see the note of Enbetsu Town in Rumoi sub-
  prefecture. ) 

( 6 ) The head of the household went off to military training every 
day.  Land reclamation was the duty of other family members.  First, we 
had to cut down trees and mow the grass around the house.  Then, we 
collected the trees and grass, and burned them.  The flames rose high 
into the sky.  The flames continue for a month.

The diameter of the trees was from 3-shaku ( 90 centimeters ) to 
4-shaku ( 120 centimeters ).  Looking back now, it was regrettable that 
we wasted those large trees.  Tree stumps remained in upland fields and 
paddy fields for a long time.  They hindered horse-drawn plowing. *

*  Good trees are scarce in Hokkaidou, although the forests are vast.  
   Good trees were burned or exported to Europe before the World War 
   II.  

   Once, I joined a survey at a ranch of 470 hectares.  The purpose was 
   to sell good trees to support the management of the ranch.  But, we 
   couldn't find a good tree.  Good trees are straight and thick. They 
   had been already cut.  

( 7 ) Many deer lived.  When I went outside the house, I found deer 
antlers scattered in the grass.  I used them as hearth hooks and 
clothes hangers.  ( Hearth is called ``irori'' in Japanese. )

There were many bird nests in the trees around the house.  Those were 
for finches ( ``mashiko'' ), cuckoos, owls, and others.  Even adult 
birds could be caught by hand.  Collecting the eggs was a job for the 
children when they came home from school. 

We also caught fish.  There were many ``ugoi ( ugui ).''  ( ``Ugui'' is 
called big-scaled redfin or dace ( de'isu ) fish in English ).  At the 
time, the size of ``ugoi'' was from 1-shaku ( 30 centimeters ) to 
2-shaku ( 60 centimeters ).  No one paid any attention to the tiny 
``funa'' ( crucian carp ) that are common nowadays.  
  
#2  

The town hall of Chippubetsu is located near the former site of the 
headquarters of Higashi-chippu-heison ( East Chippu military settlers' 
colony ).  The military colony consisted of 200 persons.  The size was 
a company in a military unit.

( An army company is called  ``chuu-tai'' in Japanese.  It consists of 
  100 to 250 soldiers.  The size is about the same as the number of 
  students admitted to large faculties at national and public 
  universities.  

  I taught at ( 1 ) College of Natural Science, University of Tsukuba, 
  ( 2 ) Faculty of Letters, Hokkaidou University, and ( 3 ) Faculty of 
  Urban and Environmental Sciences at Toukyou Metropolitan University.  
  Their undergraduate admission quotas are 200, 185, and 255, 
  respectively. ) 

The Fukagawa-Rumoi Expressway passes Chippubetsu Town.  It takes 35 
minutes from Chippbetsu Interchange to Asahikawa-Takasu Interchange, 
and 1 hour and 20 minutes to Sapporo-kita Interchange.  ( Sapporo is 
the largest city, Asahikawa is the second largest city in Hokkaidou. ) 

In Chippubetsu Town, households are permitted to install food waste 
disposers, which helps reduce food waste collection work.  ( When I was 
a child, I saw a food waste disposer in an American horror movie and 
thought it was horrible.  It was invented in the USA. )   
   
#3  

JA Kita-ibuki was established in 2003 by the merger of Chippubetsu 
Agricultural Cooperative, Moseushi Agricultural Cooperative, and Numata
Town Agricultural Cooperative.  

When I was a child, agricultural cooperatives were considered as good 
working places in the countryside as well as town halls and village 
halls.  Many agricultural cooperatives were merged and abolished.  
Today, one percent of the country's population is engaged in 
agriculture.  Most of them are elderly people.  

#4  

These are rice seed producing areas in Hokkaidou ; ( 1 ) Hokuto City in 
Oshima sub-prefecture, ( 2 ) Toubetsu Town in Ishikari sub-prefecture, 
( 3 ) the former Kurisawa Town in Iwamizawa City, Sorachi sub-
prefecture, 

( continue ) --> ( 4 ) the former Ebeotsu Town in Takikawa City, 
Sorachi sub-prefecture, ( 5 ) Chippubetsu Town in Sorachi sub-
prefecture, and ( 6 ) Nakafurano Town in Kamikawa sub-prefecture.

Chippubetsu is the navel ( ne'ivel ) of rice cultivation in the 
northern Ishikari Plain.

( For Hokuren Rice Seed Center, see Takikawa City in Sorachi sub-
  prefecture. ) 
( For rice, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
 __________________
|                  |
|      NUMATA      |
|                  |
|  MAP 5 - NO. 24  |
 ------------------
AREA 283 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 205, CULTIVATED 38 ; COUNTY = URYUU ; POP 
3,612 ( 2010 ), 5,206 ( 1990 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = FIREFLY.
 
LOCATION 

Numata ( nu-ma-ta ) ( \u6CBC\u7530 ) is a town in Sorachi sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Numata is an inland 
town.  The area extends from the northern end of the Ishikari Plain to 
the Mashike Mountains.  The Uryuu River and its tributary, the Horo-
nii-tachi-betsu ( \u5E4C\u65B0\u592A\u5200\u5225 ) River, flow.

NAME   

The name ``Numata'' is Japanese.  It comes from the surname of 
Kisaburou Numata.  He established Honganji Farm in what is now Numata 
Town.  He was a wealthy merchant in Otaru.  ( Wealthy merchant was 
called ``gou-shou'' in Japanese. )  #1

HISTORY  

In 1914 ( Taishou 3 ), the administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) 
of Kami-hokuryuu ( \u4E0A\u5317\u7ADC ) village was established.  It 
was separated from Hokuryuu village.  In 1918 ( Taishou 7 ), Horokanai 
village was separated from Kami-hokuryuu village.

In 1919 ( Taishou 8 ), Kami-hokuryuu Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) 
was established.  In 1922 ( Taishou 11 ), Numata Village was 
established.  The name Kami-hokuryuu was renamed Numata.  In 1939 
( Shouwa 14 ), Numata Village ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1947 ( Shouwa 22 ), Numata Town was established.

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Nanporo 
  Town. ) 

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC  

The town hall of Numata is located near the Uryuu River.  The Rumoi 
Main Line and National Route 275 ( Sorachi-kokudou ) pass.  In the 
past, Numata Station was a junction between the Rumoi Main Line and 
the Sasshou Line.  Ebishima Station was a junction between the Rumoi 
Main Line and the Rumoi Railway.

The Rumoi Railway ran along the Horo-nii-tachi-betsu River, connecting 
coal mines of Asano, Shouwa, and Tachibetsu.  Those were called Rumoi 
Coal Field ( Rumoi-tanden ) or Uryuu Coal Field ( Uryuu-tanden ).  
Numata flourished from coal mines between the 1930s and 1960s. 

In 1969 ( Shouwa 44 ), the last coal mine closed.  In 1971 ( Shouwa 
46 ), the Rumoi Railway closed.  The town started inviting the Japan 
Self-Defence Forces.  In 1972 ( Shouwa 47 ), the section between Numata 
Station and Shin-totsukawa Station on the Sasshou Line closed.  In 
1990, the garrison of the Japanese Self-Defense Forces was established.

( For coal mining, see Yuubari City in Sorachi sub-prefecture. ) 

INDUSTRY  

Distinctive industries in Numata are paddy rice cultivation and 
sightseeing.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 2,374 hectares, 
buckwheat 641, beans ( soybeans 220, adzuki beans 50 ), wheat 206, 

( continue ) --> sugar beets 34, pasture 33, flowers 7, tomatoes 6, 
fodder 4, melons 2, orchards 1, other grains 1, other vegetables X 
( concealed ) ; dairy cows X head in 2015. 

As for sightseeing, Ebishima Station was a filming location for the NHK 
drama ``Suzuran ( lily of the valley ).''  ( ``NHK'' means ``Japan 
Broadcasting Corporation.'' )  Filming sets including a wooden station 
building and a station-master's house remain. 

Village of Firefly ( Hotaru-no-sato ) is a tourist attraction 
located along the Horo-nii-tachi-betsu River.  Fireflies fly from late 
June to early August.  

Village of Firefly includes Horoshin Hot Spring, the Numata Fossil 
Museum, an automobile camping site, and a lifelong learning center.  
Those are managed by Numata Town.  The fossil cleaning experience at 
the Numata Fossil Museum are popular with elementary school students.  
#2

NOTE   
 
#1  

Kisaburou Numata ( 1834-1923 ) was a farmer in Toyama Prefecture.  In 
1882, he left his wife and children behind and moved to Otaru, 
Hokkaidou. 

He put waterwheels on the Myouken River and started rice milling 
business.  ( The Myouken ( \u5999\u898B ) River is the downstream of 
the Okobachi ( \u65BC\u53E4\u767A ) River, which flows through central 
Otaru and empties into the Otaru Canal. ) 

In 1891, he established a joint stock company, Kyousei.  He added 
waterwheels to the Katsunai River and the Asari River.  ( The Katsunai 
River flows through the southern part of central Otaru and empties into 
the Port of Otaru.  The Asari River flows through the former Asari 
Village, located east of Otaru. ) 

The wealthy merchants ( ``gou-shou'' ) of Otaru were called ``Numata of 
Rice'' ( Kome-no-Numata ) and ``Takahashi of Beans'' ( Mame-no-
Takahashi ).  Takahashi was Naoji Takahashi ( 1856-1926 ), who exported 
beans from Hokkaidou to Europe. 

Kisaburou Numata thought, ``As rice cultivation is spreading in 
Hokkaidou, the rice milling business will dwindle.''  At the time, he 
imported rice from Honshuu island for his rice milling business. 

He intended to cultivate rice in Hokkaidou.  In 1893, the huge Uryuu 
Farm in the northern Ishikari Plain was abolished.  He negotiated with 
Count Kouei Ootani of Higashi Honganji Temple, who was one of the 
investors of Uryuu Farm.  

Kisaburou Numata rented 10 million-tsubo ( 3,300 hectares ) of land, 
and opened Honganji Farm at the former site of Uryuu Farm.  He 
recruited immigrants in Toyama Prefecture.  In 1894, immigration to 
Honganji Farm started.  

In 1912, the headquarters building of Kyousei was constructed in Otaru 
Ward ( now Otaru City ).  It was a brick building with a copper roof.  
It's now Otaru Music Box Hall ( Otaru-orugooru-kan ).  ( A music box is 
called ``orgel'' in Dutch and ``Orugooru'' in Japanese. )

( For Uryuu Farm, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi sub-prefecture. )
 
#2  

Fossils of large scallop, ``Takahashi-hotate,'' have been unearthed in 
Numata Town.  The species lived 5 million years ago and is now extinct.

At the Numata Fossil Museum, visitors can experience cleaning fossils.  
The fossils are small ammonites and shark teeth imported from Morocco.  
The small fossils can be taken home.

The Numata Fossil Museum opened in 2000.  In May 2015, my son, Masaki 
Nihei, became the 15,000th visitor.  He received a present.  In the 
lifelong learning center of Numata Town, a large totem pole is 
displayed.  It's a gift from the sister city, Port Hardy.

Port Hardy is located in the north of Vancouver Island, Canada.  Numata 
Town and Port Hardy became sister cities through the mediation of the 
president of a fish processing factory in Numata Town. 

Another sister city is Oyabe City in Toyama Prefecture.  The first 
immigrants came from what is now Oyabe City.  Yotaka-andon Festival is 
held in Numata Town in August.  The festival originated in Oyabe City.  
In the festival, large lamp floats collide with each other.  They are 
fighting floats ( ``kenka-andon'' ).
